The soldering bullet above stages the Day 4 burn kit beside the station and the Tier chain names burn as a Tier 2 example, but until this bullet no form existed for a contact burn, and the forms that do exist do not fit it. The Tier chain gives no cooling time, no cover rule, and no Tier 3 line for a burn; the sunburn bullet cools sore red skin from the sun with shade and the camper's own bottle over a pad, but it names no burn gel, no burn dressing, no jewelry rule, and no blistering-versus-charred line for a hot-iron contact burn; and the room cut bullet gives a pressure clock for bleeding, not a cooling clock for heat. When a camper touches the hot iron, a hot part, or a freshly made joint, the iron goes back in the stand and the power switch goes off at once, and the station stays closed for the rest of the block, because the instructor cannot both treat a burn and run the station. The other campers sit at their tables with hands off tools, in line of sight, and nobody crowds the burned camper. Clothing or jewelry near the burn comes off only if it is loose and lifts free; nothing stuck to the skin is pulled, and no blister is popped. Cool the burn with clean cool water from the camper's own bottle, poured gently over the skin for 10 minutes by the clock, then a burn gel packet from the Day 4 burn kit is laid over the cooled skin and covered loosely with a burn dressing from the same kit; an instant cold pack from the kit goes over the dressing, never ice straight on the skin, and no butter, toothpaste, or ointment goes on the burn. Tier 1 on a small red burn with skin intact and pain easing after cooling, with the gel and loose dressing, a return to non-heated table work but not to the soldering station that day, and a watch-for-48-hours pickup note. Tier 2 on blistering, a burn larger than the camper's palm, a face, hand, foot, or joint burn, or pain that does not ease after cooling, with the Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes, the incident form the same hour, sick-chair rest for the rest of the session, no tool stations that day, and the parent deciding on medical follow-up or early pickup under the photo-ID door rule. Tier 3 at once, 911 first, on white, waxy, or charred skin, numbness, a burn that rings a limb, troubled breathing or smoke exposure, faintness, or a burn with a head strike, with the parent call after 911 and the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. Every burn logs time, station, body part, minutes cooled, gel and dressing used, and any Tier trigger fired, plus the parent ping time if Tier 2 fired. If the block consumes any single-use burn kit contents, the handoff tops the kit back up from the sealed refill pack at block end, on the Day 4 handoff form, so the next session's prep finds a full kit. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the burn kit, the refill pack, the water bottle, and the Twilio chain already exist, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No new rehearsal minute is needed, because the Day 4 run-of-show already teaches the iron-in-stand and power-off form, and this bullet only gives that stop a burn response.
  • Eye injury at a build station: hands off, blink, flush, cover, the call line. The cutting bullet runs one station at a time with the instructor within arm's reach, and the Day 4 box holds 6 safety glasses, but until this bullet no form existed for dust or a small piece of debris in a camper's eye, and the forms that do exist do not fit it. The splinter bullet fires Tier 3 for a splinter in the eye, but it names no blink or flush step for ordinary dust, and it forbids pulling at a splinter, which is a different move from flushing loose dust; the burn bullet cools skin, not an eye, and the room cut bullet presses gauze onto bleeding skin, not an open eye. When dust or debris gets in a camper's eye at a build station, tools go down at once, the other campers sit at their tables with hands off tools, in line of sight, and the station stays closed while the eye is treated, because the instructor cannot both treat an eye and run a station. The camper does not rub the eye, and nobody else touches the eye. The camper blinks several times and lets tears work first; if the eye still feels gritty, the camper tilts the head so the hurt eye is low, holds the lids open with clean hands, and the instructor pours clean cool water from the camper's own bottle gently across the open eye, from the nose side outward, over a sick-kit absorbent pad held under the cheek, for up to 2 minutes by the clock, then the camper blinks again. If the object is a splinter, looks stuck, or pokes out of the eye, there is no flush: the splinter bullet's Tier 3 line runs at once. Tier 1 on a particle flushed out with vision normal and pain gone, with a return to non-dust table work but not to cutting or sanding that day, and a watch-for-48-hours pickup note for returning redness, pain, or blurred vision. Tier 2 on grit that still feels present after the flush, pain or redness that does not ease, light sensitivity, or blurred vision, with the eye covered loosely with a gauze pad from the first-aid kit and taped lightly with no pressure on the eyeball, the Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes, the incident form the same hour, sick-chair rest for the rest of the session, no tool stations that day, and the parent deciding on medical follow-up or early pickup under the photo-ID door rule. Tier 3 at once, 911 first, on a stuck or protruding object, a visible cut to the eyeball, blood visible in the eye, sudden vision loss, or severe pain, with the eye covered loosely and left untouched until EMS arrives, the parent call after 911, and the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. Every eye injury logs time, station, which eye, minutes flushed, particle out or not, and any Tier trigger fired, plus the parent ping time if Tier 2 fired. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the water bottle, the gauze, the absorbent pad, and the Twilio chain already exist, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No new rehearsal minute is needed, because the Day 4 run-of-show already teaches the glasses-on and tools-down forms, and this bullet only gives that stop an eye response.
  • Cutting toolsOne cutting station, one camper at a time, one instructor within arm's reach. Craft knives: retractable, cut-away-from-body rule, metal ruler, self-healing mat. The instructor pre-cuts any difficult stock during the 30-minute prep block so the camper cut is always the easy one. Scissors for precision paper work sit at the tables freely; nothing else does. No camper carries an open blade across the room: blade is retracted, handed to the instructor, instructor carries it.
  • 3D printers (two)Both printers are enclosed models; no hot-end contact is possible with the enclosure closed, and the enclosure stays closed while printing. The instructor loads filament and starts every print; campers submit files to a print queue during the project block, never touch the machine mid-print. Cooldown: parts come off the bed with one of the two printer-box scraper tools staged on the print shelf at Session Zero, after the bed reads under 50C on the display, shown to the camper as the readout. The instructor removes the first hot part of the day as the demonstration.
  • Test rig (drop and load)One rig, one team at a time, the instructor watching the drop station. The 500g weight drops from no more than a foot above the mat, straight down onto the phone dummy, hands clear of the mat on release: no thrown drops, no higher releases, no weights on backpacks or bodies. The instructor demonstrates the release once during the test-lab brief; teams then run their own drops from that mark. Mats and dummies are wear-checked in the morning prep and any torn mat or cracked dummy goes in the trash, per the Day 5 staging step, so nothing splits or shards mid-drop.
  • Field missions (single group, no splits)Field missions move as one group of 12 plus the instructor, never in buddy pairs or sub-teams out of sight. The mapped route is shared with parents in the week-of logistics pack: no unmarked street crossings, no water, no entering buildings. Buddy pairs exist for accountability (your buddy checks you are present), not for separation: a buddy pair never leaves the instructor's sightline. Count in and count out, aloud, at every transition. The instructor's field bag gets the same treatment: on a field-mission morning the instructor counts the bag's contents against the Session Zero Hour 4 list before the group leaves the venue (if anything is missing, the mission waits until it is found or re-printed), and counts the same contents back in when the group returns; nothing enters or leaves the bag uncounted on a mission day. EpiPens, the med lockbox, and the lockable medication cooler bag with its frozen ice pack ride in the instructor's field bag (the bag's full contents list is staged at Session Zero Hour 4, and the Hour 4 pack list names the cooler bag unconditionally, so the morning count always expects it). Weather call is the instructor's alone: heat over 95F or air quality over 150 AQI moves the mission to the mapped indoor alternate, decided by 8 AM and messaged to parents. Water rides with the instructor, not in camper bags: on a field-mission morning one full 24-bottle water case goes in the field bag's side sleeve before the count-out, one bottle is issued to each camper at the walk-out count, and the empties are collected at the walk-back count, so the count-out/count-back ritual covers water too. Two cases are bought in the week-before snack buy (one per field-mission morning, the Day 2 safari and the Day 3 verification walk) and folded into the $90 snacks-and-prints operating line; the same week-before buy adds one pack of 10 kraft envelopes (about $4 inside the same $90 line) as the retired-card-pocket stock, one envelope per teaching day, and two 500-sheet reams of copy paper (about $15 inside the same $90 line) as the session's printer-paper stock, the 599-sheet Hour 1 run alone outlasting a single ream; a single case issues twelve bottles per mission with the remaining twelve riding as the spare buffer the heat-illness bullet names, so one case serves one mission, the spare buffer riding full at walk-out, and two cases are bought, one per field-mission morning. The indoor alternate is the venue interior itself, mapped in the week-of logistics pack beside the outdoor route: no second room, no split group, the one-adult line-of-sight model never changes, and a weather morning adds zero print or pack work because every pivot below is pre-built at Session Zero. Day 2's data safari becomes an indoor photo safari, same 25-photo minimum and angle/light log, shot around the makerspace and hallway; the contact-sheet job and the Labeler block run unchanged. Day 3's verification sprint becomes two indoor verifications: a speakerphone call with the pre-briefed shop owner at 9:40 (the briefing sheet carries the weather line) and the public plaque verified from the photo the owner emailed in the week-before briefing; teams still close two claims against primary sources. Day 4's deploy places the stations around the room instead of the yard; the overnight row is already indoors, so the overnight log, the harvest, and the predict-check arc run exactly as written. Day 7's gauntlet builds on the makerspace floor from the same course kit; Day 7's prep names the indoor build. The call goes out by 8 AM on the Twilio parent channel with the drop-off ping, and the pivot executes inside the normal 8:45 to 9:15 prep.
  • Lightning mid-mission: the walk-out abort ruleThe weather call above covers heat and AQI at 8 AM, but a thunderstorm can build after the call, and no page rule said what happens when thunder reaches a group of twelve campers already in the field with one adult. The rule is fixed and printed on a laminated abort card in the field bag: if thunder is heard or lightning is seen, the mission aborts. No judgment call, no debate with twelve witnesses: the instructor calls "Thunder, buddies, back," buddy pairs form on the spot, the count runs aloud, and the group walks single file straight to the venue along the mapped route, no lingering under trees, no waiting it out in the open. The abort move is always walk out, never wait out: the instructor keeps the group moving on the mapped route toward the nearest hard shelter marked at the T-2 site visit, line of sight unbroken, and the water-case bottles come with the group because the walk-back count already covers the empties. Prevention rides the 9:15 walk-out count the instructor already staffs: two minutes at the door table, the instructor checks the radar on their own phone, and a National Weather Service severe-thunderstorm warning over the venue or route at walk-out moves the day to the mapped indoor alternate, the same way the 8 AM heat call does. After an abort, the group does not step out again until 30 minutes after the last thunder, counted from the inside; if that wait outlives the mission block, the pre-built indoor pivots below absorb the rest of the day because every pivot is Session Zero pre-built. Every abort logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, and the parent ping on the camp number carries one line ("field mission came in early for lightning, campers inside, day continues indoors"). No budget-line changes: the two abort cards are paper, laminated at Hour 2 with the other cards, folded into the $90 snacks-and-prints operating line. No timing changes: the radar check rides the walk-out count.
  • Thunderstorm in the room: doors shut, tools down, the 30-minute clock, the indoor day.The field bullet covers a storm that reaches a group already in the field; it never named the form for a storm that reaches the group already in the room, where the walk-out abort has nowhere to walk. The case that presents it: thunder starts during the project block, or the mission never leaves because the walk-out radar check caught the warning, or a mission aborts back through the door, and twelve campers are inside with one adult and a storm outside. The Day 1 freeze governs, with hands off tools and nobody crowding the doors or the windows: the instructor calls the hold, the group stays in the room, and the door locks from inside. The instructor closes the doors and the windows, and the group moves to the tables in the U, away from the glass, the same tables the Day 1 drop, cover, and hold rehearsal uses, so no new rehearsal minute is needed. Tool stations go cold at the instructor's call and stay down until the all-clear below, because no powered tool runs while the room shelters; the Day 4 burn kit stays where it sits, and the room returns to tools only when the clock below runs out. Nobody steps out until 30 minutes after the last thunder, counted from the inside, the same clock the field bullet runs after an abort, and the pre-built indoor pivots absorb the rest of the day, because every pivot is Session Zero pre-built and the day already continues indoors. The one-line parent ping on the camp number reads "storm shelter in place, campers inside, day continues indoors", the same line the field abort carries, and every shelter-in-place logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because doors, windows, the freeze, the U, and the indoor pivots already exist, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged.
  • Heat illness on the walk: shade, water, the ten-minute sit, the walk-out ruleThe 8 AM call cancels the walk over 95F, but heat illness does not need 95F: heavy sweating, headache, dizziness, nausea, or cramps mid-walk is the named case each field-mission water case was bought for. Prevention rides the 9:15 walk-out count the instructor already staffs: one pump bottle of sunscreen sits at the door table for self-apply before the count (staged at Session Zero Hour 4 as reusable shop stock inside the $931 shop-tools startup line; the instructor never applies sunscreen to a camper, and one adult means the bottle is pump-only, no hands on campers). Each camper keeps the one issued bottle for the whole walk, and the remaining twelve bottles of the case ride in the field bag side sleeve as spares, so a camper who sweats through the first bottle gets a second without breaking the walk-back count. The signs ladder is printed on the back of the two laminated abort cards from the lightning rule, so the Session Zero Hour 1 print run does not grow: heavy sweating, headache, dizziness, nausea, or cramps means the camper sits in the shade at the instructor's side immediately, drinks water, and gets a ten-minute sit while the group keeps working within the same sightline (the buddy stays with the pair, nobody leaves the sightline, the instructor never stops watching the group); confusion, slurred speech, hot skin without sweat, or fainting skips the ladder and fires the Tier 3 chain, 911 first, shade and water on the skin while the paramedics come. If the ten-minute sit does not bring the camper back, the walk aborts early the same way a lightning abort ends: the aloud count runs, the group walks out along the mapped route, and the parent pickup ping goes out on the camp number. Every heat episode logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, and a parent who prefers their child to carry personal sunscreen adds it to the enrollment accommodation note at no charge.
  • Heat illness in the room: cool air, cool water, the signs ladder, the call lineUntil this bullet the signs ladder lived only on the walk: the field bullet above runs heat illness on shade, a water bottle, and a walk abort, none of which fits a camper already seated at the tables while the other eleven keep working. A warm room on a hot day, a camper who came back from the walk still sweating through, or dehydration that started before arrival can present heat illness at the tables, and the 8 AM 95F call does not fix a warm room: when the walk is cancelled for heat, the room block still runs, so the room needs its own form. The signs ladder is the same one printed on the back of the two laminated abort cards from the lightning rule, and one card rides at the instructor table for the room block, so the Session Zero Hour 1 print run does not grow: heavy sweating, headache, dizziness, nausea, or cramps is the named case each field-mission water case was bought for, the same named case as on the walk. When a camper reports those signs in the room, the group freeze governs: the other eleven stay in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), nobody crowds the camper, and the camper moves to the coolest side of the room, away from sun-facing windows, beside the instructor table, inside the sightline. The camper sips cool water from the own bottle and gets cool water on the neck, forehead, and wrists from that same bottle, and the door or window opens to move air if the venue allows. The camper sits for ten minutes by the clock while the group keeps working in sightline, the same ten minutes the walk form runs. Confusion, slurred speech, hot skin without sweat, or fainting skips the ladder and fires the Tier 3 chain at once, 911 first, cool water on the skin while the paramedics come, with the instructor staying with the eleven and the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. If the ten-minute sit does not bring the camper back, the case is Tier 2: the sick-chair rule runs for the rest of the session, no tool stations that day, a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes, and the incident form the same hour, with the parent deciding on pickup under the photo-ID door rule. This bullet does not govern fever or vomiting: a hot camper with a fever or vomiting runs the mid-day illness bullet and its sick-camper script, because the thermometer and the fever line belong to that script, not to this ladder. Every room heat episode logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, room, signs seen, minutes rested, Tier trigger, and rest versus pickup. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: the water bottle, the group freeze from Day 1, the sick chair, the Twilio chain, and the abort cards already exist, the coolest side of the room and venue air are room fixtures, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the ten-minute sit rides the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Sunburn found on a walk or at return: shade, cool water, the call linePrevention rides the walk-out sunscreen count the heat bullet already staffs, but until this bullet no response form existed: a camper can come back from a walk red and sore even with sunscreen on, and the Tier chain names no sunburn line, the heat bullet covers heat illness rather than skin, and the first-aid kit carries no sunburn remedy. When a camper reports sore red skin, or the instructor spots it at the walk-back count, the camper moves into shade at once and stays out of the sun for the rest of the day: on a walk the camper sits in the shade at the instructor's side while the group keeps working within the same sightline, at return the camper stays seated in the room. The camper cools the skin themselves with clean water from their own bottle over a sick-kit absorbent pad held damp against the skin for a few minutes at a time, never ice straight on the skin, and the instructor applies nothing to the skin: no ointment, no aloe, and no lotion from another camper, because the kit carries none and one adult cannot verify what is in another family's bottle. The camper drinks water and rests while the group keeps working. Tier 2 fires, with the parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, on blistering, a large area, face or hands involved, fever, chills, or nausea or vomiting with the burn, or pain that does not ease after cooling. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first, on sunburn with confusion, fainting, or troubled breathing, which rides the heat bullet's Tier 3 chain instead of this bullet. Every sunburn logs time, place on the route or at return, body area, minutes cooled, and any Tier trigger fired.
  • Sunburn in the room: cool air, cool compress, the discomfort scale, the call line. A sunburn can also present in the room: a camper can arrive already burned from the weekend, or the redness can show up under the room light during the project block, and the walk bullet's tools do not fit indoors: its walk-back count, its place on the route, and its move-into-shade step. The room form drops the shade move, the walk count, and the place on the route, because the room is already shaded, and keeps the rest of the walk bullet's form: the camper cools the sore skin themselves with clean water from their own bottle over a sick-kit absorbent pad held damp against the skin for a few minutes at a time, never ice straight on the skin, and the instructor applies nothing to the skin: no ointment, no aloe, and no lotion from another camper, because the kit carries none and one adult cannot verify what is in another family's bottle. If window sun falls on the camper's seat the instructor moves them to a shaded table, and the group keeps working in the U while the camper cools and drinks water. Tier 2 fires, with the Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, on blistering, a large area, face or hands involved, fever, chills, or nausea or vomiting with the burn, or pain that does not ease after cooling. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first, on sunburn with confusion, fainting, or troubled breathing, which rides the heat bullet's Tier 3 chain instead of this bullet. Every room sunburn logs time, the room rather than a place on the route, body area, minutes cooled, and any Tier trigger fired.
  • Guests and judgesAny guest (interview subject, taste-panel judge, showcase attendee) is pre-briefed on the single rule: you are never alone with a camper. Guests stay in the main room, in the instructor's line of sight, for the duration of their visit. The Day 6 guest sits at a table in the middle of the room; recording pairs rotate through, the room never empties around the guest.
  • Personal devices: phones into the pouch at check-inCamper phones are the one thing the one-adult model cannot absorb mid-day. Twelve 10-to-14-year-olds with live phones means a field-mission walker filming instead of counting in, a lesson where the room screen competes with twelve notifications, and photos and video of other campers taken by children whose families may never have signed the media release, outside the DocuSign chain the safety plan already carries. So the rule is total and mechanical: personal phones go off or silent into a name-labeled resealable pouch at the door table during the 9:15 QR check-in; the twelve pouches lock in the combination-lock door-table lockbox (its combination is set at Session Zero Hour 4, held by the instructor only, and distinct from the med-lockbox and cooler-bag codes); the phones come out only at the 11:15 pickup sign-out, the empty pouches staying on the door table, so the next morning's 9:15 check-in finds all twelve where the drop-off row reaches for them. The enrollment Typeform carries the phone-policy acceptance beside the med authorization and the pickup-authorization list, so no family meets this rule for the first time at the door. Parent-to-camper contact during camp hours goes through the camp number printed on the pickup-authorization list, the same channel as every Twilio parent ping; the Day 1 walk tells every camper the number. On field missions no personal phone leaves the venue: the three field-kit cameras are the camp's only cameras, which is exactly the media-release boundary the guests-and-judges bullet draws. The only exception is a phone that is itself a medical device (a phone paired to a glucose monitor), which stays on the camper under the parent-signed med authorization form; the rest of the rule still applies to it. A phone seen out during lessons goes to the instructor table in the open, the lesson never stops to fetch it, and the pickup is logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp; the device returns at the door table at pickup. No second adult is ever needed, because collection and return ride the check-in and pickup rows the instructor already staffs. Twelve name-labeled resealable phone pouches and the combination-lock door-table lockbox are bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $931 shop-tools startup line (about $12), staged at Session Zero Hour 4 on the door table.
  • MedicationsEvery med needs a parent-signed authorization form (name, dose, time, storage) collected at enrollment through Typeform. The meds themselves arrive on Day 1 morning: each family whose authorization form names a medication hands the labeled med bag to the instructor at the 9:15 door-table check-in (the drop-off row's Day 1 med beat), the instructor matches the bag against the med/allergy sheet in the instructor folder, and locks it in the med lockbox before the first open. After that morning, all medications live in a labeled combination-lock lockbox held by the instructor (the med lockbox: its combination is set at Session Zero Hour 4, held by the instructor only, and distinct from the door-table lockbox and cooler-bag codes), not in camper bags; every dose is logged with time and the instructor's initials beside the words in the Airtable med log, visible to the parent at pickup. EpiPens and rescue inhalers ride in the instructor's field bag during field missions and sit on the instructor's table during lessons. An epinephrine auto-injector is administered by the instructor under the camper's parent-signed authorization form (the form names the device and the technique); the instructor rehearses with a trainer pen at Session Zero and keeps the trainer pen in the field bag. The trainer pen is one reusable epinephrine auto-injector trainer, about $15, bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $931 shop-tools startup line and staged with the med lockbox at Session Zero Hour 4. Refrigerated meds go in a small lockable medication cooler bag (combination lock, code set at Session Zero Hour 4, held by the instructor only, and distinct from the door-table lockbox and med-lockbox codes) with a frozen ice pack inside, staged at Session Zero. Two reusable gel ice packs rotate through the bag: each evening the day's thawed pack goes into the instructor's home freezer when the bag leaves the venue, and each 8:45 prep the frozen pack goes back in the cooler bag before the drop-off thermometer-strip check, so the bag never opens the day on a thawed pack. The bag sits with the med lockbox at the instructor's table during lessons and rides in the instructor's field bag on field missions. Pack the instructor's field bag with its full session contents, exactly: the labeled med lockbox, the EpiPens and rescue inhalers, the epinephrine trainer pen, the lockable medication cooler bag with its frozen ice pack, the camp first-aid kit, the instructor folder, (the EpiPens and rescue inhalers named here are the Day 1 drop-off collection; camper-supplied medications return to the authorized adult at the Day 10 11:45 dismissal, so the lockbox and the cooler bag travel between sessions empty of camper prescriptions), the instructor's phone and the instructor medical card in the bag's named zip phone pocket, the field bag's abort card and the route card in the bag's front pocket (before the fresh pair files, pull the prior session's laminated abort card out of the field bag's front pocket and the prior session's laminated spare out of the instructor folder, and trash both, laminated plastic is not paper recycling, so Hour 4 never files a fresh pair over a stale one; the spare abort card files in the instructor folder at Session Zero Hour 4, per the Hour 1 print list, so the front pocket never holds both; the route card prints at Day 3 prep, so the front pocket carries only the abort card on the Day 2 safari), and the laminated allergy action plans clipped to the bag's outside panel. On field-mission mornings one full 24-bottle water case goes in the bag's side sleeve before the instructor counts the bag's contents against the Session Zero Hour 4 list, so the morning count catches a full case like everything else; the Day 2 safari draws the first case, the Day 3 verification walk draws the second, and the spent case leaves the shelf after the Day 2 walk-back count. The same morning motion moves the instructor's phone into the bag's zip phone pocket after the 8:45 inbox read, then moves the instructor medical card from its table clip into the pocket beside the phone, and the walk-back count returns the card to the table clip and the phone to the instructor table beside the clip, so the room script's clip line, the Day 2 walk-out rehearsal's pocket line, and the field script's pocket line all name the same card in its right home. The bag's named home is the equipment shelf beside the med lockbox, and it rides with the instructor on every field mission, so no medication, authorization paper, or answer key ever sits in a day-box or in a camper's reach unsupervised. The bag is carried in and out by the instructor each day and is never left at the venue overnight; a stick-on thermometer strip is checked at drop-off and logged in the same Airtable med log with the dose. The instructor does not administer anything beyond the label's instructions; anything ambiguous gets a call to the parent before the dose.
  • Scheduled midday dose on a field walk: the dose gate, the buddy witness, the shielded code.Any dose whose time falls inside a field-walk window is known at enrollment from the parent-signed authorization form, and the med/allergy sheet in the folder that rides the field bag lists it. Prevention is mapped, not improvised: at the same T-2 venue walk-through that maps the routes and checks accessibility, the instructor marks one dose gate per route, a bench or shade point off the road where twelve can sit inside one sightline, and writes it into the launch plan beside the route card's 911 location line (the route card itself does not print until Day 3 prep); the route brief on a dose day names it matter-of-factly ("we sit at the bench for a few minutes"), no announcement of whose dose it is. At the gate the group sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline on the illness bullet's sit form, a planned pause, never the "Freeze, buddies" emergency call. The dosing camper comes to the instructor's side with their buddy, who stands as the accountability witness (the same witness the lost-camper bullet names), inside the group's sightline, watching the dose and the log, never handling medication. The instructor opens the lockbox or the cooler bag with body and bag shielding the combination code from the group: the cooler bag's thermometer strip is read first and logged with the dose, the bag opens for seconds, dose out per the label and the authorization form, re-locked and zipped back into the field bag before the group stands; a water bottle from the side-sleeve case covers a dose that needs water, the empty rejoining the walk-back count. The dose is logged in the Airtable med log with time and the instructor's initials beside the words on the phone from the zip phone pocket right there at the bench, the same authorization-and-log chain the room runs. If anything is ambiguous the room rule holds on the route: call the parent before the dose, the group seated meanwhile. After the dose the aloud count resumes and the walk continues: no split, no shop entry, no one leaves the group. A restroom need at the gate still aborts the walk per the restroom bullet; a dose that comes back up hands the script to the illness-on-a-walk bullet.
  • Scheduled midday dose in the room: the instructor table, the buddy witness, the shielded code.A dose whose time falls inside the 60-minute block is known at enrollment from the parent-signed authorization form, and the med/allergy sheet in the instructor folder lists it. The dose happens at the instructor table, where the med lockbox and the lockable medication cooler bag sit during lessons: at a natural block transition the instructor calls a planned pause, never the "Freeze, buddies" emergency call, and the dosing camper comes to the instructor's side with their buddy while the group stays seated at the tables in the U inside the sightline. The buddy stands as the accountability witness (the same witness the lost-camper bullet names), watching the dose and the log, never handling medication. The instructor opens the lockbox or the cooler bag with body shielding the combination code from the group: the cooler bag's thermometer strip is read first and logged with the dose, the bag opens for seconds, dose out per the label and the authorization form, re-locked before the pause ends; the camper's own water bottle covers a dose that needs water. The dose is logged in the Airtable med log with time and the instructor's initials beside the words, the same authorization-and-log chain the field bullet names. If anything is ambiguous the room rule holds: call the parent before the dose, the group keeps working meanwhile. A dose that comes back up hands the script to the illness mid-day bullet, the sick chair and the sick kit, matching the field bullet's handoff to illness-on-a-walk.
  • Mid-session medication changes: the re-sign, the swap, the same-hour log.The med chain's four documents (the parent-signed authorization form, the med/allergy sheet, the lockbox contents, and the Airtable med log) can all go stale mid-session: a parent changes a dose, adds a new prescription, or discontinues a medication. The rule is flat: nothing enters or leaves the med chain on a verbal say-so, and no dose is given under a changed instruction until a fresh parent-signed authorization form is in the instructor's hand. The parent sends the change over the camp number the evening before (text or email through the camp number, never a verbal instruction at the door table); the instructor acknowledges in the 8:45 morning read, the overnight inbox check that opens the prep window every teaching morning, so the change never lands as a surprise during the 9:15 check-in rush. The new authorization form arrives the same morning, either a fresh DocuSign or a paper form signed at the door table at 9:15 with the group already in the U and the instructor never leaving the room; a dose under the changed instruction is not given until the signed form exists. The swap runs at the 9:15 prep window the instructor already staffs: the instructor updates the med/allergy sheet in the instructor folder, swaps the lockbox contents (the old medication is sealed in its original bag, initialed, and handed back to the parent at the 11:15 pickup the same day, never left at the venue overnight), and writes the change into the Airtable med log with time and the instructor's initials beside the words (old dose, new dose, the parent's name, "form re-signed"), the same log every dose already writes to. A discontinued medication is handed back at pickup and its row on the med/allergy sheet is struck through, initialed, and dated; a dose time that moves (a new midday dose on a field-walk day) is added to the med/allergy sheet and, where it applies, to the dose-gate brief the bullet above names, so the walk-out count knows about it. An increased dose or a brand-new prescription takes effect only after the signed form lands: a morning dose that falls before the form arrives is missed, and the miss is logged with the reason ("authorization pending"), never approximated by giving the old dose. A parent who discovers a change too late for the evening notice sends it with the camper to the door table with a signed paper form: the swap still runs at the 9:15 prep window, and the first dose under the new instruction follows the form, not the verbal. The pharmacy label on the new bottle is checked against the new form before the first dose; a label that does not match the form means the parent is called before anything is given, the room rule the dose-gate bullet already holds. The four-document chain needs no audio slot: a voicemail that names a med change is notice only (the form script above runs on the reply), and the Airtable med log's line for that dose names the notice channel ("notice by voicemail, [time]"), which is the audio's paper trail. A voicemail that contradicts the signed form in the folder loses to the form: the parent is called before anything is given, the same room rule the label-mismatch line already holds, and no dose runs under the voicemail's instruction until a re-signed form exists. No budget-line changes: the DocuSign envelope and the paper form sit inside the launch plan's existing documents; no new materials, no new prints beyond the one form. No timing changes: the swap rides the 9:15 prep window the check-in row already staffs.
  • The camp number after hours: the night's boundary, the 8:45 morning read, the named reply.The camp number is a Twilio SMS and voice line routed to the instructor's phone, provisioned at T-10w, routed and dry-run at T-6w, and three of the safety plan's rules already lean on it: the pickup-change rule, the med-change rule, and every Twilio parent ping. None of them named who reads the line after dismissal, so the first overnight message would have landed on an unread phone. The boundary is now fixed: after the last camper is collected at the 11:15 pickup, the camp number is not monitored until the next teaching morning. Parents learn this before paying: the enrollment confirmation states the hours line beside the 24-hour illness rule, the pickup-authorization list carries the camp number in its footer with the same line, and the week-of logistics pack repeats it: camp hours are 9:15 to 11:15, and messages sent overnight are read first thing each teaching morning. A true after-hours emergency is not a camp emergency: a camper home with family is a family 911 call, and anything for the next camp day waits for the morning read, because there is no second adult to staff a night desk and the instructor's off hours are off. The morning read opens the 8:45 prep window every teaching morning, about five minutes, on the instructor's own phone: every SMS and voicemail that landed since dismissal is read, and each parent message gets a "got it" reply on the camp number, so the family has a receipt before the doors open. Anything needing action is routed before the door table opens: a med change hands the script to the med-change bullet (the form-before-the-dose rule runs on the reply), a pickup change is checked against the pickup-authorization list (a same-day door-table change still gets a signature on the list, never a verbal), and an absence is written onto the paper attendance sheet, so the 9:15 count already knows the no-show. A message that lands after the morning read but before doors is read at the 9:15 check-in row the instructor already staffs, the same minute-block boundary the late-arrival rule runs on. Voicemails get a handling rule of their own, because the read promises to listen to every voicemail but no line said how long one lives on the account or what one authorizes. Every voicemail lives on the camp's Twilio account, never on the instructor's personal phone, and the Twilio voice configuration is hour-aware: a call inside camp hours rings the instructor's phone for about 20 seconds and an unanswered call hands to the camp's own Twilio voicemail greeting, so a parent's message never sits in a personal inbox the 8:45 read does not open, while a call outside camp hours skips the ring entirely and goes straight to the camp voicemail greeting, so the "not monitored" boundary is a routing fact and the instructor's phone never rings overnight; the camp's voicemail greeting states the hours line to the caller first. The Studio flow's date schedule holds the ten teaching dates (minus any sick-cancelled day, which the sick-day bullet pulls off the schedule the morning it cancels; a mid-lesson incapacitation keeps the date on the schedule while the script's Do Not Disturb step keeps the phone from ringing, because nobody can reach the Twilio console mid-crisis), entered at the T-6w build and re-verified at the T-2w step; the Say widget's greeting TTS text is read back at the same T-2w step, so the hours line the dry run heard is confirmed again before the first parent call; a calendar change in planning re-enters the dates before the first affected day, so the hour-aware ring rule always matches the real teaching day. The T-6w dry run proves both paths and both branches before launch, the test SMS answered from the same thread, the in-hours test call's voicemail played back from the Twilio console and deleted, and an after-hours test call confirming the phone never rings before the voicemail lands, so the retention rule's delete step and the silent overnight are practiced actions, not first tries on live messages. A voicemail is notice only, never authorization: a voicemail naming a med change hands the script to the med-change bullet (the reply asks for the re-signed form, and the med-change bullet's contradiction rule settles any conflict with the signed form), and a voicemail naming a pickup change is checked against the pickup-authorization list (the list holds until a change is signed on it, the same as a verbal change at the door table). A voicemail that contradicts the signed list loses to the list: the instructor calls the parent, replies on the camp number asking for a re-signed authorization, and nothing changes until the re-signed form lands. Retention is the camp's own rule, not Twilio's vendor default: a voicemail that names a med or pickup change is kept on the camp's Twilio account until the change it names is resolved and written into the log, then deleted at the next morning read; every other voicemail is deleted in the same morning read after its "got it" reply, so no voicemail lives on the account longer than it is needed. No budget-line changes: the Twilio parent-comms line, the camp number, and the instructor's phone are bought and provisioned stock. No timing changes: about five minutes inside the existing 30-minute prep the instructor's paid hours already cover.
  • Allergies and foodThe room is nut-aware: no nuts in snacks, all snack ingredients carry labels, the ingredient list is in the week-of logistics pack. The Day 8 "taste panel" is a critique panel of camper work, not a food tasting: the only food at camp is the shelf-stable snacks bought in the week-before window. Any camper with a food allergy on file gets their snack portion served first, from sealed packages, before shared surfaces touch it. Allergy action plans from parents are printed, laminated, and clipped to the instructor's field bag, next to the EpiPens.
  • Allergic reaction in the room: the action plan on the clip, the EpiPen on file, the 911 call. The field-bag rule carries EpiPens and rescue inhalers, and the Tier chain names allergic reaction as a Tier 2 example with airway involvement as Tier 3, epinephrine per the authorization form first, then 911, then the parent, then the incident form. But until this bullet no room form existed: the Tier chain never says what the instructor does in the first minute at a table; the Allergies and food bullet above is prevention, nut-aware snacks, sealed portions, the ingredient list, laminated action plans clipped to the field bag next to the EpiPens, and names no first-minute form; and the sting room bullet's scrape, cover, and airway watch is sting-specific, not a snack-time reaction form. When a camper reports a reaction at the tables, the Day 1 freeze governs at once: hands off tools, the group holds the U in the sightline, nobody crowding the camper, and the door and windows stay as they are. The instructor reads the camper's laminated allergy action plan off the field-bag clip and follows it exactly as written, the named symptoms, the named response, the named medication and dose, no improvisation, the same no-diagnosis rule the distressed-camper bullet runs. Airway involvement or suspected anaphylaxis runs the Tier 3 chain at once: the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first, then 911 from the instructor-table phone with the venue address and street cross from the session contact card, then the parent on the camp number, then the incident form, in that order. Hives or swelling without airway involvement: the plan's named antihistamine path only if the plan and the signed authorization form both name it, the parent called at once on the camp number, and the camper stays at the instructor's side in the sightline, not back at the tables, until the parent answers. The episode logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with the symptom time, the action-plan steps followed, the epinephrine time if given, the 911 time, and the parent-contact time. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because the EpiPens, the med lockbox, the laminated plans, the freeze, the U, the session contact card, and the Airtable incident log already exist, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged.
  • Allergic reaction on a field walk: the bag in hand, the plan on the clip, the abort on the airway signs. The sting walk bullet covers a sting-triggered reaction on a walk, and the Tier chain names allergic reaction only in the abstract, so a non-sting reaction on a walk has no first-minute form: a food trace, a plant contact, or an unknown trigger can present hives, swelling, or airway trouble on the route, and the sting bullet's slow walk-away and no-swat rule do not fit a case with no insect, while the room allergy bullet's form needs the tables and the U. The difference from the room form is where the EpiPens ride: the Allergies and food bullet clips the laminated action plans and the EpiPens to the instructor's field bag, so on a walk the epinephrine is in the instructor's hand, not at the instructor table. When a camper reports a reaction on the walk, the instructor calls "Freeze, buddies" once, the group's field-freeze call, and the campers sit off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, while the reacting camper sits at the instructor's side. The instructor reads the camper's laminated allergy action plan off the field-bag clip and follows it exactly as written, the named symptoms, the named response, the named medication and dose, no improvisation, the same no-diagnosis rule the room bullet runs. If the trigger is a sting, the sting walk bullet's form runs instead and this bullet stands down. Airway involvement or suspected anaphylaxis runs the Tier 3 chain at once: the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first, then 911 with the exact location read off the route card's printed 911 line, then the parent on the camp number from the field bag's named zip phone pocket, then the incident form, in that order, and the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, the aloud count, the group walking out together with the reacting camper at the instructor's side, no split. Hives or swelling without airway involvement is Tier 2: the plan's named antihistamine path only if the plan and the signed authorization form both name it, a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes, the camper at the instructor's side for the rest of the walk with the buddy inside the sightline, and the walk resumes at a slow pace only when the camper says they feel able. Every reaction logs to the Airtable incident log on return with the time, the place on the route, the trigger if known, the action-plan steps followed, the epinephrine time if given, the 911 time, and the parent-contact time. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because the EpiPens, the laminated plans, the field bag, the freeze, the route card, the camp number, and the Airtable incident log already exist, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes.
  • Accommodations and access, one adultThe camp enrolls from the general public, so the plan names what one adult can support and what it cannot, before any family pays. The Typeform intake asks the accommodation question beside the med authorization and the pickup-authorization list: IEP or 504 plan, ADHD, autism or sensory needs, dyslexia or low vision, mobility, or anything else the family wants the instructor to know. Every requesting family gets a 15-minute accommodation call with the operator in the week before the session (T-1, operator labor like the other week-before tasks, outside the instructor's paid hours); the call ends with a written agreement, one page per camper, filed in the instructor folder at Session Zero Hour 4 beside the pickup-authorization list and the med/allergy sheet, so the instructor teaches from paper, not memory. Named supports, all engineered for the one-adult line-of-sight model: large-print handouts, the ten week-before layout files reprinted at 18pt, riding the Hour 1 print run at about 10 extra sheets per requesting camper, inside the run's margin; seating, the U's two end seats carry extra space and a standing option, no separate table, because no camper sits outside the instructor's sightline; sensory, fidget tools from shop stock at the camper's own seat and noise-reducing earplugs for the loud blocks (the Day 5 test lab, the Day 7 gauntlet), with self-directed sensory breaks at the camper's own table, never a separate room, because there is no second adult to staff one; the instructor sets the fidget tools at the T-1-named camper's own seat from the caddy during the Day 1 seat staging, where they stay all session under the same camper-kept home motion the camp pens carry, so no collection step is owed; the instructor issues the 12 earplug pairs from the caddy before each loud block (the Day 5 test lab and the Day 7 robotics gauntlet) and collects them back into the caddy after the block, so the reusable set is whole for the next loud block and rides the caddy the 11:15 pickup row returns to the shelf; reading, the day's handout steps read aloud to the whole room at the 0:55 handoff, about 60 seconds, and the five quiet-voice project blocks (Days 3, 6, 8, 9, 10) already permit task-words speech to a partner, so a buddy can re-read a step on request without breaking the silence rule the other five days hold. Field missions: route accessibility is checked at the same T-2 venue walk-through that maps the route; a route the whole cohort cannot walk is a route nobody walks, and the mapped indoor alternate runs instead, because the single-group rule has no accessible-route exception that needs a second adult. Honest limits, stated at enrollment: the camp cannot provide 1:1 aides, cannot modify the 1:12 line-of-sight model, cannot run a separate quiet room, and cannot deliver behavioral interventions beyond the published three-rule room contract. If the accommodation call finds the camp cannot support a camper safely, the family hears that before paying, and an enrolled family in the same position gets a full Stripe refund on the tuition collection and refunds API the launch plan already carries. Midday ADHD meds ride the existing med authorization and med-log chain; no new procedure. No budget-line changes: large print is venue-printer paper inside the $90 snacks-and-prints operating line; fidget tools and earplugs are reusable shop stock inside the $931 shop-tools startup line.
  • Incident handlingThree tiers. Tier 1 (bandaid level: paper cut, blister): treated on the spot, logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp. Tier 2 (beyond a bandaid: burn, deeper cut, twisted ankle, allergic reaction without airway involvement): first aid from the camp first-aid kit, parent pinged via Twilio within 15 minutes, incident report form filled the same hour. Tier 3 (head injury, suspected concussion, lost camper): 911 first, then the parent call, then the incident form. Tier 3 (airway involvement / suspected anaphylaxis): the instructor administers the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first, then calls 911, then the parent, then the incident form. Group freeze governs every incident while one camper is being treated: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), buddy pairs check each other in, and treatment happens at the instructor table inside the room, where the first-aid kit already sits, so line of sight never breaks. Nothing resumes until the instructor counts twelve. Every incident, including Tier 1, gets a timestamped log entry; the log is exported to the parent at session end. The camp's one-adult design means no one chases a runaway while 11 wait: a camper who leaves the room, or who walks away from the group on a field walk, triggers Tier 3 immediately, the instructor calls 911 and counts the group. The form is one page, printed blank in Session Zero Hour 1: 12 copies filed in the instructor folder at Hour 4, so it is on the instructor table during lessons and in the field bag on field walks. Its fields are fixed: date, time, tier, location (room station, or the route name with the nearest landmark), the camper's full name (a formal safety record handed to the child's own parent at pickup, extending the full-name exception the Day 9 rule grants the take-home diplomas, the Airtable roster, and the pickup-authorization list), what happened, first aid given, the name of the parent contacted and the time of the Twilio ping, the 911 call time if any, the buddy witness's first name, the instructor's initials, and the follow-up needed. 'Filled the same hour' runs at the next block transition, never mid-lesson: the group settles into the next counted block with hands-off tools, the instructor counts twelve aloud, then fills the form at the instructor table in line of sight, so the count is never unwound to do paperwork. The parent of the involved camper signs the form at pickup; the timestamped entry goes into the Airtable incident log when the group is back at the venue (the paper log sheet is the tech-outage backstop); completed forms stay in the instructor folder through move-out; at the move-out close, before the numbered sheets retire, the instructor files every completed incident report form in the trip folder beside the retired numbered sheets, so the session's formal safety records archive with the session and the next session's instructor folder opens on the blank stock only. In the same move-out beat, the instructor files the session's ten filled end-of-day summary digest pages in the trip folder beside the completed incident report forms, so the session's daily record archives with the session and the next session's instructor folder opens on the ten fresh blank digest templates only. In the same move-out beat, before the numbered sheets retire, the instructor pulls the session's med/allergy sheet and the session's pickup-authorization list out of the instructor folder, tears both into narrow strips by hand until no camper name, medication, allergy, or pickup adult reads whole, and bins the strips in the venue trash. In the same beat the instructor pulls the session's hospital handoff sheets out of the instructor folder, the blank spare and whichever second copy the morning count's found-or-re-printed rule left in hand, and tears them into narrow strips too: the copies carry the T-2-mapped emergency department of this session's venue, stale the moment the next session's T-2 visit maps its own, and any filled handoff block carries a camper's name with the med and allergy summary, so the sheets are destroyed, never archived in the trip folder and never recycled whole; the next session's Hour 4 files its two fresh venue-mapped copies into a folder that holds no stale sheets. The two sheets name minors' health details and pickup contacts, so they are destroyed, never archived in the trip folder and never recycled whole; the destroy closes the reusable-folder chain the Session Zero Hour 4 assembly opens, and the next session's instructor folder opens on the current roster's sheets only. In the same move-out beat the instructor pulls the session's one-page paper attendance sheet and the session's one-page paper med/incident log sheet out of the instructor folder: any still-untranscribed absence or outage entry on either sheet runs through the founder's move-out read-off first, the check-plus-transcription on the first regained connectivity before the move-out closes, and then the instructor tears both sheets into narrow strips by hand until no camper name, absence mark, or incident entry reads whole, and bins the strips in the venue trash, so the sheets are destroyed, never archived in the trip folder and never recycled whole; the next session's Hour 4 files its two fresh backstop sheets into a folder that holds no stale roster. In the same move-out beat the instructor unclips the session's laminated allergy action plans from the field bag's outside panel: each laminated plan carries a minor's name, food allergy, and EpiPen authorization, and the cold lamination pouch cannot be torn like paper, so the instructor cuts each laminated plan into small pieces with the table-supply scissors until no name, allergy, or authorization reads whole and bins the pieces in the venue trash, so the laminated plans are destroyed, never archived in the trip folder and never reused; the next session's Hour 4 clips its fresh laminated plans to a bag that holds no stale set. In the same move-out beat the instructor pulls the labeled clock-battery envelope off the door table (the envelope the Hour 4 staging set beside the flatbed scanner), counts the AA cells inside it against the 4-pack, and files the envelope in the labeled table-supply caddy on the equipment shelf, which the section 08 move-out packs, so no camp-labeled envelope of batteries sits on venue furniture between sessions and the next session's Hour 4 re-stages the envelope from the caddy.
  • Camper illness mid-day: one adult, no side roomWhen a camper reports nausea, headache, or a feverish feeling, or vomits, the sick-camper script runs, engineered so the instructor never leaves the room and line of sight never breaks. The sick camper moves to the sick chair (an ordinary chair pulled beside the instructor's table), stays inside the instructor's sightline with the other eleven, and no one leaves the building. The instructor takes the temperature with the sick kit's digital thermometer (100.4 F / 38 C or higher counts as fever) while the other eleven keep working, so no teaching minute is lost. A fever or any vomiting means same-day parent pickup: the instructor sends the pickup message over the camp number, the same Twilio chain that carries the 9:15 and 11:15 pings, and the camper rests in the sick chair with a water bottle until a listed adult arrives, expected within 30 minutes. If no parent or emergency contact answers within 30 minutes, the instructor works the pickup-authorization list top to bottom; the camper stays in the sick chair, resting, until an authorized adult collects them. Headache or stomach ache without fever or vomiting gets water, the sick chair, and twenty minutes: if it passes, the camper rejoins the group; if it does not, the parent gets the same pickup message. Vomit on the floor gets the two-minute spill treatment (gloves on, absorbent pads down, the mess bagged in the sick kit's zip bags, disinfectant wipes across the surface) during a natural pause in the lesson while the room keeps working. After any episode the instructor tops the sick kit's working stock (gloves, pads, zip bags, wipes, probe covers, emesis bags) back up from the sealed refill pack beside the first-aid kit at the next natural pause, before the next day's prep. A camper who vomited or ran a fever may not return the next day unless they have been symptom-free for 24 hours without medication, and the enrollment confirmation states the 24-hour rule before any parent pays. Every illness episode is logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, the same log the Tier chain writes to. If symptoms escalate (airway involvement, signs of anaphylaxis, any injury riding with the illness), the Tier 2/Tier 3 chain takes over from the sick chair, and nothing in this bullet changes it. No budget-line changes: the sick kit is a new tray beside the camp first-aid kit (digital thermometer with probe covers, disposable gloves, absorbent pads, emesis bags, disinfectant wipes, zip bags), bought once as reusable stock inside the $931 shop-tools startup line, about $35, assembled and staged at Session Zero Hour 4 beside the first-aid kit on the equipment shelf; it sits on the instructor table during lessons and rides inside the camp first-aid kit's bag on field missions, so the exact field-bag manifest the meds bullet lists does not change.
  • Camper illness on a field walk: the sit, the abort, the carry-out ruleThe room sick script needs a room: a sick chair, an instructor table, a floor to treat, and none of them ride on a field walk. The kit does: the sick kit rides inside the first-aid kit's bag in the instructor's field bag on every field mission, so the emesis bags, the thermometer, the gloves, and the zip bags are in the instructor's hand on the route. Detection is the same trigger as the room script: a camper says they feel sick or vomits on the route. The instructor calls "Freeze, buddies" once, the group's field-freeze call, and the campers sit off the road in buddy pairs, inside the sightline, while the sick camper sits at the instructor's side. An emesis bag comes out of the first-aid bag immediately, and any used bag is zipped and stowed back in the first-aid kit's bag (the floor spill treatment belongs to the venue; the bag is the treatment on the route). The instructor takes the temperature right there with the sick kit's thermometer; the fever line is still 100.4 F / 38 C. The mild case rides the heat bullet's ten-minute rule: headache or nausea without fever or vomiting gets water and about ten minutes seated at the instructor's side, the rest of the group in sight; if it passes, the group resumes the walk with the buddy inside the sightline the whole time. Fever or any vomiting on the walk means same-day parent pickup, sent over the camp number from the field (the instructor's phone rides the field bag's named zip phone pocket), but the pickup happens at the venue: the instructor runs the lightning bullet's abort form, the aloud count, and the group walks out together along the mapped route, the sick camper at the instructor's side, no split, no shop entry. On return the camper rests at the venue until the listed adult arrives, expected within 30 minutes, run through the same top-to-bottom pickup-authorization ladder the room script runs, and the day continues indoors on the mapped alternate. A camper who cannot walk (cannot stand, fainting, cannot keep water down) skips the ladder and fires the Tier 3 chain, 911 first with the exact location read off the route card, the group holding together at the last point off the road, exactly the escalation the room script names. The 24-hour symptom-free return rule and the timestamped incident log hold unchanged. No budget-line changes: the sick kit is bought and rides the field bag already, and prevention rides the existing walk-out brief, which adds "say so right away if you feel sick on the walk" to the no-shame line the restroom rule already states at every walk-out.
  • Hospital transport: the instructor stays, the paperwork ridesTier 3 calls 911 first, and when the paramedics decide a camper goes to the emergency department, the rule is fixed: the instructor stays with the eleven and the camper rides with EMS. This is the only honest configuration under the one-adult model: the instructor cannot leave eleven children unsupervised, and paramedics do not take direction from a chaperone in the rig. The safety plan says this plainly at enrollment so no family is surprised by it. The parent call follows the 911 call (Tier 3 order, unchanged), and the parent meets the camper at the hospital, not at the camp: the parent's drive starts on the 911 call, so the parent and the ambulance arrive in the same window. If the parent cannot be reached, the instructor works the pickup-authorization list top to bottom while the camper still rides; the signed emergency-medical-treatment authorization (below) covers the gap, and the camp number keeps publishing the hospital name and address until a listed adult confirms they are en route. What rides with the camper is paper, prepared before anything goes wrong: Session Zero Hour 4 files a one-page hospital handoff sheet in the instructor folder beside the med/allergy sheet and the pickup-authorization list. The sheet names the nearest emergency department to the venue (mapped at the T-2 site visit, address and phone printed on the Hour 1 print run) and carries a handoff block the instructor fills in about thirty seconds: camper name, med and allergy summary copied from the med/allergy sheet, what happened, what was administered (epinephrine, meds from the med log). Two copies are printed: one goes with the paramedics, one stays in the folder. On field missions the sheet rides in the field bag inside the instructor folder, which the field-bag manifest already carries, so a Tier 3 on a walk has the sheet in hand with no day-box pull; the same two copies serve every mission, riding out and back with the folder, and the morning count's found-or-re-printed rule replaces a copy a paramedic transport consumes. Enrollment consent: the Typeform intake carries an emergency-medical-treatment authorization beside the med authorization and the pickup-authorization list, signed before payment: consent for paramedics and emergency-department staff to treat the camper when a parent cannot be reached in time, and explicit agreement that the instructor remains with the group and does not ride in the ambulance. The enrollment confirmation restates the rule in one line: "In an emergency the instructor stays with the other campers; a parent meets the child at the hospital." No budget-line changes: the handoff sheet is paper folded into the $90 snacks-and-prints operating line; the nearest-ER mapping rides the T-2 site visit that already walks the venue.
  • Emergency: fire, lockdown, lost camper, earthquakeThe venue's evacuation plan is walked with the instructor at the T-2 site visit and rehearsed with the campers in the first 10 minutes of Day 1: the lockdown positions at the far wall with attendance called silently and the door lock shown, then drop, cover, and hold at the tables, then the lost-camper freeze with the count and the inward search from the last known position, then the walk to the assembly point, headcount there; no re-entry until emergency personnel or venue staff give the all-clear. The Day 1 walk is an announced drill: the instructor ends it aloud at the assembly point before leading the group back inside, so the rehearsal never models re-entry during a real alarm. Lockdown: doors lock from inside, campers to the far wall, attendance called silently, Twilio message to parents queued but not sent until the all-clear (no panicked texts mid-incident). Earthquake: when shaking starts, campers drop, cover, and hold under the tables away from windows, and the instructor calls the drop aloud; once shaking stops, the instructor counts aloud and walks the group to the assembly point on the fire form, no re-entry until venue staff or emergency personnel give the all-clear; an aftershock repeats drop, cover, and hold wherever the group stands; if the room smells of gas or shows structural damage, the group stays at the assembly point and the Twilio parent message goes out with the pickup instruction once the group is out of the building. Lost camper drill: the group freezes, the instructor counts, the venue is searched inward from the last known position; if the camper is not found in 5 minutes, it becomes a Tier 3 call. That form is room-only: on a field walk there is no venue to search inward and the instructor cannot leave the group to search, so the field variant in the bullet below governs. Instructor incapacitation: if the instructor is hurt and cannot lead, the two oldest campers run the room by script, rehearsed in the same Day 1 walk: the two oldest campers present take the two named roles, the card's face-down spot beside the phone is shown in the walk, seniors where enrolled, because enrollment sets no age-mix floor and a cohort could be all juniors; everyone stays in the room, the door locks from inside, the oldest camper calls 911 from the instructor's phone (kept on the instructor table during lessons), but first switches the phone to Do Not Disturb, and the script names the locked-phone path because the phone is locked and the oldest camper does not know the passcode: Control Center on an iPhone, or the quick-settings shade on an Android, opens from the lock screen with no passcode, so the oldest camper taps Do Not Disturb on there, the T-6w-hardened Do Not Disturb that a redial cannot pierce (repeated calls off, no Emergency Bypass contacts), because with the day still on the Studio date schedule a parent calling 9:15 to 11:15 would otherwise ring the phone over the 911 call and interrupt the dispatcher, then taps the Emergency call button on the lock screen and dials 911 with no passcode; the hardened Do Not Disturb sends every incoming call to the camp's voicemail greeting while leaving outgoing calls untouched; when the 911 call ends, the oldest camper turns Do Not Disturb off by the same lock-screen path, no passcode needed, because 911 dispatchers call back the reporting number, and the hardened Do Not Disturb that kept the line clear during the call would send that callback to the camp's voicemail greeting unheard: the callback rings through and the oldest camper answers the dispatcher first, and a parent call ringing in the same minute goes unanswered while the dispatcher is on the line, because the parent message the Tier 3 order holds is armed at the T-6w build, not queued mid-crisis: a pre-written "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held" broadcast to every session parent, the founder's cell, and the designated break-glass adult's cell, scheduled in the Twilio console for a fixed send at 11:16 that teaching day, fires only if the instructor never cancels it, and no camper touches the phone to send it, because the phone is locked and nobody knows the passcode; the founder's cell and the break-glass adult's cell are on the recipient list so the W-2 instructor's employer and the keyholder learn the same minute parents do, which is the trigger for the owner-login fallback named in the sick-day bullet below; the second oldest's role is the aloud buddy count, keeping the group inside the instructor's sightline while the oldest camper runs the phone; then the oldest camper reads the venue address off the reverse of the pickup-authorization list, and if the dispatcher asks what is wrong with the instructor, reads the instructor medical card clipped face-down beside the phone on the instructor table (the card written in the pilot prep week and re-verified at the T-6w build: conditions, allergies, current meds, emergency contact), read to the dispatcher only, never aloud to the room, nobody queues a parent message mid-crisis, because nobody can reach the Twilio console from the locked phone; the pre-scheduled broadcast fires at 11:16 on its own if the instructor never cancels it, so the parent comms still arrive after the emergency response, the order the Tier 3 chain holds, and nobody touches the med lockbox, the tools, or any medication. The script is walked at the parent walkthrough night so every parent knows exactly what their child has been told to do.
  • Instructor incapacitation in the room: the door locks, the two oldest campers, the phone on the table. The only named incapacitation form until this bullet is the field-walk variant: the room response lives unnamed inside the Emergency bullet's body, so a solo instructor scanning the safety section for the room case meets the field-walk title first and has to know the room form is nested. The room form, now named: if the instructor is hurt and cannot lead, the two oldest campers run the room by script, rehearsed in the same Day 1 walk as the Emergency drill, the two oldest campers present taking the two named roles, the card's face-down spot beside the phone shown in the walk, seniors where enrolled because enrollment sets no age-mix floor and a cohort could be all juniors; everyone stays in the room, the door locks from inside, the oldest camper calls 911 from the instructor's phone, kept on the instructor table during lessons, but first switches the phone to Do Not Disturb, and the script names the locked-phone path because the phone is locked and the oldest camper does not know the passcode: Control Center on an iPhone, or the quick-settings shade on an Android, opens from the lock screen with no passcode, so the oldest camper taps Do Not Disturb on there, the T-6w-hardened Do Not Disturb that a redial cannot pierce, repeated calls off and no Emergency Bypass contacts, because with the day still on the Studio date schedule a parent calling 9:15 to 11:15 would otherwise ring the phone over the 911 call and interrupt the dispatcher, then taps the Emergency call button on the lock screen and dials 911 with no passcode; the hardened Do Not Disturb sends every incoming call to the camp's voicemail greeting while leaving outgoing calls untouched; when the 911 call ends, the oldest camper turns Do Not Disturb off by the same lock-screen path, no passcode needed, because 911 dispatchers call back the reporting number, and the hardened Do Not Disturb that kept the line clear during the call would send that callback to the camp's voicemail greeting unheard: the callback rings through and the oldest camper answers the dispatcher first, and a parent call ringing in the same minute goes unanswered while the dispatcher is on the line, because the parent message the Tier 3 order holds is armed at the T-6w build, not queued mid-crisis: a pre-written "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held" broadcast to every session parent, the founder's cell, and the designated break-glass adult's cell, scheduled in the Twilio console for a fixed send at 11:16 that teaching day, fires only if the instructor never cancels it, and no camper touches the phone to send it, because the phone is locked and nobody knows the passcode; the founder's cell and the break-glass adult's cell are on the recipient list so the W-2 instructor's employer and the keyholder learn the same minute parents do, which is the trigger for the owner-login fallback named in the sick-day bullet below; the second oldest's role is the aloud buddy count, keeping the group inside the instructor's sightline while the oldest camper runs the phone; then the oldest camper reads the venue address off the reverse of the pickup-authorization list, and if the dispatcher asks what is wrong with the instructor, reads the instructor medical card clipped face-down beside the phone on the instructor table, the card written in the pilot prep week and re-verified at the T-6w build: conditions, allergies, current meds, emergency contact, read to the dispatcher only, never aloud to the room, nobody queues a parent message mid-crisis, because nobody can reach the Twilio console from the locked phone; the pre-scheduled broadcast fires at 11:16 on its own if the instructor never cancels it, so the parent comms still arrive after the emergency response, the order the Tier 3 chain holds, and nobody touches the med lockbox, the tools, or any medication. The script is walked at the parent walkthrough night so every parent knows exactly what their child has been told to do. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because the phone, the medical card, the pickup-authorization list, the door lock, the Twilio console, and the sightline all already exist, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged.
  • Instructor incapacitation on a field walk: the route-grounded variantThe room script names the instructor table, the lockable door, and the venue address off the pickup-list reverse, and none of those exist on a field walk: the instructor is down on the sidewalk, the phone is in the field bag, and the venue address is the wrong address. The field variant is rehearsed once at the first field walk-out, the Day 2 safari count, in the two minutes the count already takes, the walk-out count names the card in the zip phone pocket, with the same two oldest campers taking the named roles, and it is walked at the parent walkthrough night beside the room script, so the promise made to parents names both.
