The freeze call is the oldest camper saying "Freeze, buddies" once: the group stops, buddy pairs hold, everyone sits on the grass edge or the sidewalk, never in the road, and nobody leaves the route. The oldest camper pulls the instructor's phone out of the field bag's named zip phone pocket, a pocket set at Session Zero Hour 4 separate from the front pocket that holds the abort cards, and the script names the locked-phone path because the phone is locked and the oldest camper does not know the passcode: the oldest camper opens Control Center on an iPhone, or the quick-settings shade on an Android, from the lock screen with no passcode, taps Do Not Disturb on, the T-6w-hardened Do Not Disturb that a redial cannot pierce (repeated calls off, no Emergency Bypass contacts), because with the day still on the Studio date schedule a parent calling 9:15 to 11:15 would otherwise ring the phone over the 911 call, then taps the Emergency call button on the lock screen and dials 911 with no passcode; when the 911 call ends, the oldest camper turns Do Not Disturb off by the same lock-screen path, no passcode needed, so the dispatcher's callback rings through, and answers the dispatcher first, a parent call ringing in the same minute going unanswered while the dispatcher is on the line, because the parent message the Tier 3 order holds is armed at the T-6w build, not queued mid-crisis: the same pre-written broadcast, fixed send at 11:16 that teaching day, fires only if the instructor never cancels it, and nobody queues anything from the locked phone; if the instructor can speak, the instructor talks to dispatch, and if the instructor cannot, the oldest camper reads the route card's printed 911 line: the route street, the nearest cross street, and the mapped hard shelter, set when the route was mapped at the T-2 site visit, because the pickup-list reverse names the venue and the group is not at the venue. On the Day 2 around-the-block safari the group is venue-adjacent, so the script falls back to the venue address off the pickup-list reverse; on a mapped-route walk it reads the route card; if the dispatcher asks what is wrong with the instructor, the oldest camper reads the instructor medical card from the zip phone pocket (the card written in the pilot prep week and re-verified at the T-6w build: conditions, allergies, current meds, emergency contact), read to the dispatcher only, never aloud to the group. The second oldest runs the aloud buddy count, keeps the group inside the instructor's sightline, and nobody touches the med lockbox, the tools, the field kits, or the epinephrine, exactly like the room script. The Twilio parent message is never queued mid-crisis: it is pre-scheduled at the T-6w build and fires at 11:16 only if the instructor never cancels it, so the parent comms still arrive after the emergency response, the same order the room script and the Tier 3 chain hold. Every field incident logs to the Airtable incident log when the group is back at the venue, and the sick-day bullet's mid-lesson trigger still applies: if the day cannot finish, the day counts as cancelled for refund purposes. No budget-line changes: paper inside the $90 snacks-and-prints operating line; no timing changes, the rehearsal rides the walk-out count.
  • Lost camper on a field walk: the field variantThe room lost-camper drill (freeze, count, the venue searched inward from the last known position, Tier 3 at five minutes) assumes four walls and a spare pair of hands: on a field walk there is no venue to search and one adult cannot leave eleven campers to search for one. Detection is the count-out/count-back ritual the field-missions bullet already runs at every transition: the gap is found at a count, or the moment the instructor sees it in the sightline. The instructor calls "Freeze, buddies" once, and the group stops in buddy pairs, sits at the grass edge or on the sidewalk, off the road, inside the instructor's sightline, nobody leaving the route. The instructor re-counts aloud and the missing camper's buddy names what they last saw: where, when. If the missing camper is visible in the instructor's line of sight, the group walks as one, single file, together to close the gap, then reforms buddy pairs and re-counts aloud; nobody runs ahead and the single-group rule never breaks. If the missing camper is not visible, it is Tier 3 immediately, the same order the runaway line holds: 911 first, from the instructor's phone in the field bag's named zip phone pocket, the location read off the route card's printed 911 line (route street, nearest cross street, mapped hard shelter) on a mapped-route walk, or the venue address off the pickup-list reverse on the Day 2 around-the-block safari, the same fallback the incapacitation variant uses; the missing camper's name comes off the pickup-authorization list in the instructor folder, which rides the field bag, and the clothing description comes from the buddy, the accountability witness. Nobody searches: no one chases a runaway while 11 wait holds on the walk exactly as in the room. The group holds together at the last-seen point on the mapped route, off the road, where the instructor can see the route both ways, and moves only as one group, and only on the dispatcher's direction, to the nearest T-2 hard shelter or back to the venue. The Twilio parent call goes out right after the 911 call, the Tier 3 order, unchanged. Every field incident logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp when the group is back at the venue. The field variant is rehearsed once at the Day 3 walk-out count in the two minutes the count already takes (Day 2's count carries the incapacitation rehearsal), and it is walked at the parent walkthrough night beside the room script and the incapacitation variant, so the promise made to parents names all three. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the rehearsal rides the count the instructor already staffs.
  • Earthquake on a field walk: the open-ground holdThe room earthquake procedure (drop, cover, and hold under the tables away from windows) assumes a room: on a field walk there are no tables and no windows, so the form changes but the body mechanics do not. When shaking starts on a walk, the instructor calls the drop aloud, the same call the room procedure uses, and the group drops, covers, and holds in open ground away from buildings, trees, power lines, streetlights, and overpasses, heads and necks covered with arms and backpacks, nobody running and nobody sheltering under a tree or an overpass. Once shaking stops, the instructor counts aloud in buddy pairs, checks for injuries, and walks the group as one, single file, to the mapped hard shelter on the route card on a mapped-route walk, or back to the venue on the Day 2 around-the-block safari, the same destinations the field lost-camper variant and the incapacitation variant already use; the group moves only as one and only on that walk, never split. An aftershock repeats the hold wherever the group stands, the same sentence the room procedure already holds. If a camper is injured, it is Tier 3 immediately, 911 first from the instructor phone in the field bag zip phone pocket with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, the same order the field lost-camper variant holds, and the Twilio parent message goes out right after the 911 call. Every field incident logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp when the group is back at the venue. No new rehearsal minute is needed: the Day 1 room rehearsal already teaches the drop, cover, and hold body form, and this bullet names only where that form happens on a walk and where the group walks after, so the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the hold rides the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Lockdown on a field walk: off the street, silent, behind a locked doorThe room lockdown form (doors lock from inside, campers to the far wall, attendance called silently, the Twilio parent message queued but not sent until the all-clear) assumes a room: on a field walk there is no far wall and no door to lock, so the form changes but the silence does not. When the instructor sees a threatening person near the route, or police activity closing the street the group is walking, the instructor moves the group as one, single file, off the street to the mapped hard shelter on the route card on a mapped-route walk, or back to the venue on the Day 2 around-the-block safari, the same destinations the field earthquake bullet already uses; if the shelter lies in the threat's direction, the group instead holds behind the nearest building wall off the street, out of sight, and does not move again until the instructor calls the move. At the shelter the door locks if the shelter has a lock, the group stays silent, and the instructor counts silently in buddy pairs, no names aloud, the field form of the room form's silent attendance. If the threat is ongoing or anyone is hurt, it is Tier 3 immediately, 911 first from the instructor phone in the field bag zip phone pocket with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, the same order the field earthquake bullet holds, and the Twilio parent message goes out only after the all-clear, the same order the room form holds. Every field incident logs to the Airtable incident log on return. This is the emergency exception to the walk's no-entering-buildings routine rule (the restroom bullet's rule governs routine stops, not a threat): the shelter is already on the route card, so no new stop is invented mid-walk. No new rehearsal minute is needed: the Day 1 room rehearsal already teaches the silent attendance and the far-wall form, and this bullet names only where that form happens on a walk and where the group walks after, so the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the hold rides the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Fire on a field walk: upwind, away from smoke, to the hard shelter. The room fire form (walk to the assembly point on the fire form) assumes a building to leave: on a field walk the group is already outside, so the danger is flame and smoke along the route, not evacuation from a room. When the instructor sees fire, heavy smoke, or a burning building or vehicle near the route, or smells smoke thick enough to cut visibility, the instructor moves the group as one, single file, upwind and away from the smoke, never toward it and never through it, nobody running, mouths and noses covered with shirts if smoke is in the air. The destination is the mapped hard shelter on the route card on a mapped-route walk, or back to the venue on the Day 2 around-the-block safari, the same destinations the field earthquake and lockdown bullets already use; if the shelter lies toward the fire or the smoke, the group instead holds in open ground upwind of the smoke and does not move again until the instructor calls the move. Once clear, the instructor counts aloud in buddy pairs and checks for injuries and breathing distress. An injury or breathing distress is Tier 3 immediately, 911 first from the instructor phone in the field bag zip phone pocket with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, the same order the field earthquake bullet holds, and the Twilio parent message goes out right after the 911 call. The group does not walk back toward the fire and does not restart the walk until the instructor calls the move and the route is clear; if the route stays blocked, the group walks back to the venue as one. Every field incident logs to the Airtable incident log on return. This bullet invents no new stop mid-walk: the shelter is already on the route card, and entering it follows the same emergency exception the lockdown bullet already states; the upwind hold, used when the shelter lies toward the fire or the smoke, uses open ground, not a building entry, so the walk's no-entering-buildings routine rule is untouched for routine stops. No new rehearsal minute is needed: the Day 1 room rehearsal already teaches the walk to the assembly point and the aloud count, and this bullet names only the upwind direction and the destination on a walk, so the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the hold rides the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Stranger approach on a field walk: the group keeps walking, nobody stops alone. The room visitor rule (the door locks to visitors at 9:30, a late camper waits at the door with the parent) assumes a door: on a field walk there is no door and no parent at the curb, so the form changes but the single-group rule does not. When a stranger who is not threatening speaks to the group, offers something, asks campers questions, or tries to walk along with them, the instructor answers alone with one line, "Thanks, we are a camp group on a walk, we need to keep moving," and the group keeps walking as one, single file, nobody stopping, nobody splitting off, nobody answering for themselves, nobody accepting anything, nobody giving a name, a school, or any personal detail, and nobody following the stranger anywhere. The instructor does not debate, does not trade contact details, and does not leave the group to talk privately. After the group has passed, the instructor counts aloud in buddy pairs and checks that no camper stayed behind. If the stranger follows the group, blocks the path, or turns threatening, the form escalates to the field lockdown bullet: off the street, silent, behind a locked door at the mapped hard shelter, and Tier 3 immediately if the threat is ongoing or anyone is hurt, 911 first from the instructor phone in the field bag zip phone pocket with the location read off the route card printed 911 line. A non-threatening approach that ends when the group walks on is not a Tier 3 call and sends no mid-walk parent message; it logs to the Airtable incident log on return, with the time, the place on the route, and what was said. This bullet invents no new stop mid-walk: the group never stops for a stranger, and the escalation destination is the hard shelter already on the route card under the same emergency exception the lockdown bullet already states. No new rehearsal minute is needed: the walk-out count already rehearses moving as one and the aloud count, and this bullet names only the instructor one-line script and the no-stop rule on a walk, so the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the walk-on rides the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Stranger at the door in the room: door stays locked, verify, the room holds, the call line. The field bullet above names the form for a stranger meeting the group on a walk, but until this bullet no first-minute room form existed: the check-in rule (the door locks to visitors at 9:30, a late camper waits at the door with the parent) is prevention, not a response, and the photo-ID door rule (campers leave only with a person on the pickup-authorization list) names no script for an adult at the locked door who claims pickup rights but is not on the list, or any other stranger pressing to enter mid-block. The instructor never opens the door to talk: the group freezes on the Day 1 freeze call with hands off tools and nobody crowding the door, the campers stay in the U in the sightline, and the instructor alone speaks through the locked door from the door side of the room while the group keeps working. The instructor asks for the adult's name and the camper's name, checks the pickup-authorization list without leaving the sightline, and holds the answer. A name on the list with matching photo ID is admitted through the door-side check as a late or early pickup, and the form ends there with a timestamped Airtable note. A name not on the list is told through the door that pickup runs only through the camp number and the authorized list, and the door stays locked; the instructor Twilio-messages the custodial parent at once from the door side, the room still in sightline, and the camper does not move toward the door until the parent's answer lands. An adult who will not leave, raises a voice, tries the door handle, or turns threatening moves the case to the lockdown form at once, door already locked, campers to the far wall, silent attendance count, with 911 first and the venue address and street cross from the session contact card. A calm but unauthorized attempt logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, the name given at the door, whether the list matched, the parent contacted and the time of the Twilio message, and the outcome. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because the locked door, the pickup-authorization list, the camp number, the Day 1 freeze, the U, and the Day 1 lockdown rehearsal already exist, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: prose only, no new materials, no timing changes.
  • Loose animal on a field walk: freeze, nobody runs, the group backs away as one. A venue has walls and a locked door and a walk does not, so a loose dog or other animal meeting the group outside keeps its own walk form here; the room form, for an animal that slips through an open door or window while the group works the tables, stands in the bullet directly below. When a loose dog or other animal approaches the group on a walk, the instructor calls "Freeze, buddies" once, the same field-freeze call the illness bullet uses, and the group freezes where it stands: nobody runs, nobody screams, nobody reaches out a hand, nobody makes eye contact with the animal, and nobody splits off. The instructor steps to the front of the group, between the campers and the animal, and speaks calmly without approaching it. If the animal passes or moves off, the group backs away slowly as one, single file, still facing forward, until the animal is well behind, then the instructor counts aloud in buddy pairs and the walk resumes. If the animal blocks the path, follows the group, or acts aggressively, the group retreats as one along the route already walked, back toward the venue or to the mapped hard shelter on the route card, whichever is nearer, and the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form. A bite or scratch that breaks skin is Tier 2 at minimum: first aid from the field bag's first-aid kit, parent pinged via Twilio within 15 minutes, incident report form filled the same hour, and the parent decides on medical follow-up; a bite to the face or neck, a deep bite, or any sign the animal may be ill or a stray with no owner in sight escalates to Tier 3, 911 first with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, so animal control can be dispatched with the medical call. Every animal encounter, even one that ends when the animal moves off, logs to the Airtable incident log on return, with the time, the place on the route, what the animal looked like, and whether anyone was touched. This bullet invents no new stop mid-walk beyond the freeze the illness bullet already uses, and the retreat destination is the venue or the hard shelter already on the route card under the same emergency exception the lockdown bullet already states. No new rehearsal minute is needed: the Day 1 room rehearsal already teaches the freeze call and the aloud count, and this bullet names only the stand-still rules and the slow back-away on a walk, so the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the freeze and the retreat ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Loose animal in the room: nobody runs, the group holds the U, out the door it came through The case this names is the one the walk bullet cannot reach: a loose dog or other animal slipping through an open door or window while twelve campers work the tables, the same open doors the heat-illness bullet keeps available and the same snack food on the tables the sting bullet names. The Day 1 freeze governs: hands off tools, nobody moving toward the animal, nobody crowding the door or windows, and the campers stay in the U in the instructor's sightline. The stand-still rules ride over from the walk bullet: nobody runs, because running is what triggers a chase, nobody screams, nobody reaches out a hand, nobody makes eye contact with the animal. The instructor steps to the front of the group, between the campers and the animal, and speaks calmly without approaching it. The way out is the door the animal came through: the instructor opens it wide and holds it, calm voice, and the animal is let out on its own, never chased, never grabbed, never cornered. An animal that settles and will not leave keeps the room in the hold: the group stays in the U, nobody between the animal and its door, and the instructor waits it out rather than penning it. An animal that bares teeth, lunges, or blocks the only door holds the room the same way with the campers against the far wall, and if it keeps pressing and will not let the room settle, 911 from the instructor-table phone with the venue address and street cross off the session contact card, the group staying put until help arrives. A bite or scratch that breaks skin is Tier 2 at minimum: first aid from the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table, the wound washed with clean water from the camper's own bottle and covered, parent pinged via Twilio within 15 minutes, the incident form filled the same hour, and the parent decides on medical follow-up; a bite to the face or neck, a deep bite, or any sign the animal may be ill or a stray with no owner in sight escalates to Tier 3, 911 first with the venue address and street cross read off the session contact card, so animal control is dispatched with the medical call. Every animal encounter in the room, even one that ends when the animal walks out, logs to the Airtable incident log with the time, the place in the room, what the animal looked like, and whether anyone was touched. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: the camp first-aid kit already sits at the instructor table, water bottles already ride the tables, the Day 1 freeze already teaches the hands-off hold, the U and the far wall already name the group's positions, and the session contact card already carries the venue address and street cross, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: prose only, no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the hold rides the block the instructor already staffs.
  • Bee or wasp sting on a field walk: walk away slow, nobody swats, watch for the airway signs. The room rules name no sting form at all: a sting happens outside, on a walk, and the Tier chain names allergic reaction only in the abstract, without saying what the group does in the first minute. When a bee or wasp stings a camper on a walk, the group does not freeze beside the insect, the opposite of the animal bullet: the instructor calls "Walk away slow, buddies" once, and the group walks away slowly as one, single file, away from the spot, nobody running, nobody swatting, nobody waving arms, because running and swatting provoke more stings and split the group. Once clear, the instructor counts aloud in buddy pairs, then the stung camper sits at the instructor's side while the group stays in sight: the instructor scrapes a bee stinger off sideways with a fingernail, never squeezes it (a wasp leaves no stinger, so there is nothing to scrape), and covers the spot from the field bag's first-aid kit, then keeps the camper at the instructor's side and watches for airway involvement, spreading hives, swelling of the face or lips, vomiting, or faintness for the rest of the walk. A sting without airway involvement is Tier 2: first aid from the field bag's first-aid kit, a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes, and the incident form the same hour, and the walk resumes once the camper feels able, with the buddy inside the sightline; a sting to the face or neck, many stings at once, a camper with a known sting allergy on file, or any airway sign escalates to Tier 3 at once, the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first, then 911 with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, then the parent, then the incident form, and the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form. Every sting, even one that settles with first aid, logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, bee or wasp if known, sting count, and whether epinephrine was given. This bullet invents no new stop mid-walk beyond the slow walk-away itself, and the abort destination is the venue or the hard shelter already on the route card under the same emergency exception the lockdown bullet already states. No new rehearsal minute is needed: the Day 1 room rehearsal already teaches the aloud count and the Tier chain, and this bullet names only the slow walk-away, the no-swat rule, and the airway watch on a walk, so the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, the first-aid kit and the EpiPens already ride in the field bag and the allergy action plans already clip to its outside panel; no timing changes, the walk-away and the watch ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Bee or wasp sting in the room: scrape, cover, watch for the airway signs, the call line. The field bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a bee or wasp can come through a door or window into the venue, and snack time is eaten at the tables, so a sting at a table is a live case, not a walk case, and the field form does not fit a table, because there is no spot to walk away from slowly, no group to move as one, and no route card to read a location off. The near-hits do not fill it: the field sting bullet gives a slow walk-away, a stinger scrape, a cover from the field bag kit, and an airway watch for the rest of the walk, none of which names what happens at a table while eleven other campers keep working; the room allergy bullet names nut-aware snacks and allergy action plans, but gives no first-minute sting form; the room cut bullet presses gauze onto bleeding skin, but a sting with no bleeding needs no pressure and no dressing; and the Tier chain names allergic reaction as a Tier 2 example and airway involvement as Tier 3 without saying what the instructor does in the first minute at a table. When a camper reports a sting in the room, the group freeze governs: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), nobody crowds the camper, nobody swats at the insect, and the instructor checks at the camper table, keeping the room in sightline. If the insect is still in the room, the instructor opens the nearest door or window and lets it leave on its own; nobody chases it, nobody traps it, and the group stays seated until it is gone or the instructor calls the room clear. The instructor scrapes a bee stinger off sideways with a fingernail, never squeezes it (a wasp leaves no stinger, so there is nothing to scrape), and covers the spot from the camp first-aid kit. A sting inside the mouth, from a snack or a drink, skips the scrape and the cover and fires Tier 3 at once, because swelling inside the mouth can block the airway: the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first if the camper has one on file, then 911, then the parent, then the incident form. Otherwise the instructor keeps the camper at the instructor table and watches for airway involvement, spreading hives, swelling of the face or lips, vomiting, or faintness for the rest of the session. A sting without airway involvement is Tier 2: first aid from the camp first-aid kit, a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes, and the incident form the same hour, and the camper returns to table work but not to tool stations that day. A sting to the face or neck, many stings at once, a camper with a known sting allergy on file, or any airway sign escalates to Tier 3 at once, the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first, then 911, then the parent, then the incident form, with the instructor staying with the eleven and the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. Every sting in the room logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, table or station, bee or wasp if known, sting count, whether epinephrine was given, and rest versus pickup. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: a fingernail scrape, a cover from the camp first-aid kit, and the freeze and hands-off-tools stop that the Day 1 Open already teaches use only kit and furniture the page already runs, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the freeze and the watch ride the lesson and the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Asthma attack on a field walk: sit up, inhaler first, the walk waits. The room rules name no asthma form at all: the sick-camper script covers nausea, headache, fever, and vomiting in the room, and the Tier chain names airway involvement only for anaphylaxis, with epinephrine, not an inhaler, so a camper struggling to breathe on a walk has no field form. When a camper reports trouble breathing, wheezing, or a tight chest, the group stops where it stands and the camper sits upright at the instructor's side, never lying flat, while the group stays in sight and nobody leaves to fetch help. The instructor takes that camper's own prescribed rescue inhaler from the field bag, checked against the med/allergy sheet and the camper's written asthma action plan clipped to the bag, and the camper uses it exactly as the action plan directs; inhalers are never shared between campers, and the instructor never gives a dose the action plan does not name. The camper rests at the instructor's side while the group waits, and the walk resumes only when the camper says breathing feels normal again; an attack that eases is Tier 2, with a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour. The form escalates to Tier 3 at once, 911 first with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, then the parent, then the incident form, and the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, when breathing does not ease after the action plan's doses, when the camper cannot speak a full sentence, when lips or face look blue or gray, when no action plan is on file for that camper, or when the attack follows a sting or a food exposure that also shows airway signs, in which case the epinephrine line in Tier 3 runs first. Every attack logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, trigger if known, inhaler doses given per the action plan, and whether 911 was called, and each inhaler use also logs to the Airtable med log. The bullet invents no new stop mid-walk beyond the sit at the instructor's side the heat-illness bullet already uses, and the abort destination is the venue or the hard shelter already on the route card under the same emergency exception the lockdown bullet already states, and states no new rehearsal minute is needed, because the Day 1 room rehearsal already teaches the aloud count and the Tier chain and the bullet names only the sit-upright rule, the own-inhaler rule, and the Tier 3 triggers on a walk, so the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, the rescue inhalers and action plans already ride in the field bag; no timing changes, the sit and the watch ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Asthma attack in the room: sit up, own inhaler, the group stays in the U The field bullet covers an attack on a walk, but the room rules name no asthma form at all: the sick-camper script covers nausea, headache, fever, and vomiting in the room, and the Tier chain names airway involvement only for anaphylaxis, with epinephrine, not an inhaler, so a camper struggling to breathe at the tables has no room form. When a camper reports trouble breathing, wheezing, or a tight chest, the camper sits upright at the instructor's side, never lying flat, while the other eleven stay seated in the U and keep working and nobody leaves the room to fetch help. The instructor takes that camper's own prescribed rescue inhaler from the med lockbox, checked against the med/allergy sheet in the instructor folder and the camper's written asthma action plan on file, and the camper uses it exactly as the action plan directs; inhalers are never shared between campers, and the instructor never gives a dose the action plan does not name. The camper rests at the instructor's side while the group keeps working, and the lesson resumes its normal pace only when the camper says breathing feels normal again; an attack that eases is Tier 2, with a parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour. The form escalates to Tier 3 at once, 911 first, then the parent, then the incident form, when breathing does not ease after the action plan's doses, when the camper cannot speak a full sentence, when lips or face look blue or gray, when no action plan is on file for that camper, or when the attack follows a sting or a food exposure that also shows airway signs, in which case the epinephrine line in Tier 3 runs first. Every attack logs to the Airtable incident log with time, place in the room, trigger if known, inhaler doses given per the action plan, and whether 911 was called, and each inhaler use also logs to the Airtable med log.
  • Seizure on a field walk: clear space, nobody holds the camper down, time it, 911 on the triggers. The room form is the companion bullet below: the Tier chain names head injury and suspected concussion at Tier 3, and the meds bullet rides rescue inhalers and EpiPens in the field bag, but neither says what the group does in the first minute when a camper seizes on a walk, so a seizure on a walk has no field form. When a camper seizes, the instructor calls "Stop, buddies" once, the group stops where it stands and stays in sight, and nobody crowds the camper and nobody leaves to fetch help. The instructor clears space around the camper, cushions the head with a jacket or a bag if one is in reach without moving the camper, and stays at the camper's side timing the seizure on a watch or phone; nobody holds the camper down, nobody restrains arms or legs, nobody puts anything in the camper's mouth, and nobody gives food, water, or medicine while the shaking lasts. If the spot itself is unsafe, a roadway or a slope toward traffic, the instructor moves the camper only as far as safety requires and only if it can be done without restraining the seizure, otherwise the group stays put. When the shaking stops, the camper is rolled onto one side if breathing, kept warm and watched, and not left alone; the instructor counts aloud in buddy pairs and checks that no camper stayed behind. A seizure that ends within five minutes with the camper returning to normal responsiveness, and a written seizure action plan on file for that camper, is Tier 2, with a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, and the walk still aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, because a camper who seized does not continue a walk. The form escalates to Tier 3 at once, 911 first with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, then the parent, then the incident form, when the seizure lasts longer than five minutes, when a second seizure follows without full recovery, when breathing is troubled after the shaking stops, when the camper is hurt during the seizure, when the seizure follows a head injury, or when there is no seizure history and no action plan on file for that camper. No dose is given for a seizure except a rescue medication the camper's written seizure action plan names, given exactly as the plan directs. Every seizure logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, the timed length of the seizure, whether 911 was called, and whether any rescue medication was given per the action plan. The bullet invents no new stop mid-walk beyond the stop the illness bullet already uses, and the abort destination is the venue or the hard shelter already on the route card under the same emergency exception the lockdown bullet already states, and states no new rehearsal minute is needed, because the Day 1 room rehearsal already teaches the aloud count and the Tier chain and the bullet names only the clear-space rule, the do-not-hold rule, the timing rule, and the Tier 3 triggers on a walk, so the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, the first-aid kit already rides in the field bag and the med/allergy sheet and any written seizure action plan already ride with it; no timing changes, the stop and the watch ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Seizure in the room: same rules, at the tables, the group stays in the U. The walk bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a camper can seize at a table during a lesson or the project block just as on a walk, and the Tier chain alone names no seizure response. When a camper seizes in the room, the group freeze governs: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), buddy pairs check each other in, and nobody crowds the camper. The instructor clears space around the camper, pushing chairs and trays back without moving the camper, cushions the head with a jacket or a bag if one is in reach without moving the camper, and stays at the camper's side timing the seizure on a watch or phone; nobody holds the camper down, nobody restrains arms or legs, nobody puts anything in the camper's mouth, and nobody gives food, water, or medicine while the shaking lasts. When the shaking stops, the camper is rolled onto one side if breathing, kept warm and watched, and not left alone; the instructor counts aloud in buddy pairs and checks that no camper left the U. A seizure that ends within five minutes with the camper returning to normal responsiveness, and a written seizure action plan on file for that camper, is Tier 2, with a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour; the camper rests in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for the rest of the session and does not return to tool stations, because a camper who seized does not resume tool work that day. The form escalates to Tier 3 at once, 911 first, then the parent, then the incident form, when the seizure lasts longer than five minutes, when a second seizure follows without full recovery, when breathing is troubled after the shaking stops, when the camper is hurt during the seizure, when the seizure follows a head injury, or when there is no seizure history and no action plan on file for that camper; the instructor stays with the group under the hospital-transport rule, and the paperwork rides. No dose is given for a seizure except a rescue medication the camper's written seizure action plan names, given exactly as the plan directs. Every seizure logs to the Airtable incident log with time, place in the room, the timed length of the seizure, whether 911 was called, and whether any rescue medication was given per the action plan. The bullet invents no new rule beyond the walk form's rules moved indoors: the clear-space rule, the do-not-hold rule, the timing rule, and the Tier 3 triggers are the walk bullet's, the group freeze is the Tier chain's own rule for every incident, and the sick chair is the illness bullet's, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, because the Day 1 Open already teaches the group to stop and stay seated on the instructor's word in the lost-camper freeze, and the Day 2 walk-out count already rehearses the seizure rules themselves; the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged, because the room form adds no new rule, only the room setting of rules already rehearsed. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, the first-aid kit already sits at the instructor table and the med/allergy sheet and any written seizure action plan already ride in the instructor folder; no timing changes, the freeze and the watch ride the lesson and the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Choking in the room: cough first, then back blows and abdominal thrusts, 911 when the airway stays blocked. Until this bullet no choking form existed: the Tier chain names airway involvement only for anaphylaxis, and no room bullet says what the group does when a camper chokes on a snack or a drink at a table. When a camper chokes in the room, the group freeze governs: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), buddy pairs check each other in, and nobody crowds the camper, nobody leaves the U to fetch help, and nobody gives the choking camper food or water. If the camper is coughing strongly and can speak, the instructor stays at the camper's side, encourages the cough, and does not slap the back, does not reach into the mouth, and does not start thrusts while an effective cough is clearing the airway; a cough that clears the object with breathing back to normal is Tier 2, with a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, and the camper rests in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for 20 minutes, then rejoins the group only if breathing stays normal. If the camper cannot cough, cannot speak, or cannot breathe, or a strong cough weakens and turns silent, the form is Tier 3 at once: the instructor calls 911 on speakerphone first, then gives cycles of 5 back blows between the shoulder blades with the heel of the hand, the camper leaning slightly forward, followed by 5 abdominal thrusts, fist above the navel and below the ribs with quick inward and upward thrusts, repeating the cycles with the phone on speaker until the object clears or help arrives; chest thrusts replace abdominal thrusts in the same cycle if abdominal thrusts are not possible. The parent call follows 911 in Tier 3 order but waits until the object clears or EMS is on the line, because the instructor's hands are on the camper, and the incident form follows after. If the camper becomes unconscious, the instructor lowers the camper gently to the floor, keeps 911 on speaker if it is not already called, starts CPR as the instructor's first-aid and CPR certification trains, checks the mouth only if an object is seen, and never does a blind finger sweep; care continues until EMS takes over or breathing resumes, and the instructor stays with the group under the hospital-transport rule, with the paperwork riding, exactly as the seizure bullet states. If the object clears after thrusts but breathing stays troubled, or the choking followed a known allergen and airway swelling follows, the Tier 3 airway line takes over, 911 first, then the parent, then the incident form, with the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form if an allergic airway reaction is the cause. Every choking episode logs to the Airtable incident log with time, place in the room, what was eaten or drunk if known, whether the cough cleared it or thrusts were given and how many cycles, whether 911 was called, and whether EMS transported. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: back blows, abdominal thrusts, and CPR are the instructor's existing first-aid and CPR certification skills, and the campers only need the group freeze and the sit in the U that the Day 1 Open already teaches, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, the first-aid kit already sits at the instructor table; no timing changes, the freeze and the watch ride the lesson and the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Choking on a field walk: cough first, then back blows and abdominal thrusts, 911 with the route card location. Until this bullet the only choking form was the room bullet: no field bullet says what the group does when a camper chokes on a snack or a drink on a walk, away from the tables and the sick chair. When a camper chokes on a walk, the group stops where it stands and stays in sight, buddy pairs check each other in, and nobody runs ahead, nobody runs back to the venue to fetch help, nobody crowds the camper, and nobody gives the choking camper food or water. If the camper is coughing strongly and can speak, the instructor stays at the camper's side, encourages the cough, and does not slap the back, does not reach into the mouth, and does not start thrusts while an effective cough is clearing the airway; a cough that clears the object with breathing back to normal is Tier 2, with a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, and the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, with the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for 20 minutes after return, then rejoining only if breathing stays normal. If the camper cannot cough, cannot speak, or cannot breathe, or a strong cough weakens and turns silent, the form is Tier 3 at once: the instructor calls 911 on speakerphone first, reading the location off the route card printed 911 line, then gives cycles of 5 back blows between the shoulder blades with the heel of the hand, the camper leaning slightly forward, followed by 5 abdominal thrusts, fist above the navel and below the ribs with quick inward and upward thrusts, repeating the cycles with the phone on speaker until the object clears or help arrives; chest thrusts replace abdominal thrusts in the same cycle if abdominal thrusts are not possible. The parent call follows 911 in Tier 3 order but waits until the object clears or EMS is on the line, because the instructor's hands are on the camper, and the incident form follows after. If the camper becomes unconscious, the instructor lowers the camper gently to the ground, keeps 911 on speaker if it is not already called, starts CPR as the instructor's first-aid and CPR certification trains, checks the mouth only if an object is seen, and never does a blind finger sweep; care continues until EMS takes over or breathing resumes, and the instructor stays with the group under the hospital-transport rule, with the paperwork riding, exactly as the room choking bullet states. If the object clears after thrusts but breathing stays troubled, or the choking followed a known allergen and airway swelling follows, the Tier 3 airway line takes over, 911 first with the location off the route card printed 911 line, then the parent, then the incident form, with the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form if an allergic airway reaction is the cause. Every choking episode on a walk logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, what was eaten or drunk if known, whether the cough cleared it or thrusts were given and how many cycles, whether 911 was called, and whether EMS transported. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: back blows, abdominal thrusts, and CPR are the instructor's existing first-aid and CPR certification skills, and the campers only need the stop and the stay-in-sight that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the other field bullets already use, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, the first-aid kit already rides in the field bag; no timing changes, the stop and the abort ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Twisted ankle on a field walk: sit, elevate, the three-step weight test, the hold-or-walk-out call. Until this bullet the only ankle line on the page is the Tier 2 line in Incident handling: first aid from the camp first-aid kit, a parent ping within 15 minutes, and the incident form the same hour. That line names no field form, and the field illness bullet's cannot-walk line does not fill it: that line fires Tier 3 for a camper who cannot stand, faints, or cannot keep water down, while a twisted ankle is a Tier 2 injury in a camper who can sit upright, talk, and drink. So a solo instructor meeting a twisted ankle on a walk has faced two wrong moves: walk the group out together on the lightning abort form with a camper who cannot walk the route, or call 911 for a Tier 2 injury. When a camper reports ankle pain or starts limping, the group stops where it stands on the freeze call, sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, and the injured camper sits at the instructor's side. The shoe stays on, because a shoe removed over a swelling ankle may not go back on for the walk out, and the ankle rests elevated on a pack while the instructor checks the Tier 3 triggers: a foot or ankle that looks deformed, toes that are numb or discolored, pain that stays severe after sitting, or toes the camper cannot move. Any Tier 3 trigger fires the Tier 3 chain at once: 911 first with the location read off the route card, the group holding together at the point off the road, the parent call after 911, the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. Cold is not part of the field care, stated plainly so no instructor goes looking for it: the instant cold packs live in the Day 4 burn kit at the soldering station, not in the field bag, so on the walk the care is rest and elevation, and cold goes on at the venue after return. If no Tier 3 trigger fires, the instructor runs the three-step weight test: the camper stands holding a buddy's shoulder, takes three slow steps on the flat ground beside the group, and reports whether pain stays mild and the ankle bears weight. If the camper passes the test, the group walks out together on the lightning abort form, slowly, the injured camper beside the instructor with the buddy's shoulder for support, stopping whenever pain rises; the instructor sends the Tier 2 parent ping within 15 minutes from the field phone, the incident form follows on return, and the camper rests at the venue until the listed adult arrives. If the camper cannot stand or cannot finish three steps even supported, nobody carries the camper: a solo instructor cannot carry a camper ages 10-14 while keeping eleven other campers in sight, and a buddy carry risks a second fall. Instead the group holds together at the point off the road, the instructor calls the listed adult from the field phone with the route card location and the nearest route access point, and the parent meets the group there; this is the one pickup that happens on the route instead of at the venue, because the camper cannot walk to the venue, and the group walks out together only after the injured camper is with the listed adult. Every ankle episode logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, the weight test result, whether the group walked out or held for pickup, the parent ping time, and any Tier 3 trigger that fired. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: rest, elevation, and a supported three-step walk use the first-aid kit and the buddy pairs the page already runs, and the Day 1 Open already teaches the stop and the stay-in-sight, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the stop and the hold ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Twisted ankle in the room: sit, elevate, the three-step weight test, the stay-or-pickup call. The field bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a camper can twist an ankle at a table just as on a walk, by tripping over a mat edge or a cable, stepping off a mat wrong, or falling from a chair during the project block, and the field form does not fit a table, because there is no group to stop on a freeze call, no road to sit off, and no walk-out abort or route pickup to make. The near-hits do not fill it: the field ankle bullet gives a foot injury a shoe-on rule, elevation on a pack, and a three-step weight test on flat ground beside the group, none of which names what happens at a table while eleven other campers keep working; the room arm bullet gives a still-arm rule and a sick-chair rest, but an ankle cannot be held still against its own side and is never tested by bending it; the room cut bullet presses gauze onto bleeding skin, but a twisted ankle with no bleeding needs no pressure and no dressing; and the Tier chain names twisted ankle as a Tier 2 example without saying what the instructor does in the first minute at a table. When a camper reports ankle pain or starts limping in the room, the group freeze governs: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), nobody crowds the camper, and the instructor checks at the camper spot, keeping the room in sightline, without moving the camper to the sick chair to be checked. The shoe stays on, because a shoe removed over a swelling ankle may not go back on, and the ankle rests elevated on a chair or a pack while the instructor checks the Tier 3 triggers from the field bullet: a foot or ankle that looks deformed, toes that are numb or discolored, pain that stays severe after sitting, or toes the camper cannot move. Any Tier 3 trigger fires the Tier 3 chain at once: 911 first, the parent call after 911, and the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports, with the instructor staying with the eleven and the paperwork riding. Cold is part of the room care, completing the field bullet's own line that cold goes on at the venue after return: an instant cold pack from the Day 4 burn kit beside the soldering station, wrapped in cloth so it never sits bare on the skin, goes on the ankle while it stays elevated, because the kit is in the room. If no Tier 3 trigger fires, the instructor runs the three-step weight test on the flat floor between the tables: the camper stands holding a buddy's shoulder with the instructor at their side, takes three slow steps, and reports whether pain stays mild and the ankle bears weight. If the camper passes the test, the camper moves to the sick chair beside the instructor's table with the buddy's shoulder for support, rests there for the rest of the session, and does not return to tool stations that day; the Twilio parent ping goes within 15 minutes and the incident form is filed the same hour, and the parent decides on medical follow-up or early pickup under the photo-ID door rule. If the camper cannot stand or cannot finish three steps even supported, nobody carries the camper: a solo instructor cannot carry a camper ages 10 to 14 while keeping eleven other campers in sight, and a buddy carry risks a second fall. Instead the camper stays where they are in the instructor's sightline, resting with the ankle elevated, until a listed adult arrives at the venue door under the photo-ID door rule; this is the ordinary venue pickup, not the field bullet's route pickup, because the camper is already at the venue and the group is already seated. Every ankle episode in the room logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, table or station or floor spot, the weight test result, whether cold was applied, any Tier 3 trigger that fired, and whether the camper rested or was picked up. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: a shoe left on, elevation on a chair or a pack, a supported three-step walk, and a cold pack from the burn kit already in the room use only kit and furniture the page already runs, and the campers only need the freeze and the hands-off-tools stop that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the room arm bullet already uses, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the freeze and the test ride the lesson and the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Arm injury on a field walk: hold it still, the Tier triggers decide. Until this bullet no arm form exists anywhere on the page, room or field, even though a fall on a walk can hurt an arm, wrist, or shoulder as easily as an ankle. The near-hits do not fill it: the twisted ankle bullet gives a foot injury a shoe-on rule, elevation on a pack, and a three-step weight test, none of which fits an arm, since an arm cannot bear a weight test and elevation on a pack does not hold a shoulder still; the head strike bullet keeps a neck still and watches for signs, but says nothing about a painful arm with normal alertness; the cut bullet presses gauze onto bleeding skin, but an arm injury with no bleeding needs no pressure and no dressing; and the Tier chain names burn, deeper cut, twisted ankle, and allergic reaction as Tier 2 examples, but names no arm line at all. When a camper reports arm, wrist, or shoulder pain after a trip or fall, or holds an arm still and will not use it, the group stops where it stands on the freeze call, sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, and the injured camper sits at the instructor's side. The arm stays exactly as found: nobody straightens it, nobody tests the joint by bending it, nobody lifts it to check it, and the camper holds it still against their own side with the uninjured hand, elbow bent, while the instructor checks the Tier 3 triggers: an arm, wrist, or shoulder that looks deformed, fingers that are numb, tingling, pale, blue, or cold, fingers the camper cannot move, severe pain that stays severe after sitting, or bone visible through skin. Any Tier 3 trigger fires the Tier 3 chain at once: 911 first with the location read off the route card, the group holding together at the point off the road, the parent call after 911, the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. If no Tier 3 trigger fires and the pain eases with the arm held still, the injury rides Tier 2: the group walks out together on the lightning abort form, slowly, the injured camper beside the instructor with the arm still held against their side, stopping whenever pain rises; the instructor sends the Tier 2 parent ping within 15 minutes from the field phone, the incident form follows on return, and the camper rests at the venue until the listed adult arrives. If pain rises on the walk out so the camper cannot keep walking, nobody carries the camper: the group holds together at the point off the road, the instructor calls the listed adult from the field phone with the route card location and the nearest route access point, and the parent meets the group there, the same one route pickup the ankle bullet allows, because a camper in rising pain cannot walk to the venue. A cut over the injured arm does not change the arm form: the cut bullet's press and cover runs on the bleeding only, with the arm still held still throughout, and any Tier 3 trigger from either bullet fires the Tier 3 chain. Every arm episode logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, arm, wrist, or shoulder, any Tier 3 trigger that fired, whether the group walked out or held for pickup, and the parent ping time. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: a still arm held by its own owner uses no kit item and no new form, and the Day 1 Open already teaches the stop and the stay-in-sight, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the stop and the hold ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Arm injury in the room: hold it still, at the tables, the group keeps working. The field bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a camper can hurt an arm, wrist, or shoulder at a table just as on a walk, by falling from a chair, tripping over a mat edge or a cable, or taking a test-rig weight on a hand during the project block, and the field form does not fit a table, because there is no group to stop on a freeze call, no road to sit off, and no walk-out abort or route pickup to make. When a camper reports arm, wrist, or shoulder pain after a fall at a table, or holds an arm still and will not use it, the group freeze governs: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), nobody crowds the camper, and the camper stays seated at the table while the instructor checks at that table, keeping the room in sightline. The arm stays exactly as found: nobody straightens it, nobody tests the joint by bending it, nobody lifts it to check it, and the camper holds it still against their own side with the uninjured hand, elbow bent, while the instructor checks the Tier 3 triggers: an arm, wrist, or shoulder that looks deformed, fingers that are numb, tingling, pale, blue, or cold, fingers the camper cannot move, severe pain that stays severe after sitting, or bone visible through skin. Any Tier 3 trigger fires the Tier 3 chain at once: 911 first, the parent call after 911, and the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports, with the instructor staying with the eleven and the paperwork riding. If no Tier 3 trigger fires, the case is Tier 2: a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, with the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for the rest of the session and not returning to tool stations that day, and the parent deciding on medical follow-up or early pickup under the photo-ID door rule. A cut over the injured arm does not change the arm form: the room cut bullet's press and cover runs on the bleeding only, with the arm still held still throughout, and any Tier 3 trigger from either bullet fires the Tier 3 chain. Every arm injury in the room logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, table or station, arm, wrist, or shoulder, any Tier 3 trigger that fired, and whether the camper rested or was picked up. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: a still arm held by its own owner uses no kit item, on the field bullet's form, and the campers only need the freeze and the hands-off-tools stop that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the room seizure bullet already uses, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the freeze and the watch ride the lesson and the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Nosebleed on a field walk: lean forward, pinch soft, the clock decides. Until this bullet no nosebleed line exists anywhere on the page, room or field, even though a nosebleed is one of the commonest minor field events for ages 10 to 14. The group stops on the freeze call and sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, the bleeding camper at the instructor's side, sitting upright and leaning slightly forward so blood runs out, never back down the throat, breathing through the mouth. The instructor hands the camper gauze or tissues from the field bag's first-aid kit and the camper pinches the soft part of the nose below the bridge, firm and steady, for 10 minutes by the clock, without peeking early to check, because peeking restarts the clot. The head never tilts back, the nose is never blown hard, and nothing is packed up the nose. If the bleeding stops, the camper rests at the instructor's side for 10 minutes more, sips water, does not blow or pick the nose, and the group walks on together slowly; a restart means another full 10 minutes of pressure before the group moves. Tier 2 fires when bleeding continues past 20 minutes of correct pressure, when it stops and restarts twice, or when the flow is heavy enough to soak gauze fast: the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, the Twilio parent ping goes within 15 minutes, and the incident form is filed the same hour, with the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline after return. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first with the location off the route card printed 911 line, when a nosebleed follows a blow to the head or face, when the camper is dizzy, pale, or weak, when breathing is troubled, or when blood runs down the throat and the camper vomits; the parent call follows 911 and the hospital-transport rule applies if EMS transports. Every nosebleed on a walk logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, minutes of pressure, whether it stopped, restarted, or escalated, and any Tier trigger fired. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: gauze and tissues already ride in the field bag's first-aid kit, and the campers only need the stop and the stay-in-sight that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the other field bullets already use, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the stop and the abort ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Nosebleed in the room: same clock, at the tables, the group keeps working. The walk bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a nosebleed at a table during the project block is just as common for ages 10 to 14 as one on a walk, and the field form does not fit a table, because there is no group to stop on a freeze call, no road to sit off, and no slow walk-on to make. When a camper gets a nosebleed at a table, the camper stays seated at that table, leaning slightly forward so blood runs out, never back down the throat, breathing through the mouth, and the group keeps working; nobody crowds the camper and no second camper touches blood or bloodied materials. If the camper was at a tool station, tools go down first on the room splinter bullet's form (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats) before pressure starts. The instructor brings gauze from the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table to the camper's table, keeping the room in sightline the whole way, and the camper pinches the soft part of the nose below the bridge, firm and steady, for 10 minutes by the clock, without peeking early to check, because peeking restarts the clot. The head never tilts back, the nose is never blown hard, and nothing is packed up the nose. If the bleeding stops, the camper rests seated at the table for 10 minutes more, sips water from the camper's own bottle, does not blow or pick the nose, and then returns to table work; a restart means another full 10 minutes of pressure before table work resumes. After any stop, the instructor cleans the table on the sick kit's two-minute spill form (gloves on, absorbent pads down, soiled gauze bagged in a zip bag, disinfectant wipes across the surface) during a natural pause, and the camper washes hands before touching shared materials again. Tier 2 fires when bleeding continues past 20 minutes of correct pressure, when it stops and restarts twice, or when the flow is heavy enough to soak gauze fast: a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, with the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for the rest of the session and not returning to tool stations that day, and the parent deciding on medical follow-up or early pickup under the photo-ID door rule. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first, when a nosebleed follows a blow to the head or face, when the camper is dizzy, pale, or weak, when breathing is troubled, or when blood runs down the throat and the camper vomits; the parent call follows 911 and the hospital-transport rule applies if EMS transports. Every nosebleed in the room logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, table or station, minutes of pressure, whether it stopped, restarted, or escalated, and any Tier trigger fired. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: gauze already sits in the camp first-aid kit and gloves, pads, zip bags, and wipes already sit in the sick kit, and the camper only needs the pinch the instructor names in the moment, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes.
  • Splinter on a field walk: wash it, one steady pull or cover it, never dig. Until this bullet no splinter line exists anywhere on the page, room or field, even though a splinter from a bench, a fence rail, or a fallen branch is one of the commonest minor field injuries for ages 10 to 14. The group stops on the freeze call and sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, the injured camper at the instructor's side, hands still, nothing squeezed and nothing picked at while the instructor looks. The instructor washes the spot with the camper's water bottle and pats it dry with gauze from the field bag's first-aid kit, then looks for the end: if the splinter end sticks out above the skin, the camper may pull it out in one steady motion along the angle it went in, clean hands, no squeezing around it, and the instructor never digs for a buried end, never uses a needle, a craft knife, or a pin, and never lets a second camper try. If it comes out whole, the spot is washed again, dried with gauze, covered with a bandaid from the kit, and the group walks on together slowly; the camper tells the instructor at once if pain, redness, or swelling grows, and the parent hears about it at pickup with a watch-for-48-hours note for redness, swelling, warmth, red streaks, or fever. If the splinter does not come out in that one steady pull, or if it is deep, broken off under the skin, under a fingernail, in the face or eye, dirty or rusty, or one of several, the instructor stops trying, covers it with gauze and a bandaid without pressing it deeper, and the case is Tier 2: the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, the Twilio parent ping goes within 15 minutes, and the incident form is filed the same hour, with the parent deciding on medical follow-up and the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline after return. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first with the location off the route card printed 911 line, if a splinter is in the eye, or if bleeding from the spot will not stop under gauze pressure; the parent call follows 911 and the hospital-transport rule applies if EMS transports. Every splinter on a walk logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, site on the body, whether it came out whole or was covered, and any Tier trigger fired. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: the water bottle, gauze, and bandaid already ride the walk, and the campers only need the stop and the stay-in-sight that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the other field bullets already use, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the stop and the abort ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Splinter in the room: same rules, at the tables, tools down first. The walk bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a camper can pick up a splinter from a wood offcut, a cardboard edge, or a rough 3D print edge during the project block just as on a walk, and the Tier chain alone names no splinter response. When a camper gets a splinter at a table, the group freeze governs: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), buddy pairs check each other in, and nobody crowds the camper, and treatment happens at the instructor table inside the room, where the camp first-aid kit already sits, so line of sight never breaks. The camper holds the hand still, nothing squeezed and nothing picked at while the instructor looks. The instructor washes the spot with clean water and pats it dry with gauze from the camp first-aid kit, then looks for the end: if the splinter end sticks out above the skin, the camper may pull it out in one steady motion along the angle it went in, clean hands, no squeezing around it, and the instructor never digs for a buried end, never uses a needle, a craft knife, or a pin, and never lets a second camper try. If it comes out whole, the spot is washed again, dried with gauze, covered with a bandaid from the kit, and the camper returns to table work; the camper tells the instructor at once if pain, redness, or swelling grows, and the parent hears about it at pickup with a watch-for-48-hours note for redness, swelling, warmth, red streaks, or fever. If the splinter does not come out in that one steady pull, or if it is deep, broken off under the skin, under a fingernail, in the face or eye, dirty or rusty, or one of several, the instructor stops trying, covers it with gauze and a bandaid without pressing it deeper, and the case is Tier 2: a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, with the parent deciding on medical follow-up and the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for the rest of the session and not returning to tool stations that day. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first, then the parent, then the incident form, if a splinter is in the eye, or if bleeding from the spot will not stop under gauze pressure; the instructor stays with the group under the hospital-transport rule, and the paperwork rides. Every splinter in the room logs to the Airtable incident log with time, table or station, site on the body, whether it came out whole or was covered, and any Tier trigger fired. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: water, gauze, and a bandaid already sit in the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table, and the campers only need the freeze and the hands-off-tools stop that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the room seizure bullet already uses, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the freeze and the watch ride the lesson and the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Blister on a field walk: cover it, never pop it, the walk test decides. Until this bullet the only blister line on the page is the Tier 1 line in Incident handling: treated on the spot, logged with a timestamp. That line names no field form, and the field illness bullet's cannot-walk line does not fill it: that line fires Tier 3 for a camper who cannot stand, faints, or cannot keep water down, while a blister is a Tier 1 injury in a camper who can sit upright, talk, and drink, and the twisted-ankle bullet's shoe-stays-on rule does not fill it either, because a blister cannot be covered with the shoe fully on. So a solo instructor meeting a blister on a walk has faced two wrong moves: walking the group on with a camper in growing pain at every step, or aborting the walk for a bandaid-level injury. When a camper reports a hot spot or a blister, the group stops on the freeze call and sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, the camper at the instructor's side. The camper loosens the laces and slips the shoe back just enough for the instructor to see the spot, then re-laces loosely once it is covered; the shoe stays on for the walk out, because a barefoot walk or a shoe carried in hand risks a second injury. The instructor never pops, drains, or peels a blister, and never lets a camper do it: popping opens the skin to infection and turns a Tier 1 blister into a wound. If the blister is intact, the instructor covers it with a bandaid from the field bag's first-aid kit; no ointment, moleskin, or blister pad is claimed, because none of them is itemized in the field bag manifest, so the care is cover and rest, not a padded dressing the kit does not carry. The camper sits for five minutes, then takes a short test walk beside the instructor, a few slow steps on flat ground with the buddy pair in sight. If the pain stays mild and the camper walks comfortably, the group walks on together slowly, stopping whenever pain rises, and the parent hears about it at pickup with a watch-for-48-hours note for redness, swelling, warmth, pus, or fever. If the blister is already open or torn, the instructor washes it gently with the camper's water bottle, pats it dry with gauze from the kit, covers it with gauze and a bandaid without pressing the torn skin flat, and the case is Tier 2: the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, the Twilio parent ping goes within 15 minutes, and the incident form is filed the same hour, with the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline after return. If the camper cannot walk comfortably even after covering and rest, nobody carries the camper: a solo instructor cannot carry a camper ages 10 to 14 while keeping eleven other campers in sight, and a buddy carry risks a second fall. Instead the group holds together at the point off the road, the instructor calls the listed adult from the field phone with the route card location and the nearest route access point, and the parent meets the group there; this is the same route pickup the ankle bullet names, because the camper cannot walk to the venue, and the group walks out together only after the camper is with the listed adult, with the same Tier 2 ping within 15 minutes and incident form the same hour.
