Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first with the location read off the route card, only when the foot injury is not a blister at all: a foot or ankle that looks deformed, toes that are numb or discolored, pain that stays severe after sitting, or toes the camper cannot move, each taken from the ankle bullet's trigger list, with the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. Every blister episode logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, intact or open, walked on or held for pickup, the parent ping time if Tier 2 fired, and any Tier 3 trigger that fired. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because the water bottle, gauze, and bandaid already ride the walk, and the Day 1 Open already teaches the stop and the stay-in-sight, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes:
  • Blister in the room: cover it, never pop it, the stay-or-pickup call. Until this bullet the blister line lived only on the walk and in the Tier chain: the field blister bullet runs cover, a five-minute sit, and a test walk with a walk abort, none of which fits a camper already seated at the tables while the other eleven keep working. The Tier chain's Tier 1 line (paper cut, blister: treated on the spot, logged with a timestamp) names no room form, the twisted-ankle room bullet's three-step weight test does not fill it because a blister is tested by no weight test and cannot be covered with the shoe fully on, and the splinter room bullet's pull-or-cover form does not fill it because a blister is never pulled out. So a solo instructor meeting a blister during the project block has faced two wrong moves: freezing the group for a freeze call written for a road shoulder, or reaching for a wound script when the skin is only rubbed raw. A hot spot or blister presents at the tables, at snack time, or after the walk returns: campers cross cable runs, shift between stations, and the walk form's covered spots can still ache when the shoe comes back off. When a camper reports a hot spot or a blister, the group freeze governs: the other eleven stay in the U with hands off tools (the iron in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), nobody crowds the camper, and the check happens at the camper's own table inside the sightline. The camper loosens the laces and slips the shoe back just enough for the instructor to see the spot, then re-laces loosely once it is covered; the shoe stays on for the rest of the day, because a barefoot walk around the tables risks a second injury. The instructor never pops, drains, or peels a blister, and never lets a camper do it: popping opens the skin to infection and turns a Tier 1 blister into a wound. If the blister is intact, the instructor covers it with a bandaid from the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table; no ointment, moleskin, or blister pad is claimed, because none of them is itemized in the room kit manifest, so the care is cover and rest, not a padded dressing the kit does not carry. The camper returns to table work but not to a standing station that day, tells the instructor at once if pain, redness, or swelling grows, and the parent gets a watch-for-48-hours note at pickup for redness, swelling, warmth, pus, or fever. If the blister is already open or torn, the instructor washes it gently with water from the camper's own bottle, pats it dry with gauze from the kit, covers it with gauze and a bandaid without pressing the torn skin flat, and the case is Tier 2: the sick-chair rule runs for the rest of the session, no tool stations that day, a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes, the incident form the same hour, and the parent deciding on pickup under the photo-ID door rule. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first with the venue address and street cross read off the session contact card, only when the foot injury is not a blister at all: a foot or ankle that looks deformed, toes that are numb or discolored, pain that stays severe after sitting, or toes the camper cannot move, each taken from the ankle bullet's trigger list, with the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. Every blister episode logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, table or station, intact or open, covered and returned or sick chair, the parent ping time if Tier 2 fired, and any Tier 3 trigger that fired. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because water, gauze, and bandaids already sit in the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table, and the campers only need the freeze and the hands-off-tools stop that the Day 1 Open already teaches, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: prose only, no new materials, no timing changes.
    • prose only, no new materials, no timing changes.
  • Head strike on a field walk: stop, keep the neck still, watch, 911 on the signs. Until this bullet the Tier chain names head injury and suspected concussion at Tier 3, but no field form says what the group does in the first minute when a camper falls or is struck on the head on a walk. The seizure bullet names a head injury only as one of its Tier 3 triggers, and the twisted-ankle bullet's sit-and-test form does not fill it, because a head strike is never tested by walking on it. So a solo instructor meeting a head strike on a walk has faced two wrong moves: walking the group on with a camper who looks fine and then deteriorates away from help, or calling 911 with no group hold and no watch form while eleven campers stand unwatched. When a camper takes a blow to the head, from a fall, a thrown or falling object, or a collision with another camper, the instructor calls "Stop, buddies" once, the group stops and sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, and nobody crowds the camper and nobody leaves to fetch help. The camper stays where they are: the instructor does not sit the camper up, does not walk the camper to test them, and does not move the camper except out of immediate danger, a roadway or a slope toward traffic, the same exception the seizure bullet states. If the camper reports neck pain, cannot move the neck, or was knocked out, the head and neck stay still: no pillow repositioning, no sitting up to check alertness, and the head is cushioned with a jacket or a bag only if one is in reach without moving the camper, the same cushion rule the seizure bullet states. If the scalp bleeds, the instructor presses gauze from the field bag's first-aid kit gently over the cut, but stops pressing and covers loosely if a dent or a soft spot is felt under the skin, because pressing into a skull injury makes it worse. A camper who says they feel fine still does not walk on: watch signs can arrive after the strike, so every head strike aborts the walk on the lightning bullet's abort form, and the camper does not pick up a tool or rejoin the project block that day. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first with the location read off the route card printed 911 line, then the parent, then the incident form, when any watch sign appears: loss of consciousness even briefly, vomiting, confusion, slurred speech, unusual drowsiness or trouble staying awake, a headache that worsens instead of easing, a seizure, clear fluid from the nose or the ear, neck pain, a fall from above head height, or a strike the camper cannot describe, with the instructor staying with the group under the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports. A strike with no watch sign is not treated as a suspected concussion on the evidence the instructor has, so it rides Tier 2 after the abort, while any sign at any point moves the case to Tier 3 at once: the Twilio parent ping goes within 15 minutes, the incident form is filed the same hour, the parent decides on medical follow-up, and the camper rests in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline after return. Every head strike logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, what struck the head and from what height if known, consciousness at the first check, each watch sign seen or none, the 911 call time if Tier 3 fired, the parent ping time, and whether EMS transported. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill, because the water bottle, gauze, and bandaid already ride the walk, and the Day 1 Open already teaches the stop and the stay-in-sight, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the stop and the abort ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Head strike in the room: stay where they are, keep the neck still, watch, 911 on the signs. The walk bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a camper can strike their head at a table just as on a walk, by falling from a chair, tripping over a mat edge or a cable and hitting a table edge, or taking a falling object from a shelf, and the field form does not fit a table, because there is no group to stop on a freeze call, no road to sit off, and no walk-out abort or route card to read a 911 location from. The near-hits do not fill it: the seizure bullet cushions a head without moving the camper, but only during a seizure, and says nothing about a head strike with the camper awake and talking; the room cut bullet presses gauze onto bleeding skin, but says a cut on the head follows the head strike bullet first without giving the room version of that bullet, so a scalp cut in the room pointed at a field form; the room nosebleed bullet fires Tier 3 on a nosebleed after a blow to the head or face, but names no watch-sign list and no stay-still rule for the strike itself; and the Tier chain names head injury and suspected concussion at Tier 3 without saying what the instructor does in the first minute at a table. When a camper takes a blow to the head in the room, the group freeze governs: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), buddy pairs check each other in, and nobody crowds the camper. The camper stays where they are: the instructor does not sit the camper up, does not walk the camper to test them, and does not move the camper except out of immediate danger, a toppling shelf or a hot station, the same exception the field bullet states for a roadway or a slope toward traffic. If the camper reports neck pain, cannot move the neck, or was knocked out, the head and neck stay still: no pillow repositioning, no sitting up to check alertness, and the head is cushioned with a jacket or a bag only if one is in reach without moving the camper, the same cushion rule the seizure bullet states. If the scalp bleeds, the instructor presses gauze from the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table gently over the cut, but stops pressing and covers loosely if a dent or a soft spot is felt under the skin, because pressing into a skull injury makes it worse; the room cut bullet's head-cut carve-out now points here in the room, just as the field cut bullet's points to the field head bullet. A camper who says they feel fine still does not return to tool stations: watch signs can arrive after the strike, so the camper does not pick up a tool or rejoin the project block that day, and the sick chair in the instructor's sightline is where the camper rests for the rest of the session. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first, then the parent, then the incident form, when any watch sign appears: loss of consciousness even briefly, vomiting, confusion, slurred speech, unusual drowsiness or trouble staying awake, a headache that worsens instead of easing, a seizure, clear fluid from the nose or the ear, neck pain, a fall from above head height, or a strike the camper cannot describe, with the instructor staying with the group under the hospital-transport rule if EMS transports, and the paperwork riding. A seizure after a head strike fires the seizure bullet's Tier 3 trigger at once, 911 first, on top of this bullet's watch list. A strike with no watch sign is not treated as a suspected concussion on the evidence the instructor has, so it rides Tier 2, while any sign at any point moves the case to Tier 3 at once: the Twilio parent ping goes within 15 minutes, the incident form is filed the same hour, the parent decides on medical follow-up or early pickup under the photo-ID door rule, and the camper rests in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for the rest of the session. Every head strike in the room logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, table or station, what struck the head and from what height if known, consciousness at the first check, each watch sign seen or none, the 911 call time if Tier 3 fired, the parent ping time, and whether EMS transported. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: gauze already sits in the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table and a jacket or bag is already in the room, and the campers only need the freeze and the hands-off-tools stop that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the room seizure bullet already uses, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the freeze and the watch ride the lesson and the project block the instructor already staffs.
  • Cut on a field walk: press, cover, the bleed decides. Until this bullet no cut form exists anywhere on the page, room or field, even though a cut from a fall, a sharp rock, or a snagged branch is one of the commonest minor field injuries for ages 10 to 14. The Tier chain names a paper cut at Tier 1 and a deeper cut at Tier 2, but it never says what the group does in the first minute, how long pressure lasts, or what turns a cut into a 911 call. The splinter bullet fires Tier 3 only when bleeding from a splinter spot will not stop under gauze pressure, which assumes a cut form without giving one, and the nosebleed bullet gives a pressure clock for a nose only. A cut on the head does not ride this bullet: it follows the head strike bullet first, neck still, loose cover if a dent or a soft spot is felt, because pressing a scalp cut can hide a skull injury. When a camper is cut on a walk, the group stops on the freeze call and sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline, the cut camper at the instructor's side, sitting, with the cut limb held still and, if the camper can do it without pain, raised slightly above the level of the heart. The instructor rinses visible dirt off the cut briefly with clean water from the walk water bottle, then presses clean gauze from the field bag's first-aid kit firmly over the cut for 10 minutes by the clock, without peeking early to check, because peeking restarts the bleed. If the gauze soaks through, the instructor adds more gauze on top and keeps pressing, never removing the first layer. Nothing is packed into the wound, no ointment is claimed, and an object stuck in the wound is never pulled out: the instructor pads gauze around it, holds it still, and the case rides Tier 3 below. If the bleeding stops within the 10 minutes and the cut is shallow with edges that sit together, the camper can move the limb normally, and there is no numbness, the case is Tier 1: a bandaid over the gauze, the camper walks on with the group slowly, and the instructor watches the dressing at the next stop. Tier 2 fires when the bleeding needs a second full 10 minutes of pressure to stop, when the cut edges gape open even though the bleeding is controlled, or when dirt or gravel cannot be rinsed out: the walk aborts on the lightning bullet's abort form, the Twilio parent ping goes within 15 minutes, the incident form is filed the same hour, and the camper rests in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline after return, with the parent deciding on medical follow-up. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first with the location off the route card printed 911 line, when blood spurts or soaks gauze fast despite steady pressure, when bleeding has not stopped after 20 minutes of correct pressure, when the camper reports numbness or cannot move the fingers or toes past the cut, when an object is stuck in the wound, or when the cut is on the face or over a joint with bleeding that will not stop; the parent call follows 911 and the hospital-transport rule applies if EMS transports. A camper who feels faint or dizzy with a cut does not ride the sit-and-watch path: the field illness bullet's cannot-stand line fires Tier 3 at once, 911 first, with the group holding together. Every cut on a walk logs to the Airtable incident log on return with time, place on the route, body part, minutes of pressure, whether it stopped, needed a second round, or escalated, any Tier trigger fired, the parent ping time if Tier 2 fired, and whether EMS transported. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: gauze, bandaids, and the water bottle already ride the walk, and the campers only need the stop and the stay-in-sight that the Day 1 Open already teaches and the other field bullets already use, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the stop and the abort ride the walk the instructor already staffs.
  • Cut in the room: same clock, at the tables, tools down first. The walk bullet above names the field form, but until this bullet no room form existed: a camper can be cut at the cutting station or with table scissors during the project block just as on a walk, and the Tier chain names only a paper cut at Tier 1 and a deeper cut at Tier 2 without saying what the room does in the first minute, how long pressure lasts, or what turns a table cut into a 911 call. When a camper is cut in the room, tools go down first across the room on the room splinter bullet's form (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats, table scissors down), the other campers stay seated at their tables, nobody crowds the cut camper, and the cutting station stays closed for the rest of the block, because the instructor cannot both treat and supervise it. The cut camper stays seated at the table, with the cut limb held still and, if the camper can do it without pain, raised slightly above the level of the heart. The instructor brings gauze from the camp first-aid kit at the instructor table to the camper's table, keeping the room in sightline the whole way, rinses visible dirt off the cut briefly with clean water from the camper's own bottle, then presses clean gauze firmly over the cut for 10 minutes by the clock, without peeking early to check, because peeking restarts the bleed. If the gauze soaks through, the instructor adds more gauze on top and keeps pressing, never removing the first layer. Nothing is packed into the wound, no ointment is claimed, and an object stuck in the wound is never pulled out: the instructor pads gauze around it, holds it still, and the case rides Tier 3 below. A cut on the head does not ride this bullet: it follows the head strike bullet first, neck still, loose cover if a dent or a soft spot is felt, because pressing a scalp cut can hide a skull injury. If the bleeding stops within the 10 minutes and the cut is shallow with edges that sit together, the camper can move the limb normally, and there is no numbness, the case is Tier 1: a bandaid over the gauze, the camper returns to table work but not to the cutting station that day, and the parent gets a watch-for-48-hours note at pickup for redness, swelling, warmth, red streaks, or fever. Tier 2 fires when the bleeding needs a second full 10 minutes of pressure to stop, when the cut edges gape open even though the bleeding is controlled, or when dirt or gravel cannot be rinsed out: a Twilio parent ping within 15 minutes and the incident form the same hour, with the camper resting in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for the rest of the session and not returning to tool stations that day, and the parent deciding on medical follow-up or early pickup under the photo-ID door rule. Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first, when blood spurts or soaks gauze fast despite steady pressure, when bleeding has not stopped after 20 minutes of correct pressure, when the camper reports numbness or cannot move the fingers or toes past the cut, when an object is stuck in the wound, or when the cut is on the face or over a joint with bleeding that will not stop; the parent call follows 911 and the hospital-transport rule applies if EMS transports. A camper who feels faint or dizzy with a cut does not ride the sit-and-watch path: Tier 3 fires at once, 911 first, with the group holding in the room. After any stop, the instructor cleans the table on the sick kit's two-minute spill form (gloves on, absorbent pads down, soiled gauze bagged in a zip bag, disinfectant wipes across the surface) during a natural pause, and the camper washes hands before touching shared materials again. Every cut in the room logs to the Airtable incident log the same hour with time, table or station, body part, minutes of pressure, whether it stopped, needed a second round, or escalated, any Tier trigger fired, the parent ping time if Tier 2 fired, and whether EMS transported. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: gauze and bandaids already sit in the camp first-aid kit and gloves, pads, zip bags, and wipes already sit in the sick kit, and the campers only need the hands-off-tools stop the Day 1 Open already teaches, so no new rehearsal minute is needed, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes.
  • Restroom at the venue: one-at-a-time, the door stays in sightThe restroom procedure is venue-scoped: one camper at a time, in the single-use restroom beside the main room whose door is visible from the instructor's seat (the §07 venue spec item). The instructor never leaves the room and line of sight never breaks: the camper calls the run out loud, the other eleven stay in the room with the instructor, and tool stations go hands-off while a camper is out of the room, hands back on when they return. No pairs, no lines at the door, no hallway trips: the rule holds through the 60-minute lesson and the 45-minute project block alike. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints.
  • Restroom on a field walk: the walk aborts, the venue restroom waitsThe restroom procedure above is venue-scoped (the single-use restroom beside the main room, one at a time, door visible from the instructor's seat), and the field-missions bullet's mapped routes carry no public restrooms and no entering buildings, so a camper needing a restroom on a walk has one honest answer under the one-adult model: the walk comes home. Prevention rides the walk-out count: the instructor names the restroom gate in the route brief every walk-out morning ("restroom now, because the walk has no restroom"); campers who need it go one at a time at the venue while the rest of the group stays seated in the U hearing the route brief, and the instructor never leaves the room, so the single-use door stays visible from the instructor's seat exactly as the venue procedure requires. If a camper needs the restroom mid-walk, the camper says so and the walk aborts early on the same form the lightning bullet uses: aloud count, the group walks straight back to the venue together along the mapped route, no splits, no buddy pair peeling off to find a restroom, no ducking into a shop. On return the camper uses the venue restroom while the group does the walk-back count; the mission continues on the indoor alternate or the remaining walk time becomes self-directed project work on the day's handout, which the route brief already names. The no-shame rule is stated matter-of-factly at every walk-out, so asking is the expected action and no camper is ever asked to wait it out. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the gate rides the two minutes the walk-out count already takes.
  • Behavior and conflict, one adult, no side roomTwelve campers ages 10 to 14 and one adult, so disputes are handled in the open, never in a side room: there is no office, no pull-aside, no camper leaving the room with the instructor alone, and the line-of-sight model holds during a dispute exactly as during an incident. Day 1's 0:00-0:10 open sets the room contract in its three-minute camp-rules segment: the instructor reads three rules aloud, campers repeat each back, and the rules go on the truth-rubric poster board for all ten days: (1) voices down and tools down when a person is speaking; (2) hands off other campers' work; (3) when stuck, ask the handout before you ask the room. A dispute is settled at the table, in the open, with the instructor stating facts only ("the two sets of parts go back to their owners' tables now"), no feelings, no verdict on who started it. A camper who cannot keep participating takes the reset chair beside the instructor's table, inside the room and in line of sight, with a blank journal page and five minutes; the group keeps working. If the camper is still out after five minutes, the parent gets a Twilio heads-up that hour, and the camper's buddy keeps the buddy station warm. Physical aggression (a shove, a strike, a thrown object) is a behavioral Tier 2: the group freezes as in Incident handling, tools down, the instructor counts twelve, the parent is called for same-hour pickup, and the camper does not return until the instructor and the parent agree the room contract holds again. A second same-session incident ends the session for that camper with a pro-rated refund under the published refund policy. Every behavioral incident is logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, the same as any Tier 1 or above.
  • Distressed camper in the room: the reset chair, a different script. The behavior bullet above names the reset chair for a camper who cannot keep participating after a dispute, and the illness bullet names the sick chair for nausea, headache, fever, or vomiting, but until this bullet no distress form existed: a camper who arrives tearful, becomes overwhelmed mid-project, is homesick, or asks to go home is neither disputing nor ill, and the dispute script (facts only, no feelings, no verdict on who started it) is the wrong tool for tears, while the sick chair script (temperature, 24-hour return rule) does not fit a camper with no symptoms. When a camper is tearful, overwhelmed, or asks to go home, the instructor invites the camper to the same reset chair beside the instructor table, inside the room and in line of sight, with a blank journal page, while the group keeps working; the instructor does not pull the camper aside, does not leave the room, and does not touch the camper. At the chair the instructor speaks low, at the instructor table, so the reason is not announced to the room, says "Take your reset, I will check back at the boundary", and offers three choices in one sentence: write on the journal page, drink from the camper's own water bottle, or sit quietly until the next minute-block boundary. The instructor does not ask the camper to explain in front of the room, does not promise a call home, and does not diagnose a panic attack or any other condition. The five-minute clock from the behavior bullet runs: if the camper is calmer and ready to rejoin within five minutes, the camper rejoins the buddy station and the episode logs as a one-line note in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp. If the camper is still distressed after five minutes, the instructor sends the same Twilio heads-up that hour that the behavior bullet sends, and the parent decides: the camper stays in the reset chair with the journal page until the parent collects under the early pickup rule (photo-ID matched at the door, instructor staying in the room) or the parent asks the instructor to keep the camper in the chair for the rest of the block. If distress arrives with breathing trouble, wheeze, chest tightness, faintness, or any airway sign, this bullet does not govern: the asthma, allergy, or Tier chain forms govern, and the instructor does not coach breathing until an airway cause is ruled out by those forms. If the camper heads for the door, this bullet stops and the lost-camper form governs. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the reset chair, the journal page, and the Twilio chain already exist, the five minutes ride the behavior bullet's clock, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No new rehearsal minute is needed, because Day 1 already teaches the reset chair location and the group-keeps-working form, and this bullet only gives that chair a distress script.
  • Distressed camper on a field walk: walk at the instructor's side, the group stops, it never splits. The room bullet above gives a tearful, overwhelmed, or homesick camper the reset chair beside the instructor table, but a field walk has no chair, no table, and no room to wait in: until this bullet no walk form existed for distress, and the walk forms that do exist do not fit it. The heat bullet stops the group for a sick camper and the field illness bullet aborts the walk for a camper who cannot continue, but neither names a low-voice script, a three-choice offer, or a five-minute rejoin branch for a camper who is not ill and not disputing, and the behavior bullet's reset chair cannot be carried onto a walk. When a camper becomes tearful, overwhelmed, or asks to go home on a walk, the group stops where it stands and stays as one: nobody walks ahead, nobody is sent back alone, and the camper walks at the instructor's side, in line of sight, never pulled aside and never touched. The instructor speaks low, at the camper's side, so the reason is not announced to the group, says "Take your reset, I will check back at the next stop", and offers three choices in one sentence: walk quietly beside the instructor, drink from the camper's own water bottle, or hold the route card while the group is stopped. The instructor does not ask the camper to explain in front of the group, does not promise a call home, and does not diagnose a panic attack or any other condition. The five-minute clock from the behavior bullet runs: if the camper is calmer and ready to rejoin within five minutes, the camper rejoins the buddy pair, the walk resumes, and the episode logs as a one-line note in the Airtable incident log on return, the same return logging every field incident uses. If the camper is still distressed after five minutes, the instructor sends the same Twilio heads-up that hour that the behavior bullet sends, and the parent decides: the walk aborts and the group returns to the venue as one for door pickup under the early pickup rule, with the instructor staying with the group to the door, or the parent asks the instructor to keep the camper walking at the instructor's side for the rest of the walk. If distress arrives with breathing trouble, wheeze, chest tightness, faintness, or any airway sign, this bullet does not govern: the asthma, allergy, or Tier chain forms govern, and the instructor does not coach breathing until an airway cause is ruled out by those forms. If the camper leaves the group or heads off the route, this bullet stops and the field lost-camper form governs. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints, no timing changes, the route card, the water bottle, and the Twilio chain already exist, the five minutes ride the behavior bullet's clock, and the parent walkthrough night promise naming three items is unchanged. No new rehearsal minute is needed, because the Day 2 walk-out count already teaches the group-stops-and-counts form, and this bullet only gives that stop a distress script.
  • Instructor sick day: one adult, zero backupThe staffing model has exactly one background-checked adult: no assistant, no CIT, no substitute roster. A day without the instructor is a day the camp cannot run, because the room, the field, and the project block all require the instructor's presence every minute. The instructor does not teach sick. If the instructor wakes up too ill to hold the line-of-sight count (fever, vomiting, anything that degrades supervision), the day cancels: a Twilio cancellation goes to every parent by 8 AM, the same 8 AM call the weather pivot uses; the same 8 AM moment pulls that day's date off the Studio flow's date schedule in the Twilio console, a one-minute edit from the instructor's phone with no venue needed, so a parent who misses the cancellation text and calls between 9:15 and 11:15 lands on the camp's voicemail greeting instead of ringing the sick instructor's phone, and the removed date stays off the schedule so the ring rule always matches the days the session actually runs; the same 8 AM Twilio console moment also cancels that day's pre-scheduled crisis broadcast, 11:16 that day or 11:46 on the Day 10 showcase, about one more minute with the console already open, because the 11:15 pickup-row cancel never runs on a day with no pickup: without this step the dead-man's switch would fire a false "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held at the venue, pickup instructions to follow" text at 11:16 on top of the 8 AM cancellation, the exact contradictory panic the broadcast exists to prevent. This broadcast cancel runs only on the 8 AM pre-lesson path: a mid-lesson incapacitation leaves the broadcast armed, because that is the day it is needed and the instructor never reaches the console mid-crisis; the enrollment confirmation states this policy so no parent is surprised. The cancelled day refunds pro-rated through Stripe at one-tenth of tuition ($42.50 per seat, processed within 48 hours) on the tuition collection and refunds API the launch plan already carries; the session resumes the next scheduled day with no make-up added. Two consecutive cancelled days, or three in one session, trigger the hard-cancel terms: a full refund of the remaining days via Stripe and the wind-down checklist, the same gate the launch plan uses at T-4. The wind-down checklist is seven steps, run by the instructor from the phone except where a step names the founder, in this order: (1) in the Twilio console, cancel every remaining pre-scheduled crisis broadcast for the session's future teaching dates, 11:16 each day or 11:46 on the Day 10 showcase, because the 11:15 pickup-row cancel and the 8 AM sick-day cancel only ever touch the current day, and without this step the dead-man's switch would fire a false "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held at the venue, pickup instructions to follow" text at 11:16 on a date the session no longer runs; (2) in the same console moment, pull every remaining teaching date off the Studio flow's date schedule, so an in-hours parent call lands on the camp's Twilio voicemail instead of ringing the instructor's phone; (3) send the hard-cancel notice to every session parent over the camp number and process the remaining-day full refunds via Stripe within 48 hours; (4) notify the venue in writing that the remaining dates release, so the hold and the deposit terms close out cleanly; (5) mark the session cancelled in Airtable, so no waitlist ping, roster export, or Session Zero print batch treats it as live; (6) in the same Twilio console moment as steps 1 and 2, edit the Say widget's TTS text in the Studio flow to the cancelled-session line, the same mechanism the T-6w build uses for the hours line (one line of text in the console, never a recording), because step 2 routes post-cancel parent calls to that greeting and the live greeting still says "camp hours are 9:15 to 11:15, leave a message, read first thing each teaching morning," which is false after a hard-cancel: the cancelled line reads, "You have reached the camp number. This session is cancelled; there are no further teaching mornings. The hard-cancel notice sent over this number carries the details and the refund timing."; (7) once steps 1 through 6 are confirmed done, the founder revokes the instructor's restricted Twilio user from the owner login, so no provisioned login survives a cancelled session: the T-4 hard gate and the binding re-score gate fire after the T-10w hire pinned the provisioning to the background-check-clear day, and the sick-day and mid-lesson hard-cancels fire after the instructor has been running console moments on that login all session, so every path that runs this checklist has a live instructor login that no future step will use again; the revocation is founder labor on the owner login, never instructor labor, because a login cannot retire itself mid-checklist and the incapacitation path runs this checklist when the instructor may be unable; when the founder teaches, no instructor user was ever provisioned, so step 7 is a one-line check that no restricted user exists, not a revocation; and when this checklist runs, the session never reaches the venue section's move-out, so the move-out revocation never fires and the two revocations cannot double-revoke. A hard-cancel triggered off a mid-lesson incapacitation runs the same checklist as soon as the instructor is able, the same scoping the date-schedule pull-off already carries for that path. If the instructor never becomes able (hospitalized, unconscious, unreachable), the founder runs steps 1 through 6 from the owner login: if the instructor has not confirmed by 4 PM that teaching day that they can run the checklist, the founder assumes they cannot and runs it that evening, unless the founder holds a logged false-fire account for that day's 11:16 broadcast under the Day 3 false-fired stand-down rule, in which case the run is stood down and the founder sends the parent correction text instead; the run happens on the owner login the founder holds, because the dead-man's broadcast fired to the founder's cell at 11:16 and every step is founder-reachable, steps 1, 2, and 6 in the Twilio console, step 3's Stripe refunds and step 5's Airtable cancellation on the founder's operator accounts, step 4's venue release as the founder's written notice; step 7 is unchanged, already founder labor on the owner login once steps 1 through 6 are confirmed done. Behind the founder's 4 PM run sits the one deeper trigger, for the evening the founder themself cannot be reached: if the 11:16 broadcast fired and by 9 PM that teaching day neither the instructor nor the founder has confirmed that the checklist ran or will run that evening, and two contact attempts to the founder, a call and a text, both fail, the designated break-glass adult opens the sealed envelope and runs steps 1 through 7 from the owner login that evening, the same owner-login labor the founder would have done: steps 1, 2, and 6 in the Twilio console, step 3's refunds and step 5's Airtable cancellation on the envelope's Stripe and Airtable logins, step 4's venue release sent as the camp's written notice under the venue agreement's break-glass cancellation-notice clause, invoked through the keyholder roster authority, addressed to the roster's re-confirmed contact, or to all three roster lines, the last-known contact, the signing counterparty, and the agreement's legal-notice address, when the roster marks the T-2w re-confirm unconfirmed, the notice sent by email under the clause's email sufficiency, carrying the clause's sender-identity pin, so the venue verifies the sender by the quoted roster name rather than the personal sending domain, and the clause's subject-line convention, so the release names the clause and the remaining session dates in the subject line rather than sitting under a generic subject, with the remaining dates read off the printed session calendar sealed in the envelope, the keyholder circling the dates printed after the broadcast night, copying them onto the subject-line template's blank date line, and sending the release with the template's clause-named subject line verbatim, and the notice body quoted off the envelope's clause-quote card and roster card, the keyholder copying the boxed operative passage out of the card's isolated box and the roster entry into the email body verbatim, so the convention is executed by copying from the box, not by calendar arithmetic, memory typing, fragment-picking, or starting from an adjacent unmarked line at 9 PM, and effective upon sending by the clause's timing so an unreachable venue cannot later claim it was late, and step 7's revocation as owner-login labor, because the envelope holds the owner login itself. The envelope is never opened on a day the broadcast did not fire, and never before the 9 PM check, so a reachable founder or an able instructor never loses the evening to a false break-glass. A logged false-fire account for that day's 11:16 broadcast under the Day 3 false-fired stand-down rule counts as the founder's confirmation for the 9 PM check, so the envelope is never opened on a stood-down false fire either. In a founder-operated session the founder is the incapacitated instructor, so the founder's 4 PM run cannot happen; the same 9 PM break-glass trigger applies instead, and the keyholder runs the checklist that evening from the envelope, with the login rotation riding the next console moment the founder can reach. The parent-facing pieces are covered in the meantime, because the 11:16 broadcast told every parent the instructor is incapacitated and 911 was called, and the hard-cancel notice rides the keyholder's step 3 run that evening. If the instructor falls ill mid-lesson, the rehearsed incapacitation script runs (the Day 1 walk) and the day counts as cancelled for refund purposes. Nobody can reach the Twilio console mid-crisis, so the date stays on the Studio date schedule, and the script's Do Not Disturb step is the mid-session analogue of the 8 AM pull-off: it keeps any parent call from ringing the incapacitated instructor's phone while the oldest camper runs the 911 call from it, every incoming call rolling to the camp's voicemail greeting, and the Do Not Disturb turns off when the 911 call ends, because that is when the dispatcher's callback may ring through; the date comes off the Studio date schedule as soon as the instructor is able, which is when the cancelled-day refund formalizes. No budget-line changes: this is a contingency, not a line item; the Stripe refund machinery already exists in the launch plan.
  • Tech outage: no Wi-Fi, no power, dead printerEvery lesson leans on the venue: Wi-Fi (the model tabs, the Day 2 shared album, the Airtable med, incident, and attendance logs, the QR check-in at the door table), power (the 8 cart laptops, the venue printer, the 3D printers), the venue printer (the Day 2 contact-sheets, the Day 5 photo pages, the Wednesday restock reprints), and the room screen (the fail-of-the-day beats, the teardown video). The page named no procedure for any of them dying mid-day, a real gap under the one-adult model: the instructor cannot spend twenty minutes on a router while twelve campers wait. The rule is five minutes: the instructor may reboot, rejoin, or re-plug, from inside the room only, line of sight never broken. After five minutes, or immediately when the power goes (there is nothing to troubleshoot), the day flips to its paper version and the lost segment is not chased. Every dependent segment has a named analog cut-over, all on paper the page already prints: the room-screen beats run from the AI Fail binders at each team table; the model-tab grades run from the answer keys in the instructor folder, which every handout already promises; the shared-album upload runs from the cameras' playback screens with the paper tally sheets carrying the record; the contact-sheets are a convenience, not the lesson, so the labeling runs on the tally sheets when the printer dies. The Airtable logs and the QR check-in degrade to the paper backstop filed in the instructor folder at Session Zero Hour 4 beside the answer keys: the printed pickup-authorization list, a one-page paper attendance sheet, and a one-page paper med/incident log sheet, two new pages on the Hour 1 print run, folded into the $90 snacks-and-prints operating line. Outage entries are written there during the day and transcribed to Airtable the same evening, never left unlogged. The 8 AM Twilio calls (the weather pivot, the sick-day cancellation) run on the instructor's cellular phone, not venue Wi-Fi, so an internet outage never blocks the 8 AM call. No budget-line changes: this is procedure, plus two sheets of paper.
  • Power outage in the room: freeze, the hot iron safe, the group stays seated, the paper day The case this names is the one the tech-outage bullet cannot reach: venue power dropping in the middle of the project block, lights out or flickering, the room screen dead, the cart laptops running on battery, and possibly a hot iron sitting at the instructor-run soldering station. The tech bullet names the paper cut-over for the rest of the day; this bullet names the first minute, because a solo instructor cannot flip to paper while twelve startled campers move around cable runs in the dark. The Day 1 freeze governs at once: the instructor calls freeze, hands off tools across the room, nobody leaves a table, nobody stands up to see what happened. The instructor steps to the instructor-run soldering station first: the iron goes into its stand, the station is switched off, and only the instructor touches it; the station stays cold until the power is back, no exceptions, because a hot iron no one can see is the worst thing in a dark room. The group stays seated at the tables in the U in the instructor's sightline: nobody crosses the room in the dark, nobody steps into the hallway to look, nobody crowds the soldering station or the charging tray. Where the venue allows, the instructor opens the door for daylight, from the door side, without leaving the sightline. The room holds the settle minute: nobody moves until the instructor calls the move, the freeze keeping the cables, the trays, and the open tool bins where they are. Once the group is settled, this bullet hands the rest of the block to the tech-outage bullet: the day flips to its paper version, the lost segment is not chased, and the paper backstop carries the logs. An injury in the transition, a trip or a fall against a table edge, runs through the existing injury forms with 911 on the existing triggers, no new trigger invented here; ending the session early runs the existing early-dismissal rule with its existing parent-ping path. Every power outage in the room, even one that flickers back after two minutes, logs to the Airtable incident log with the time the power went, how long it stayed out, whether anyone was hurt in the transition, and whether the paper cut-over ran. The bullet invents no new tool and no new camper skill: the Day 1 freeze already teaches the hands-off hold, the U already names the group's positions, the soldering station and its stand already sit at the instructor table, and the tech-outage bullet already carries the paper cut-over, so no new rehearsal minute is needed and the parent walkthrough night promise, which names the room script, the incapacitation variant, and the field lost-camper variant, is unchanged. No budget-line changes: prose only, no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the settle minute rides the block the instructor already staffs.
  • Qualifications and checksThe instructor is background-checked (Checkr) and first-aid/CPR certified before any session is announced, AB 506 trained (California's youth-service training), and insured under the camp's $1,685/session insurance, licensing, and first-aid row (the $850 GL share, the $600 rider share, the $200 permit share, and the $35 first-aid share) with workers' comp bound when the instructor is hired W-2. The instructor also serves as the on-site Health Supervisor the county organized-camp permit requires: first-aid/CPR certified, holder of the med lockbox and the medication cooler-bag combination, owner of the med log and the incident log. One adult, two named responsibilities. The incident log, the med log, and the attendance record are camp property, exported to the parent at session end, and kept on file for three years. If the background check is not clean by T-10, the session postpones: that is kill gate 3 in the launch plan, and it is not negotiable.

The pilot: where this paper plan meets real kids.

No website can score what only real campers can prove. So the plan names the proof it will collect before any paid session runs: one pilot day, one parent night, measured results, and a published re-score.

  • Pilot day: Saturday, April 17, 2027Eight volunteer campers, four ages 10 to 12 and four ages 13 to 14, recruited through the school scholarship partners: in early March the founder emails the partners the donor-funded scholarship bench already flows through, asking each partner to nominate volunteer campers, two ages 10 to 12 and two ages 13 to 14, and to forward the Tuesday walkthrough-night invite to their parents; the eight confirmed campers, four per age band, are named to the pilot roster by April 9, the founder prints one roster sheet and files it in the instructor folder that day so it rides to the pilot day with the consent forms, and if nominations fall short the founder backfills from the T-18w paid-enrollment funnel's interested families with the same consent paperwork. The parent consent and pickup-authorization forms are signed and collected at the Tuesday walkthrough night, when parents review the pickup-authorization list in person, and filed in the instructor folder that night, so no camper arrives Saturday without paperwork on file. The four-year spread is deliberate: the 13-14s are the only ones who can answer whether the senior extensions are hard enough, and a pilot with no teenagers in it cannot test them. The day does not try to fit two full sessions into one morning; it tests each risk once, compressed: 8:00-8:30 setup of the pre-kitted pilot paper plus check-in, each arriving camper checked against the filed pilot roster, the roster carrying an attendance line the instructor initials as each camper arrives, an arrival time the instructor writes beside any camper who arrives after the 8:30 check-in close, and a departure time the instructor writes beside any camper who leaves before the 12:20 dismissal, so the pilot's attendance record is paper by 8:30 and the 12:20 dismissal counts against the same roster, and each block's whole-block-present set reads off the roster rather than memory. If the roster sheet is lost or damaged before check-in, the instructor rebuilds it at setup from the consent and pickup-authorization forms filed in the same instructor folder, which name the eight confirmed campers, initials arrivals on the rebuilt sheet, and files the rebuilt sheet in the instructor folder, so check-in, the 12:20 dismissal count, and each block's whole-block-present set proceed on the rebuilt sheet with no attendance data lost, and the rebuilt sheet rides into the pilot evidence file in the roster's place. If the roster sheet is lost or damaged after check-in, the instructor rebuilds it at the next block boundary from the same consent and pickup-authorization forms, which name the eight confirmed campers but carry no initials and no times, re-initials only the campers present at the rebuild, writes no arrival time and no departure time the surviving paper does not show, and files the rebuilt sheet in the instructor folder, so the 12:20 dismissal counts against the rebuilt sheet and each later block's whole-block-present set reads off it; no morning initial, arrival time, or departure time is reconstructed from memory, and at the May 12 evening a camper whose rebuilt entry is incomplete for a block before the loss leaves no box and no row for that block under the incomplete-entry rule, a block whose entries are all incomplete failing both its rate lines outright like an empty block. If the pilot day cannot run, because the instructor is ill, the venue is closed, or field-mission weather makes the outdoor block unsafe, the pilot moves to the backup date, Saturday, April 24, 2027, with the same times, the same roster, and the same paper, and the founder holds that backup date when booking the pilot room; the founder messages the eight pilot families on the camp number by 8:00 AM on the cancelled morning, before the 8:30 check-in close, so no family travels to a cancelled pilot. A camper arriving after the 8:30 check-in close waits at the door with their parent, never alone, and the parent messages the camp number; the instructor opens the door from inside at the next pilot block boundary (9:15, 10:00, 10:10, 10:55, or 11:40), checks the camper against the filed pilot roster at the door, writes the door-arrival time from the parent's camp-number message beside the attendance line, hands the camper their block paper, seats the camper with their team, and re-runs the count aloud; a camper arriving during the 11:40-12:20 interviews waits with their parent in the gallery corner until their interview slot, and an interview slot already run is not re-run, so a senior whose slot already ran leaves no exit-interview sheet. Mounting the two blank poster boards from the April copy-shop run, one on the Day 1 wall and one on the Day 8 wall, with painter's tape from the startup shop stock (the Day 1 wall board is the surface the reveal's mini truth-rubric writes on, the board the schedule names as the truth-rubric poster board), 8:30-9:15 the Day 1 blind-taste equivalent (45 minutes: 10-minute open with one AI Fail, 25-minute gauntlet with teams of up to four formed from the campers present and one tray of 20 cards per team present, no team of one: a leftover single camper joins the smallest team, so a short pilot day runs the gauntlet with the teams in the room, 10-minute reveal and mini truth-rubric on the truth-rubric poster board; laminated checklists issued at the close), 9:15-10:00 the "Failure Autopsy" project block at full 45 minutes, 10:00-10:10 reset, taping the morning pieces present to the Day 8 wall board, one piece per volunteer present for the morning block, at most eight, so the panel runs off the pieces on the board and a short pilot day tapes fewer than eight, 10:10-10:55 the Day 8 taste-panel equivalent (45 minutes: 5-minute brief, panel of the morning pieces present at 2.5 minutes each with the guest judge watching, 5-minute judge's gambit, each team present drafts its gate during the QC-gate draft segment, 5-minute stress test of the drafts at 2.5 minutes each, at most two, with any drafts beyond two read by the instructor during the 10:55 project block; the panel runs 20 minutes with eight pieces present and the QC-gate draft takes the panel's freed minutes on a short day and the stress test's freed minutes when fewer than two drafts exist (10 minutes at full attendance), so the block still totals 45 minutes), 10:55-11:40 the "QC Gate" project block at full 45 minutes, and 11:40-12:20 the five-minute exit interviews, one for each camper interviewed, the interviews running back to back from 11:40, each camper with their own parent, in the room, the other families in the gallery corner in the instructor's sightline, and after the last interview the interviewed campers waiting with their parents in the gallery corner in the instructor's sightline until the 12:20 dismissal, so a short pilot day with fewer interviews still ends at 12:20, the freed minutes being supervised waiting with no early departure, all campers still present counted until the last authorized-adult dismissal at 12:20. The block's answers are not memory-held: each interview runs off a printed exit-interview sheet from the April copy-shop run, the two standard questions (what confused you, where did you get stuck) on every sheet and the senior-only question (were the hard versions hard enough, and where did they break) as a third line on the four 13-14s' sheets, the instructor writing the answers down during the five minutes with the camper's parent beside them. Each interviewed camper's sheet goes into the instructor folder at the 12:20 dismissal and rides into the pilot evidence file, so the May 12 binding re-score evening reads the exit-interview answers off paper, and the senior-hardness bar counts off the four senior sheets: three of four sheets carrying a hard verdict for the extensions as a set, a split answer or a sheet with no verdict on hardness not counting toward the three. At dismissal the instructor pulls the morning pieces present off the Day 8 wall board and hands each volunteer present their own piece, then takes both wall boards down and recycles them (April session paper, no camper names on the boards; the mini truth-rubric carries no names), so no camp artifact stays on the rented room's walls. The lesson equivalents are compressed so both lessons and both blocks fit one Saturday; the project blocks run at full length because the block is what the panel is scoring. The Day 8 equivalent's guest judge is one pre-briefed volunteer from the school scholarship partner's faculty, briefed by email the week before, in the room only for the 10:10-10:55 panel block; no fee. If the volunteer cancels, the panel runs anyway from the one-page Day 8 judge brief: the guest is an extra scorer, never a required second adult. The judge is thanked at the room door and shown out at 10:55, the instructor staying in the room with the campers present, because the safety plan's line-of-sight rule has no exceptions. The pilot instructor is the same person who will teach the summer sessions. The pilot has a cost the page never owned until now: it needs a room, Saturday 8:00 to 12:20 (the schedule above, 4.33 hours), plus the Tuesday parent-walkthrough 45 minutes, all at the session's $75/hr venue rate (about 5.1 hours, about $381); its own April paper: 16 handouts (eight "Failure Autopsy," eight "The QC Gate"), the taste-lesson paper (2 artifact trays of 20 cards (2 of the 3 trays bought once on the $931 shop-tools startup line as reusable shop stock, not paid from the pilot's $435 all-in total), 8 answer cards, 8 laminated Producer's Checklists), 2 1-inch AI Fail binders (the pilot's 2, reusable shop stock, bought once on the $931 shop-tools startup line alongside the third binder the June session's third team table needs, not paid from the pilot's $435 all-in total; the 24 blunder sheets print in the April run from the pilot-prep-week selection file, see Phase 6, and slide in for the pilot, refreshed again in June), 8 panel scorecards, 8 QC-gate page templates, and 2 blank poster boards for the Day 1 and Day 8 walls, plus 8 camp journals, one per volunteer, all from an April copy-shop run of about 120 sheets (the eight exit-interview sheets, the two pilot observation-and-timing sheets, and the one incident-and-near-miss log sheet among them) plus the boards plus the journals, markers from shop stock (the June Session Zero print run is two months after the pilot and cannot print them), paid from the pilot's $54 materials slice (the $30 paper plus the $24 journals), inside the $435 all-in total itemized at the end of this paragraph. The journals are not decoration: the pilot runs the Failure Autopsy block at full 45 minutes, and that handout's needs list starts with the Day 1 journal page; the autopsy table, the three binder adds, and the trade-journals stuck protocol all assume it, and the pilot has no session stock to draw on, so eight notebooks join the April run (about $24). The Day 1 one-take teardown video is recorded before the pilot too, not only before the June session: the pilot's 9:15 block needs the same 0:00-0:10 room-screen teardown, and the same file serves June. Materials are the startup equipment the pilot's two lessons exercise, ordered under the Phase 5 T-8 ordering deadline, not re-bought. The pilot's instructor hours are pre-session labor (founder time or the candidate's paid working interview inside the three Gusto months), outside the session's $2,000 pay line. That prep week also carries the one-time digital authoring the June session reuses, about seven and a half hours across thirty-six tasks: the Gauntlet deck (45 minutes), the ten answer keys (60), the logging program (25), the teardown video (15), the wiring diagram (20), nine paper layouts at 15 each, the credit-roll template (15), the blunder selection (30), the ten late-batch pieces (90), and the fail-of-the-day slide (15). The same prep week writes the instructor medical card the incapacitation scripts name: the founder asks the instructor, the same person who will teach the summer sessions, for the four fields, conditions, allergies, current meds, and emergency contact, and writes them on one card cut from the April paper run, face down from then on, so the card exists before the Tuesday parent walkthrough night that walks the script naming it; when the founder teaches, the founder writes their own card. About $435 all in: about $381 of venue time (5.1 hours at $75), about $30 of April paper (120 copy-shop sheets, 2 poster boards, self-seal pouches for the 8 laminated checklists), and about $24 for the 8 pilot journals; the 2 pilot binders are reusable shop stock on the $931 shop-tools startup line, not pilot consumables, so the $435 subtotal stands as itemized. The pilot's actual books are the paper behind that subtotal: the instructor files every paid receipt behind the $435, the venue invoice for the 5.1 hours, the April copy-shop receipt for the about-$30 paper run, and the 8-journals receipt, into the instructor folder at the pilot's 12:20 dismissal beside the exit-interview sheets, so the receipts ride into the pilot evidence file with them and the budget realist's May-12 re-score reads the pilot's actual books off paper, not memory. A paid receipt lost or damaged before the May 12 evening leaves no readable paper for that cost, so the May 12 budget realist re-score reads unknown on the pilot books, named as unknown beside the re-score in the May 12 post, not counted as a pass. Any one lost receipt leaves the books unknown rather than partly verified, because the $435 total is verified receipt by receipt, and the evening does not reconstruct the missing cost from memory or from the $435 total, since a reconstructed cost would not be pilot-day evidence and the total minus a missing receipt proves nothing about the missing receipt. It lives in the launch ledger, not the operating budget: the pilot is pre-launch validation, not session delivery. The pilot day gets its own dead-man's switch, because the ten fixed-send broadcasts the T-6w build arms are all June dates and the camp number is only provisioned at T-10w: during the pilot's prep week the founder arms one pre-written "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held" broadcast on the owner login, to the eight pilot families, the founder's cell, and the designated break-glass adult's cell, with a fixed send at 12:26 on Sat Apr 17, a plain scheduled send, not a Studio-routed one, because the Studio date schedule the T-6w build enters holds only the ten June teaching dates. The instructor opens the Twilio console on the instructor laptop at the 12:20 count-down dismissal and cancels the broadcast in that console moment, about one extra minute, on the restricted instructor login the T-10w row provisioned, or on the owner login when the founder teaches. A pilot day that runs normally sends nothing; the broadcast fires only on a pilot day the instructor never reaches the console, exactly the day it is needed, the same 11:16 logic the June session runs on its ten dates. No budget-line changes: the camp number's monthly rent rides the existing $5/session Twilio parent-comms line, and the pilot's extra weeks are pre-launch validation spend like the rest of the pilot's $435.
  • What gets measuredFive numbers: handout completion rate inside each 45-minute block, stuck-protocol trigger count per handout, time-on-task against each block's 45-minute plan, segment-by-segment timing of both lesson equivalents against their condensed run-of-shows, and the instructor's incident and near-miss log. The log is not memory-held: the April copy-shop run prints one pilot incident-and-near-miss log sheet, it rides a clipboard at the instructor table through the pilot day beside the observation-and-timing sheets, and the instructor writes each incident or near-miss on it in the pause when the response is complete, never mid-teaching, tagging as a ratio breach any span campers are present with no instructor in the room in line of sight, or any headcount over twelve campers per instructor, and tagging as a tool incident any tool-related injury or damage event written as an incident, a near-miss written as a near-miss and never tagged as an incident, so the bar's zero line reads off tagged entries; the sheet is filed in the instructor folder at the 12:20 dismissal so it rides into the pilot evidence file with the exit-interview sheets, and the May 12 evening puts it on the table with the other measured numbers. The first four numbers are not memory-held either: the April copy-shop run prints two pilot observation-and-timing sheets, one per lesson equivalent, and each sheet rides a clipboard at the instructor table during its lesson. The instructor ticks each segment's actual start and end time as the lesson turns (one glance at the sheet at each boundary, the same motion that moves the lesson on), tallies every stuck-protocol trigger in the sheet's tally row: at each boundary glance, one tick per camper who used a handout Stuck box since the last boundary, marked with that handout's short name, and the tick is circled when the camper still needs instructor help after the Stuck-box step, so a circled tick is an escalation and an uncircled tick is a self-resolved trigger, checks one completion box per camper present for the whole block at each block's close, eight boxes when all eight campers are whole-block present, a camper absent from the block or present for only part of it leaving no box, so a short block is judged on its whole-block-present boxes only (a whole-block-present camper whose block work is visibly unfinished leaves the box unchecked), and, for time-on-task, keeps one row per camper present for the whole block on the sheet: during the block's silent supervision, which is not teaching, the instructor notes each whole-block-present camper's settle-to-work time and last-pen-down time, giving one per-camper duration per whole-block-present camper, eight durations when all eight campers are whole-block present, a part-block camper leaving no row and no truncated duration for that block, a whole-block-present camper who never settles to work likewise leaving no row and no duration, a camper still working at the block close having last-pen-down at the close, so the duration runs settle-to-close, a block with no whole-block-present campers leaving no boxes and no rows at all, and a block whose whole-block-present campers all never settle leaving no rows at all, so the May 12 evening reads no median for such a block and fails its median line outright rather than averaging an empty set, with the empty block or the all-no-settle count reported beside the bar, the roster's arrival initials, arrival times for late arrivals, and departure times showing which campers were whole-block present, a camper whose roster entry for a block is incomplete, an arrival initial missing, a late arrival noted without an arrival time, or an early departure noted without a departure time, leaving no box and no row for that block, so the sheet shows only the campers with complete roster entries, a complete entry meaning an arrival initial present, an arrival time recorded for any camper arriving after the 8:30 check-in close, and either no early departure or a departure time recorded, and the May 12 evening judges the block on those campers only, the unrecorded count reported beside the bar, and a camper whose recorded arrival time falls after a block starts and before it ends leaves no box and no row for that block as a part-block camper, while a camper arriving exactly at a block start counts whole-block present for that block; the May 12 evening takes the median over the campers with durations only, the middle ordered duration when the present count is odd and the average of the two middle ordered durations when it is even, matching the bar, and the success bar's 35 minute line reads that median. The lesson rows are filled only at natural transition pauses, never mid-teaching; the block rows are filled during the block's silent supervision, which is not teaching; and both sheets go into the instructor folder at the 12:20 dismissal and ride into the pilot evidence file with the exit-interview sheets, so the May 12 evening reads all five numbers off paper. An observation-and-timing sheet lost or damaged before the May 12 evening leaves no readable numbers for its lesson equivalent, so the evening fails that equivalent's shell line and its blocks' completion and median lines outright rather than passing any of them by vacuity, fails the pilot-day stuck total outright since the total needs both sheets, reports the loss beside the bar rather than judging it as a pass, does not reconstruct ticked times, tallies, boxes, or durations from memory, since reconstructed numbers would not be pilot-day evidence, and does not copy the surviving sheet's numbers onto the lost equivalent. A completed senior sheet lost or damaged after the dismissal leaves no readable verdict on paper, so the May 12 evening counts that senior the same way as an absent senior, not toward the three, the denominator staying four, and the evening does not re-ask the senior weeks later, since a re-asked answer would not be pilot-day evidence. Every camper also does a five-minute exit interview: what confused you, where did you get stuck. The four 13-14s answer a senior-only question as well: whether the hard versions (the senior extensions in each handout) were actually hard enough, and where they broke. No opinion questions about how fun it was; the pilot tests teachability, not entertainment.
