  • Parent walkthrough nightThe Tuesday before the pilot, 6:30 to 7:15 PM, parents walk the full safety plan above with the instructor, ask questions, and review the pickup-authorization list in person. Any plan change a parent asks for gets a written answer within 48 hours. The night runs on a written 45-minute run-of-show so one instructor can run it without improvising. 0:00-0:10 (6:30-6:40): sign in at the door table, and each family initials the pickup-authorization list beside their child's name, confirming the authorized pickup persons and the photo-ID match rule. 0:10-0:30 (6:40-7:00): the instructor walks the safety plan end to end, one-adult supervision through the lesson and the project block, tool gating (soldering, cutting, 3D printers), field-mission buddy rules, meds and incident handling, then reads the three camper scripts aloud, the room script, the field variant, and the incapacitation variant, so every parent knows exactly what their child has been told to do. 0:30-0:40 (7:00-7:10): questions, with the instructor writing every requested plan change on the visible list so nothing is promised aloud and forgotten. 0:40-0:45 (7:10-7:15): close, with the 48-hour written-answer promise restated in front of the room, and the visible requested-changes list filed in the instructor folder before the instructor leaves, so the capture the May 12 evening re-scores against has a named home. The founder writes and sends the 48-hour answers, every requested change answered in writing by Thursday night, and files a copy in the instructor folder beside the list: the founder owns the plan, and pre-session plan labor is founder labor, outside the instructor's paid session hours. At the pilot's 12:20 dismissal the list and the answers ride into the pilot evidence file with the exit-interview sheets, so the safety officer's May 12 evidence reads off paper, not memory. A requested-changes list or 48-hour answers lost or damaged before the May 12 evening leaves no readable paper for that piece, so the evening reads the walkthrough evidence unknown, named as unknown in the May 12 post beside the safety officer re-score, not counted as a pass and not failing the binding verdict, whose bar is the five cards the pilot answers, and the evening does not reconstruct requested changes or answers from memory, nor treat the surviving piece as covering the lost piece, since answers without the list cannot be checked for completeness and a list without the answers leaves every request unanswered on paper. The night's 45 minutes are the Tuesday 45 minutes already billed at the session's $75/hr venue rate inside the pilot's about-$381 room cost: no new room time, no new paper, no new role. This is the first real parent walkthrough the panel asked for, and it happens before any child arrives.
  • Published re-scoreThe §11 panel's scores update on this page after the pilot, with the measured numbers shown alongside them, by Friday, May 14, 2027, three days before the T-4 kill-gate decision on Monday, May 17: the founder posts the update the night of the May 12 evening, not on the deadline day, so the gate's trigger has an author and a work day. If the panel does not clear 9.0 after the pilot, no summer session is sold: the date, the bar, and the re-score are all public, which is what makes them binding. A pilot that ran on the April 24 backup date counts as the pilot for this re-score, and the May 12, May 14, and May 17 dates do not shift for a backup-date pilot; if neither April 17 nor April 24 runs, the May 12 evening reads no pilot numbers, the binding mean cannot clear 9.0, and the session cancels on the same terms as a below-9.0 re-score.

05 · Capstone

Every camper leaves with proof they did what the machine couldn't.

The capstone isn't a diorama, it's a small, real production with an AI in the crew and a kid in charge. Teams of four pick one track and ship it at Showcase:

Track A · The Field Guide

Verified local dataset

A human-collected, human-verified dataset of the neighborhood (species, sounds, measurements), with a model graded against it and every miss explained.

Track B · The Object

Fabricated thing

A physical product born from an AI design, rescued by human engineering: original prompt, failed print, fixed revision, load test on video.

Track C · The Story

Human-sourced piece

An oral-history audio piece no model could record, a real interview, edited, fact-checked, and published with a producer's credit roll.

Track D · The Demo

Live robot run

A real rover on the real course against the AI's predicted route: timed trials, the debug log of every physical fix, and the verdict on whether the prediction survived contact with the floor.

Every capstone ships with the same final artifact: the credit roll, one page naming what the AI did, what the humans did, what failed, and who fixed it. Parents don't just see the project; they see their kid taking responsibility for a machine's work. That page is the diploma.

06 · Staff

One instructor, by design.

The whole program is engineered so a single adult can run it: twelve campers, sixty minutes a day, every material pre-kitted, every post-lesson project block self-directed. There is no assistant, no CIT (counselor-in-training), no second pair of eyes, which is exactly why the format, the tool gating, and the admin automation are built the way they are. The instructor is either the founder operating the camp or one hired educator; either way the AB5 classification question is settled, not dodged.

Age-differentiation model (10–14 range): The four-year spread within the cohort is bridged through tiered challenge variants, not separate tracks. Each day's mission ships with a base version (accessible at 10) and an extension layer (expected at 13–14): e.g., Day 2's photo dataset uses GPS tagging for juniors and adds metadata schema design for seniors; Day 5's CAD redesign uses guided templates for 10-year-olds and freeform modeling for 14-year-olds; Day 7's rover gauntlet adds autonomous waypoint navigation for the older cohort. The labels juniors and seniors mean the same ages everywhere on this page: juniors are ages 10–12 and work the base version, seniors are ages 13–14 and work the extension layer, in every lesson plan, handout margin mark, answer key, and the pilot's senior-only exit question. Rotating crew roles give every camper a turn at the materials captain, Wall of Almost curator, and reset lead jobs, performed inside the group, in the instructor's line of sight, never as supervision; the 13–14s get challenge depth from the senior mission extensions, not exclusive staffing roles. Kids who already code get harder variants of every mission, the rubric scales, the thesis doesn't.

The instructor · 1 adult

A generalist maker-educator

One person: teaching experience plus hands-on maker fluency, background-checked, first-aid certified, AB 506 trained. Must be able to run a sensor build, a CAD session, a taste panel, and an interview drill in the same week. Pay: $2,000 per two-week session (10 instruction hours, a 4-hour Session Zero kitting day, 30 minutes of prep each teaching day (5 hours across the session), plus the 15-minute daily drop-off and check-in window at the door table (2.5 hours across the session), plus present-but-silent supervision through the daily project blocks and the 15-minute daily pickup window (2.5 hours across the session), the 30-minute mid-week restock, the 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18, pulling each finished fixed-revision print off the beds once it reads under 50C and shelving the before-and-after pairs on the museum shelf for the showcase), and the 30-minute Day 10 parents' showcase, and the ~20-minute Day 10 gallery strike after the 11:45 dismissal, roughly 35.8 hours all in, about $56/hr blended). If hired rather than founder-operated, the instructor is a W-2 employee on Gusto payroll with California workers' comp bound before Day 1. Contractor arrangements are off the table: under California's ABC test (AB5, Labor Code §2775) an on-site, on-schedule instructor delivering the camp's core service is an employee, full stop.

Camper crew · rotating, all campers 10–14

Responsibility, not supervision

Three campers per day take crew jobs with a printed checklist: materials captain (kit distribution), Wall of Almost curator, reset lead (end-of-hour reset). The rotation runs through the whole cohort, so every camper serves about every fourth day, and the jobs are built so a 10-year-old can run them straight off the printed sheet: the checklist, not the camper's age, is the qualification, and the instructor re-checks the materials captain's kit count before kits go out. These are leadership roles performed inside the group, in the instructor's line of sight at all times. Nobody under 18 ever supervises, and no camper is ever out of the group. Enrollment sets no age-mix floor, so gating the jobs to 13–14s would leave them unfillable in a younger cohort; the paper-path logic applies here too.

The automation layer

Admin that needs no human

Everything a second adult used to absorb, check-in, parent pings, attendance, pickup authorization records, incident timestamps, is handled by the API stack in §10 (Airtable, Twilio, Typeform). The solo instructor teaches; the software does the paperwork. That is what makes one adult enough.

Prep · Session Zero + 30 min/day

The kitting day: four hours, budgeted, paid

Every day's box is packed by the same person who teaches, in one four-hour Session Zero at the venue the Saturday before the session, not the night before. This is the work the daily 30-minute checklists stand on. It is scheduled, paid, and inside the instructor's hours, not a favor done after hours. After Session Zero, each day's prep is genuinely 30 minutes or less: first the 8:45 morning read of the camp-number inbox, about five minutes, with a "got it" reply to every overnight parent message; then open the day-box, lay out twelve stations, run the tool-safety check, charge the shared devices.

If the instructor phone is replaced or factory-reset mid-session, the new or reset phone gets the toggle check before its first close-of-day re-verification: open the phone's Settings, confirm automatic date-and-time is toggled on, turn it on and leave it on all session if it is off, let one network-synced date read land, and initial the re-check in the morning checklist margin; the close-of-day re-verification never reads a new or reset phone's date until the re-check is initialed, so a replaced or wiped device cannot hand the re-verification an unverified date. If the replacement phone cannot reach a network before its first close-of-day re-verification, because its line is not activated and it has no data and no venue Wi-Fi, the re-check does not stall the close: on a named no-data branch the instructor logs 'replacement phone has no network, re-check not completed, date unverified ' with the date in the morning checklist margin, and the close-of-day re-verification runs the dead-phone close fallback on this live phone, the margin-line read alone with a 'phone has no network, date not cross-checked' margin log and a reach-a-network-before-next-prep clause, so the unverified date is still never read. The re-check gives the network-synced date read a fixed wait budget of five minutes of wall time at the close, timed on the room clock, and inside the budget it pins an attempt sequence of three named attempts, so no passive wait can burn the budget on one stalled attempt: attempt one is the initial read with automatic date-and-time already confirmed on, watching the read for the first two minutes; attempt two fires at the start of the third minute, an airplane-mode cycle, airplane mode on for thirty seconds then off, then a fresh read; attempt three is the venue network join, venue Wi-Fi or the founder's hotspot, then one final read before the budget ends; and if the fifth minute ends with no synced read the no-data branch fires at once and the re-verification proceeds with the margin-line read alone, so a stalled sync can never hold the close past five minutes. Every attempt also leaves an attempt log in the morning checklist margin beside the re-check: after each attempt the instructor writes the attempt number, the room-clock time, and initials, so a failed attempt always leaves a paper trace, and each failed attempt's line also carries one failure word, its failure signature: stale when the phone displayed an old unmoved date, spinning when the sync indicator ran without resolving, blank when no date display appeared at all, so the margin records why the sync failed and not just that it did; when the margin's words disagree, stale on one attempt and spinning on another, the mixed-margin rule names which word outranks the others: the latest attempt's word outranks the earlier ones, because it reflects the phone's most recent state, so the morning retry chases the latest attempt's fix first and only falls back to the earlier words if the latest fix lands and the read still fails; the fallback-chain cap ends the chase at two morning retries: the first morning's retry runs the latest attempt's fix, the second morning's retry runs the fallback fix from the earlier words, and if the second morning's retry still fails after its fix lands, the instructor stops chasing the margin's words and takes the dead-phone close fallback, the margin-line read alone with the margin log standing, at that close and every later close, instead of a third morning retry; only a genuinely new network, an activated line, venue Wi-Fi, or a founder hotspot not available on any earlier attempt, restarts the chase as a new first attempt. When the cap fires, the instructor writes the cap log line in the morning checklist margin beside that close's margin log and initials it beside the words: 'chain capped after second morning retry, fallback read used, date not cross-checked ', so the capped stop carries the same attribution as the successful-read log, whose read log carries the attempt number, the synced date, the room-clock time, and initials. The ordinary no-network close keeps the standing margin log 'phone has no network, date not cross-checked', so the margin shows a capped chain and an ordinary no-network close as two different lines. The date slot on the cap log line always carries the close's calendar date, never the phone's displayed date, so the paper trail never reads the unverified phone date, the one the line says was not cross-checked, as a fact. The date slot takes the spelled-out month and day, written 'October 2', never digits like '10/2', so the paper trail never reads an ambiguous date as a fact. A successful read logs the attempt number, the synced date, the room-clock time, and initials, and the margin ends up showing the full sequence of attempts the close actually ran. The synced-date slot on the successful-read log takes the spelled-out month and day, written 'October 2', never digits like '10/2', so the paper trail never reads an ambiguous date as a fact. The no-data branch fires when the budget ends without a synced read, never on the phone's signal display, so a phone showing bars with no data takes the same branch. At the next morning's 8:45 prep the instructor reads the failure words before the retry, because a run of spinning lines points the retry at the network while a run of stale lines points it at the phone's date storage, then retries the toggle check on the replacement phone when a network is available, an activated line, venue Wi-Fi, or a hotspot from the founder's cell; if the phone still has no network the branch re-fires at that close and the margin log stands for a second consecutive day. 07 · Budget sketch

Honest math for one solo-instructor session.

12 campers × $425 tuition, one two-week session (ten one-hour lessons). Equipment is a one-time startup cost (amortized over 3 summers); the operating table shows a single steady-state session. A donor-funded scholarship bench covers 2 of 12 seats. (The year-1 launch costs, entity, ads, compliance stack, are itemized separately in §10's launch ledger, so these two budgets never double-count.)

Per-session operating

LineCost
Instructor pay (10 hrs teaching, 4-hr Session Zero, 30 min/day prep (5 hrs), 45-min daily project-block supervision (7.5 hrs), the 15-min daily drop-off/check-in window (2.5 hrs), the 15-min daily pickup window (2.5 hrs), the 30-min mid-week restock, the 3-hr Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5, the 30-min Day 10 showcase, and the ~20-min Day 10 gallery strike after the 11:45 dismissal; roughly 35.8 hrs all in, about $56/hr blended; quote-capped at $2,000: the headline is the posted offer, not a billed rate; published anchor, Bay Area 2026 camp-instructor listings (Galileo Learning seasonal postings, $18.00 to $23.26/hr) sit well below the blended about $56/hr, so clearing generic camp-instructor market is not this row's risk; the risk is finding the maker-fluent specialist (sensor builds, CAD, interviews in the same week) at a fixed two-week offer, and the offer rule answers it honestly: the hire accepts at or under $2,000 for the 35.8 hours, whatever the accepted pay comes in under is released, never re-budgeted, the headline never rises, because raising it would rewrite the row's budget, and no instructor signed with a clean background check by T-10 still fires kill gate 3 (see the sourcing row); the section 11 staffing bullet names the same $2,000 terms, not a second payment)$2,000

The session runs $3,270 in the red at $425 tuition, because the venue bills actual room hours (about 34.3 hours at $75/hr, $2,575: the ~25.8 session hours (8:45 prep through 11:15 pickup daily, 11:45 dismissal plus ~20-min gallery strike on Day 10), the 4-hour Session Zero kitting Saturday, which needs venue access because the day-boxes print and pack on site, the 30-minute Wednesday-evening restock after Day 3 (June 16), the 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18), which the fixed-revision prints need the room for, and the ~1-hour post-session equipment move-out after the strike), not the ten teaching hours the old line assumed. Two $2.5k founding sponsorships cover that shortfall, the scholarship bench (2 seats/session), and an equipment-refresh reserve. The $425 price point holds because that is what makes the cohort accessible; the sponsors underwrite the margin by design. Year 2 adds a second session at the same venue, spreading startup costs across twenty lessons instead of ten.

08 · Venue & equipment

A makerspace with a yard, two blocks from reality.

The camp needs a room AI has never been in, with a door that opens onto the world it can't see.

Space
  • Makerspace / STEM lab: 1,500+ sq ft with 6 build tables and power drops
  • Separate quiet nook for instructor admin calls and guest arrival, never used for camper sessions (all twelve campers stay in the main room, in the instructor's line of sight)
  • Secure outdoor yard or adjacent park access for data safaris and rover gauntlet, plus the makerspace interior as the mapped indoor alternate for all four outdoor days (the weather pivot lives in the safety plan)
  • Walkable neighborhood (0.25 mi) for Day 3 field verification missions
  • Gallery wall space for the Showcase exhibition
  • Room screen or projector in the main room (the Day 1 teardown video plays on it during the project block; the instructor laptop drives it). The instructor laptop is the instructor's own machine, not a startup-budget line: it arrives with the instructor each morning, drives the room screen, tethers the flatbed scanner, runs the project-block silent admin, and leaves at dismissal, never stored overnight in the venue. The flatbed scanner stays on the door table all session; its cable re-plugs into the laptop each morning
  • Saturday access for the 4-hour Session Zero kitting block (June 12, 8am–12pm): the boxes pack and lock in the venue's storage closet that day; plus a 30-minute Wednesday-evening restock block after Day 3 (June 16); plus a 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18), when the six fixed-revision files print on the two enclosed printers and the instructor pulls and shelves each finished revision once it reads under 50C; plus the ~20-minute Day 10 gallery strike after the 11:45 dismissal (June 25), when the instructor works the named-home teardown checklist in   • On-site laser printer (or networked copier) for the 599-sheet Session Zero print run, the Day 2 mid-session contact-sheet print (12 pages), the 3 Thursday-morning AI forecast sheets (one per Day 4 team table, printed in the Day 4 prep), the Day 5 project block's 24 photo pages (printed in the block's first minutes), the Wednesday-evening restock reprints after Day 3 (June 16), and the Day 10 showcase programs (printed Friday morning from the filled-in master, one per camper's party)
  • Reliable venue wi-fi sized for 8 simultaneous laptops plus the instructor laptop, with content filtering the instructor can verify at Session Zero: the Day 2 image-model grading station and the Day 6 chatbot tab both need live internet; the ten project blocks run on paper and keep running either way
  • Single-use restroom adjacent to the main room, its door visible from the instructor's seat: the safety plan's one-at-a-time restroom procedure depends on it, and a multi-stall restroom down the hall would break the one-adult supervision model

  • 3× field kits: camera, lav mic, recorder, calipers, clipboard, chalk
  • 8-laptop cart with AI tool accounts + content filters
  • Shop basics: hand tools, one soldering station (instructor-run only), the cutting station kit (2 retractable craft knives, 1 metal ruler, 1 self-healing mat), the 12 blunt-tip table scissors, the camp first-aid kit, the instructor's field bag (contents listed at Session Zero Hour 4), the 8-slot NiMH charger (staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero, charging the Day 7 spare 8-cell set at Hour 3), PPE, the labeled med lockbox, the lockable medication cooler bag with frozen ice pack, a stick-on thermometer strip, fidget tools, and noise-reducing earplugs

09 · Success metrics

We measure the skill, not the vibe.

Camper growth
  • Pre/post assessment: verify-or-trust a set of AI claims, target +40% accuracy on "checked it and here's the evidence"
  • 100% of campers ship a capstone with a completed credit roll
  • Rubric-scored judgment: taste-panel scores align with instructor panel by Day 8
Family verdict
  • Parent NPS (Net Promoter Score, -100 to +100) ≥ 60 at Showcase exit survey
  • ≥ 70% of families say camper "talks about checking AI output at home" (the dinner-table test)
  • ≥ 50% re-enroll or refer for Summer 2028
Program health
  • Enrollment: 12 campers, waitlist ≥ 6 by May 1
  • Safety: zero unsupervised tool incidents; one background-checked adult supervises all 12 campers every minute of every session; field missions move as a single group, no exceptions
  • Financial: sponsorship + scholarship bench fully funded before session 1

10 · Execution plan

The launch plan: automation for the paperwork, humans for the judgment.

This camp can be built by one operator + a stack of real APIs, with humans kept exactly where the law, insurers, and child safety require them: supervising children and owning judgment calls. Below: the full launch plan, seven phases, T-minus timeline, itemized budget, kill gates, and the honest list of steps that cannot be human-free. Every named API was verified to exist in Aug 2026; every marketplace that doesn't expose an API is flagged and given a workaround.

The operating thesis APIs do the paperwork: form the entity, bind insurance, run background checks, push ads, collect tuition, text parents, track rosters. Humans own the judgment: permit walk-throughs, insurance riders, adjudicating a flagged background check, and, non-negotiably, supervising children. Roughly 90% of the launch tasks run without a human touching them; the remaining 10% is the part that makes it legal and safe, compressed into seven single-decision human gates.
Phase 0 · T-22 → T-20 weeks (Jan 2027)

Entity & money

~3 weeks · ~$3,200
  • Stripe Atlas, form the LLC (Delaware via Atlas), EIN auto-issued by the IRS within days, Mercury operating account through Atlas's bank partner. verified API
  • Honest note: Atlas defaults to Delaware; operating in California means foreign qualification ($70) + CA's $800/yr franchise tax anyway. That $870 is real and budgeted below, not a surprise.
  • DocuSign eSignature API, account + templates for the four paper shapes we need: the instructor's W-2 offer and employment agreement (contractor arrangements are off the table; the instructor is a W-2 employee on Gusto payroll), venue agreement, parent waiver, and the Day 6 guest media release (sent the week before the session; the signed copy files into the instructor folder before Session Zero). verified API Pricing honesty: API plans are quote-based; budget $1.8–4k/yr. Decision rule, T-22w: the quote is in hand before Entity + bank close. If the quote exceeds $1,800, the SignWell-class API fallback executes at about $600/yr instead, and the $1,800 launch-ledger line buys the fallback with the difference released, never a shortfall. The $1,800 is a ceiling, not a low-end wish. (Checked this run: SignWell API pricing is pay-as-you-go at $0.85 per document after 25 free documents a month, and the camp sends roughly 20 documents a year, so the ~$600 fallback figure is conservative.)
Kill gate 0, Entity + bank not live in 3 weeks → stop. Nothing downstream can hold money or sign paper.
Phase 1 · T-20 → T-16 weeks (Jan–Feb)

Compliance & insurance

~4 weeks · ~$3,450

Regulatory reality first (California, 2026): a seasonal, primarily-recreational summer day camp is exempt from child-care facility licensing (Health and Safety Code §1596.792, with CDSS, the California Department of Social Services, status confirmed in writing at T-20), but that exemption walks you into two other regimes: (a) the Organized Camps law (HSC §18897 et seq. + Title 17 CCR §§30700-30741) with a permit from the county health department and a qualified on-site Health Supervisor; and (b) AB 506, which requires background checks, mandated-reporter training, and a child-abuse-prevention policy from youth-service organizations, we apply it in full regardless of the for-profit edge case. This plan complies with all three. human seam: county inspector

  • Thimble API (thimble.com/api), binds base General Liability by API, by the job, month, or year, and issues the certificate of insurance naming the venue host as additional insured, which is what actually unblocks the venue booking. verified API Pricing honesty: Thimble publishes a national average of about $42/mo (about $504/yr) with policies starting at $17/mo, and independent reviews put the real-world blended average near $94/mo (about $1,128/yr); the camp class serving ages 10-14 in California, with hand tools and walking field missions, prices above the national small-business average. Bind rule, T-20w: the annual quote is in hand before the venue deposit; if the quote exceeds $1,700, the broker who placed the $1,200 abuse/molestation rider rebids the GL as a package and the $1,700 launch-ledger line still binds the GL alone, never a shortfall; any difference under $1,700 is released, never re-budgeted. The $1,700 is a ceiling, not a quote.
  • Honest seam: the abuse & molestation endorsement, which county inspectors and sane parents require, is underwritten by specialists (camp insurers like K&K), not purchasable via any self-serve API. One broker call. Pricing honesty: K&K's published 2026 youth day camp program prices the sexual misconduct liability option (Option 1, $250K per insured event / $1M aggregate, defense within limits) at $0.45 per camper per week with a $150 minimum premium, which would put a 12-camper, 2-week session at the $150 minimum, but that option is not sold stand-alone: it requires K&K's own GL program, which this plan does not use (the GL binds through Thimble by API), so the published floor is an anchor, not a buy. The cheaper published Option 2 ($100 flat, reimbursement of defense costs only) is not liability coverage and does not satisfy a county inspector. Bind rule, T-20w: the broker's annual quote is in hand before the venue deposit; $1,200 is the quote-capped ceiling, and about $1,200 inside the $1,200 is the annual endorsement alone; if the quote exceeds $1,200 the broker re-shops with competing camp insurers (the run-896 GL package rebid already runs through this same broker); because the endorsement is county-required it cannot be dropped or cut to the defense-only option, so a sustained overage above the ceiling is a founder launch decision at T-20w, in the open, never a silent shortfall. Any difference under $1,200 is released, never re-budgeted.
  • Checkr API, invite + run 1 adult background check (the instructor) at ~$65 incl. county fees. verified API Pricing honesty: Checkr publishes the Basic package at $29.99 per report, pay-as-you-go with no contract, plus court and database fees passed through at cost. Order rule, T-20w: the founder orders the single instructor report before the T-16 hiring sprint; San Mateo County pass-through fees ride inside the $65; $65 is a ceiling, not a quote, and whatever the county fees come in under is released, never re-budgeted. FCRA adverse-action review is the human adjudication seam: a flagged report never auto-clears, and a check not clean by T-10 fires kill gate 3, the session postpones, non-negotiable.
  • AB 506 / mandated-reporter training, online. Pricing honesty: California's own mandated-reporter training, run by CDSS with the Office of Child Abuse Prevention, is free 24/7 self-paced in English and Spanish with a CDSS-issued completion certificate, and AB 506 explicitly allows organizations to use it. Paid third-party AB 506 courses run about $15-25 per seat; the free path trades paid tracking for manual recordkeeping, which the compliance folder already does. Completion rule, T-16: the instructor's certificate is filed before the session is announced; a certificate not on file by T-10 fires kill gate 3 alongside the background check, the session postpones, non-negotiable. About $20 inside the $20 is one paid seat if the founder chooses it; the free CDSS path releases the $20, never re-budgeted. The $20 is a ceiling, not a spend.
  • County organized-camp permit, filed at T-20. Pricing honesty: San Mateo County EHS publishes no flat organized-camp permit fee; it bills fee-for-service at its fully burdened hourly rate, $311/hr effective Jan 1, 2026 (Board File 24-990), and the published California-county analog, Santa Barbara County EHS's 2026 camp plan-check form, bills a nonrefundable $149 application plus $220/hr plan review and inspection. A clean 12-camper day camp with no food, pool, or water costs the application plus under an hour of review and the one site walk-through, so $400 is a defensible ceiling. Filing rule, T-20: the founder files the application online and asks the specialist for the time estimate; if the estimate exceeds $400 the decision lands before the venue deposit, and an application not accepted by T-16 fires kill gate 1 regardless. The permit is annual, so one bind covers both June sessions and the §07 insurance row's about-$200 share is the same annual, not a second buy; whatever the county bills under $400 is released, never re-budgeted.
Kill gate 1, No insurance binder + permit application accepted by T-16 → stop before the venue deposit moves (nothing is refundable-proof at that point). The county can simply be slow; that's a stop, not a workaround.
Phase 2 · T-20 → T-14 weeks (Feb–Mar)

Venue

~4 weeks (overlaps P1) · deposit $300
  • Requirements spec, written once and reused: 1,500+ sq ft makerspace, quiet interview room, secure outdoor access, walkable block, gallery wall, room screen or projector, on-site laser printer, reliable wi-fi sized for 8+ laptops with verifiable content filtering, single-use restroom adjacent to the main room (door visible from the instructor's seat), lockable on-site storage for the day-boxes and equipment June 12–25, weekday 8am–6pm access June 7–25 plus one Saturday 4-hour kitting block (June 12, 8am–12pm) plus one 30-minute Wednesday-evening restock block after Day 3 (June 16) plus one 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18, the fixed-revision print window), COI naming the host.
  • Peerspace / Tagvenue, flagged honestly: neither exposes a public booking or inquiry API (verified Aug 2026; they are message-and-book marketplaces). no public API
  • Workaround: scripted marketplace search + browser automation builds a shortlist of 6 candidate venues with pricing and availability into Airtable; the operator makes one decision, pick + approve deposit (card on file). Hosts reply in-marketplace; the final booking is deliberately not scripted.
  • Budget anchor: ~$2,575/session (about 34.3 hours of room time at the ~$75/h makerspace rate, quote-capped at $75/hr, see the Phase 2 venue-rate rule: the $75/hr is anchored to the San Mateo County Community College District's published July 2026 facilities fee schedule, for-profit Classroom 50 and under at $75/hr (nonprofit $50/hr), corroborated by UW Bothell's 2026 classroom rates (small classrooms up to 30 at $50/hr, big classrooms 31-60 at $75/hr); dedicated makerspace event rooms and Peerspace listings price higher, about $150-$375/hr, so the plan's rate assumes a classroom-style rental (school, church, or community-college room) with the camp's own equipment, not a turnkey makerspace; a signed quote above $75/hr draws first on the $900 contingency share, and a quote the contingency cannot cover fires kill gate 2's die terms, not a second venue buy: the ~25.8 session hours (8:45 prep through 11:15 pickup daily, 11:45 dismissal plus ~20-min gallery strike on Day 10), the 4-hour Session Zero kitting Saturday, plus the 30-minute mid-week restock after Day 3 (June 16), plus the 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18, the fixed-revision print window), plus the ~1-hour equipment move-out after the Day 10 gallery strike (June 25)), deposit due at booking.
  • Venue agreement carries the break-glass cancellation-notice clause: the DocuSign venue agreement (signed by T-14 under kill gate 2) includes a clause authorizing the camp's designated break-glass adult, as named on the camp's keyholder roster at the time of notice, to send written notice cancelling or releasing the remaining dates on the camp's behalf; the notice must quote the clause and the roster entry, so the authority is on the contract, not on the keyholder's word alone. The clause also pins the notice's timing: the release notice is effective upon sending by the agreement's named notice method, not upon the venue's receipt, so on the night the clause matters, when the legal-notice address reaches the venue's legal entity but no person answers, the release is timed by the keyholder's sent notice, and the venue cannot later claim the cancellation was late or that remaining-date fees are owed. The clause also pins the break-glass notice's email sufficiency: at T-14 signing the founder reads the agreement's notice provision and the clause adopts its email path when one is named; if the venue's standard form names only certified mail, the break-glass clause adds email as a permitted method for this notice type only, so the keyholder at 9 PM sends the release by email from a phone and the effective-upon-sending timing attaches to the sent email, not to a certified-mail drop no one can reach after hours. The clause also pins the break-glass notice's sender identity to the roster name, not the sending domain: the keyholder at 9 PM sends from a personal phone with a personal sending address, so the notice's identity is the quoted roster entry, not the email domain, and the venue verifies the sender by the roster name it already holds, the name put in writing at signing, at the T-6w build, and with every keyholder change, so a personal sending address cannot dispute that the notice came from the camp's authorized sender. The clause also pins the break-glass notice's subject-line convention: the 9 PM email's subject line names the clause and quotes the remaining session dates, so the release is triage-visible in the venue's inbox on the night it goes out, not a clause-quoting body under a generic subject. The convention's dates are read off a printed page, never computed at 9 PM: the sealed break-glass envelope the T-6w build seals holds a one-page session calendar listing the ten teaching dates with their calendar dates, so the keyholder circles the dates printed after the broadcast night and pastes them into the subject line. The convention's clause name is never typed at 9 PM either: the sealed break-glass envelope holds a subject-line template beside the calendar, a printed page with the break-glass cancellation-notice clause's name already printed and a blank date line to fill, so the keyholder copies the circled dates onto the template's date line and reads the clause name off the template verbatim, typing no fixed text on the worst evening of the session, and a mistyped clause name can never undercut the triage visibility the convention exists for. The notice's quoted body is never typed at 9 PM either: the sealed break-glass envelope holds a printed clause-quote card and a printed roster card beside the calendar and the template, the clause-quote card carrying the break-glass cancellation-notice clause's full text printed from the signed agreement, with the clause's operative authority passage, the sentence granting the designated break-glass adult authority to send written notice cancelling or releasing the remaining dates, re-printed in an isolated box at the top of the card, the passage alone in larger type, the box assembled at print time by copying the passage verbatim from the card's own full-text print, never re-typed from memory, and read back against the signed agreement before the envelope is sealed, and the roster card carrying the keyholder's roster entry as the venue holds it, so the keyholder copies the boxed operative passage out of the isolated box and the roster entry into the email body verbatim, never starting from an adjacent unmarked line of the full text, and the authority the venue reads is the contract's own words, not the keyholder's memory of them, not the keyholder's choice of fragment, and not a box the founder re-typed. The roster card is re-printed in the re-walk sitting whenever the keyholder changes, so a 9 PM body can never quote a stale name; the clause-quote card is not re-printed on a keyholder change, because the clause did not change, so the re-sealed envelope holds the new roster card beside the untouched boxed card; a date shift moves neither the clause nor the roster, so the cards ride the date-shift re-print with their contents and their box unchanged, the date-shift re-seal printing a fresh envelope ID across the new band and retiring the old one, so the Day 1 photo read always covers the current envelope. If the session dates ever shift after signing, the founder re-prints and re-seals the envelope the same day the dates move, so no 9 PM trigger can fire on a stale calendar, and the template rides the re-print beside the calendar. The clause authorizes by role, not by name: the agreement is signed eight weeks before the T-6w build names the keyholder, so the roster always carries the current keyholder's name, and the venue's designated contact, the agreement's signing counterparty, and the agreement's legal-notice address are recorded on the roster at signing, the counterparty as the standing escalation fallback if the contact goes silent and the legal-notice address as the third line that cannot turn over with staff, read off the agreement's own notice provision at signing; the contact line is re-confirmed at the T-2w step the Phase 6 week-before window names; if no keyholder is named at signing, the founder sends the venue the roster name the week the keyholder is named, no later than the T-6w build, and whenever the keyholder changes afterward the founder sends the venue the updated roster name in writing as part of the re-walk the T-6w row requires, so the name the venue holds never lags the person who might send the notice.
Kill gate 2, No signed venue by T-14 → no June session. The venue is the long pole; there is no second choice without moving the cohort's dates.
Phase 3 · T-16 → T-10 weeks (Feb–early Apr)

Staffing: one instructor

~6 weeks (overlaps P2) · ~$300 sourcing + $2,000/session comp
  • AB5 settled, not dodged: the instructor is either the founder operating the camp (no classification question at all) or a W-2 employee on Gusto payroll (~$55/mo + ~$255 employer-side taxes per session at published rates), with California workers' comp bound before Day 1. Under the ABC test (AB5, Labor Code §2775) an on-site, on-schedule instructor delivering the camp's core service is an employee, so contractor arrangements, and the Deel contractor flow the original plan named, are off the table by design. Pricing honesty: Gusto publishes the Simple plan at $49/mo plus $6 per person (the base rose from $40 to $49 in March 2026); one W-2 employee costs $55/mo, so three months is about $165, not the $120 the old $40 figure implied. Employer-side taxes on $2,000 of session wages run about $235 at published rates (Social Security 6.2%, Medicare 1.45%, FUTA 0.6% on the first $7,000 with California's credit-reduction risk, CA SUI 3.4% new-employer rate on the first $7,000 per CA EDD, CA ETT 0.1%; SDI is employee-side and not an employer cost); the $255 is a ceiling, not a computed quote. Activation rule, T-16: the Gusto account opens only when a non-founder W-2 instructor signs the offer in Phase 3; if the founder operates the camp, the account never opens and the whole $420 releases, never re-budgeted; the $420 is a ceiling, not a quote, and whatever the subscription plus taxes come in under it is released, never re-budgeted. Pricing honesty: the WCIRB reports the average California charged rate at $1.64 per $100 of payroll for the first half of 2026 (WorkCompCentral, Oct 9, 2026), and the insurance commissioner approved a 6.6% advisory pure premium increase effective Sept 1, 2026, so the computed premium on $2,000 of session wages is only about $20-$35; the $450 ceiling covers the one-employee policy minimum premium plus policy and broker fees, not the computed rate. Bind rule, T-16: the workers' comp policy binds only when a non-founder W-2 instructor signs the offer in Phase 3; if the founder operates the camp, the policy is $0 and the whole $450 releases, never re-budgeted; $450 is a ceiling, not a quote, and whatever the broker binds under it is released, never re-budgeted; the policy binds before Day 1. Verify with employment counsel before launch.
  • Sourcing: one hire through local maker-space and school job boards (~$300 in listings). Upwork is flagged honestly, its hiring API is restricted (enterprise/managed access, not open for automated posting), so it is a backup, not the plan. restricted API Pricing honesty: Craigslist charges $75 per 30-day job posting in the SF Bay Area, the only US metro at the $75 premium; the maker-space and school job boards named above post free as community listings, so the $300 ceiling covers up to four Craigslist postings alongside the free boards. Posting rule, T-16: the founder posts the listings at the start of Phase 3; each posting runs 30 days; $300 is a ceiling, not a quote, and whatever the postings cost under it is released, never re-budgeted; under the founder-as-instructor path the listings never post and the whole $300 releases, never re-budgeted, mirroring the Gusto activation rule; no instructor signed with a clean background check by T-10 still fires kill gate 3.
  • Screening: async video interview transcribed by a speech-to-text API and scored against a published rubric (kid-safety judgment ranked above technical skill); two reference calls scheduled by Twilio; decision recorded in Airtable.
  • Adjudication seam: a flagged background check goes to a human decision (FCRA, the Fair Credit Reporting Act, adverse-action review). No auto-reject, ever.
  • Structure: 1 instructor behind the 1:12 cap. The adult-in-the-room floor is the irreducible human core of this plan, see Honest Seams below. There is no degraded staffing mode: the format is built so one adult is sufficient, and anything that needs a second adult does not ship.
Kill gate 3, No instructor with a clean background check by T-10 → postpone. There is exactly one adult in the room; there is no smaller crew to fall back to.
Phase 4 · T-18 → T-4 weeks (Feb–mid May)

Camper acquisition

~14 weeks · ~$2,800
  • Meta Marketing API + Google Ads API, both real, both scriptable: launch, budget, pause, and report by code. verified APIs $2,500 spend plan; target CPL (cost per lead) $40–70, implied CAC (customer acquisition cost) ≈ $210/camper. Pricing honesty: WordStream's 2026 Google Ads benchmarks put Education and Instruction CPL at $77.48 (PPC Chief's consolidated 2026 set: $90.02), so the plan's $40-$70 target is the optimistic bound and the funnel's 45-lead figure holds only at or below benchmark CPL. The $2,500 is a spend ceiling, not a forecast: the ad accounts launch with $2,500 API-side budget caps, whatever comes in under it is released, never re-budgeted, and the CPL over $90 two-week kill switch in the next bullet is the binding guard, it pauses the spend automatically.
  • Funnel math (honest version): ~$2.5k spend → ~45 qualified leads → 12 enrollments at ~27% lead→paid conversion. If CPL runs > $90 for two straight weeks, ad sets pause automatically, the API kills its own spend.
  • Stripe Payment Links (created via API), $425 tuition, deposit option, scholarship codes. verified API Processing 2.9% + 30¢ ≈ $152/session, booked on the §07 operating table's payment-processing row.
  • Typeform API intake (camper info, allergies, pickup authorizations, accommodation requests) webhooked into Airtable; DocuSign API sends the liability waiver + photo release automatically on payment. verified APIs Pricing honesty (October 2026): Typeform published cards price Basic at $28/mo billed yearly and $39/mo month-to-month (100 responses per month, 1 user); the paid plans bill yearly-only, so a 3-month launch subscription is only buyable month-to-month, about $117 for the window. File upload is a paid-plan feature with a 1GB limit on Basic, plenty for the parent-provided allergy-plan PDFs, so Plus is not needed. The free plan's 10 responses per month cannot carry a 12-camper intake with a waitlist, so the plan is paid month-to-month Basic with no annual lock. Subscription rule: the subscription opens when the enrollment forms go live at the start of Phase 4 and cancels the month after session 1 fills, or at T-4, whichever comes first; $150 is a ceiling, not a quote, and whatever the three monthly billings come in under it is released, never re-budgeted.
  • Landing page: this site, enrollment CTAs activate when the live Payment Link publishes at T-18. Hosting is free (GitHub Pages); forms and payments are all API-side.
Kill gate 4 (soft, T-8), fewer than 6 paid → cut ad spend to $800 retarget-only. Hard, T-4: fewer than 10 paid → cancel session, refund in full via Stripe, run the wind-down checklist (the seven-step version the instructor-sick-day bullet enumerates: the T-6w build already scheduled all ten crisis broadcasts, so step one is disarming them and step six edits the greeting's TTS text). Binding re-score gate: if the post-pilot panel re-score has not published on this page by Friday, May 14, 2027, or its May 12 binding mean lands below 9.0, the session cancels on the same terms, enrollments or not: 9.0 or the camp does not run. A mean of exactly 9.0 clears the binding gate under the T-4w at-or-above rule and does not cancel, so the cancel trigger reads the published mean, not a clears-below phrase that the page defines as impossible. Cancelling at T-4 strands ≈ $4.7k (sunk ad spend + the pilot spend + non-refundable setup + "the first AI-seat billing cycle, about $300" itemized: the T-6w build provisions the 12 seats before the T-4 gate, and on month-to-month business billing the first cycle bills at purchase and cancel stops future renewals only, OpenAI's published seat policy keeps the current month billed on any reduction, so the first of the 2 billed months is spent whether the session runs or not), that number is the plan's true worst case, and it's why the gates exist. Why ten: ten paid seats plus the two donor-funded scholarship seats equal the full 12-seat cohort, 12 x $425 = $5,100 against the $8,370 per-session operating budget (itemized in §07). A full session already runs $3,270 in the red, which the founding sponsorships absorb by design; at nine paid seats plus the two donor-funded scholarship seats, eleven revenue-generating seats, the hole is about $3,695 (11 x $425 = $4,675 against $8,370), and that gap would come out of the equipment-refresh reserve, so it cancels at the gate instead of burning the reserve.
Phase 5 · T-12 → T-2 weeks (Mar–late May)

Ops & equipment

~10 weeks (overlaps P3–P4) · equipment on the §07 startup list ($4,838)

Phase 6 · T-2 → T-0 (June) · then daily

Run-of-show

2 weeks setup · daily ops
  • Pre-camp: instructor on-site prep day, venue walk-through with the county inspector (permit sign-off), tool-safety certification, dismissal-flow rehearsal. Plus the session's one research task, done during the week before (about 45 minutes, not part of any teaching morning): pre-verify the 15 Day 3 town claims against primary sources (city site, shop-owner briefing sheet). The verified stack is sealed in the Day 3 box at Session Zero; the claims are town facts, so the same stack serves every session at the venue. The week-before window holds one more digital task, about 15 minutes: build the 20-thumbnail contact-sheet template the Day 2 label party assembles the 12 contact-sheets from (one page, 20 numbered photo placeholders each with label lines) on the instructor laptop; the Session Zero Hour 3 kitting step copies the finished file onto the one labeled grading-station machine, so the kitting Saturday loads a file, not a wish. The week-before items that repeat every June session, the three guest briefings (about ninety minutes), the week-of logistics pack and snack buy (about thirty), the fail-of-the-day slides (about fifteen), the Thursday forecast sheet (about ten), the Day 7 route prediction (about ten), and the blunder refresh, total about three hours, plus the claims research (about 45 minutes, at a new venue only) and the one-time year-1 digital builds the repeat list skips (the contact-sheet template, the pre-fixed CAD template, and the phone-stand designs, about 50 minutes): roughly four hours in year 1, three after; they are operator work, done by the camp founder before the instructor's paid session hours begin, so the instructor's $2,000 pay line still covers exactly the stated 35.8 hours (the figure moved up from 31 when the 15-minute daily drop-off and check-in window landed in the pay itemization, and up again when the 15-minute daily pickup window landed with it) and no hired W-2 instructor works unpaid time. The week-before window holds one more digital task, about 10 minutes: generate the Day 7 AI route prediction, the page the lesson's 0:00-0:10 open posts and the Day 7 prep prints.   • The week-before window holds one more digital task, about 30 minutes: pick the 12 blunders and lay out the blunder sheet. The Session Zero Hour 1 print run prints 36 blunder sheets (3 binders x 12, "this spring's blunders"), and the whole Day 1 AI-Fail thread runs through them: the lesson's 0:00-0:10 AI Fail of the Day opener dissects one, the 0:25-0:40 autopsy has each camper pick three and add each to the journal with one autopsy line, and the binders live in the Day 1 box, refreshed each year, but no step anywhere picks the 12 blunders, so the kitting Saturday would have printed 36 sheets off a selection that was never made. Pick the 12 in the week-before window. Selection rules: every blunder must be on the public record (news article, company postmortem, court record) with a cited source; age-appropriate for 10 to 14 (no graphic, sexual, or disturbing imagery, and no blunder whose punchline is someone getting hurt); spread across the failure types the camp teaches (hallucinated facts, bad advice taken literally, overconfident vision or captioning, wrong math, biased or unfair output); prefer blunders with a public correction or consequence, because the lesson is accountability, not mockery. Two of the 12 are standing picks, because they are the reason this camp exists: the 2023 case of the lawyer sanctioned after ChatGPT invented court citations, and Google AI Overviews' 2024 "glue on pizza" answer. The other ten are that spring's fresh picks, found with one news search per failure type. Lay out one sheet per blunder on the instructor laptop, one page each: the headline line; the what-happened line, one sentence; the where-it-was-reported line, publication and date, so the instructor can cite it at the opener; the why-it-failed line, naming the failure type; the takeaway line, what a producer checks before trusting this kind of output. The April pilot runs the Day 1 equivalent, so the pilot's prep week runs this same selection task once, picking that spring's 12, and the pilot's April copy-shop run prints the 24 sheets (12 pages x 2 binders) from the pilot-prep-week selection file. The June week-before window then refreshes any blunder that went stale between April and June and saves the pilot-prep-week selection file print-ready; the Session Zero Hour 1 run prints the 36 sheets from that file (12 pages x 3 binders), and Hour 2 slides 12 into each binder. The two fail-of-the-day slides (Day 1 and Day 6) use blunder 1 and blunder 7 of the selection, so the slides and the binders carry the same paper. The April pilot's prep week picks the 12 once and the June session reuses the saved file, the same way it reuses the Gauntlet deck; next spring the pilot-week picks a new 12, which is what "refreshed each year" means. No budget-line changes: pre-session instructor labor like the claim research.
  • The week-before window holds one more digital task, about 90 minutes: lay out the ten late-batch paper pieces. A re-sweep of the Session Zero Hour 1 print list found ten content-bearing items with a print step and no authorship step anywhere on the page: the 12 Day 2 mission cards, the Day 2 grading sheet, the 12 Day 3 verdict cards, the 12 Day 3 verdict-report templates, the 6 Day 5 queue slips, the 12 Day 7 debug log sheets, the 2 Day 9 ship lists, the 12 Day 10 diplomas, the 12 Day 10 taste-panel award cards, and the Day 10 showcase program master. They were never assigned to a week-before layout task, so the kitting Saturday would have printed them off layouts that were never drawn. Lay all ten out on the instructor laptop, print-ready. The mission card: the mission brief line ("document every plant species on the block"), the four safari rules (move as one group, inside the chalk boundary, cameras always on straps, no camper past the chalk), and the junior/senior line (juniors GPS-tag each photo; seniors add the metadata schema). The grading sheet ("AI vs. you"), one sheet hung beside the grading station: a tally table with one row per team and columns for the model's calls right, the campers' calls right, and misses moved to the Wall of Almost. The verdict card: a claim line copied from the camper's claim card, three verdict circles (verified, busted, unverifiable), evidence lines (photo, measurement, primary source), and a team-initials line. The verdict-report template: two claim blocks, each with a claim line, a verdict line, evidence lines, and a source line; a name line and a team-initials line; and an appendix box for the couldn't-confirm second-check cost. The queue slip, one per pair: a pair-name line, the fixed-CAD filename line (the file the instructor queues from the lesson), a queue-position number, and a verified tick for the 0:30 queue check. The "Crash Report" debug log sheet: a name and team line; four failure rows (what failed, the physical cause, the fix); the "the simulation said / the world said" line; a seniors' waypoint-sketch box; a file-it line. The ship list: a twelve-row table (artifact, owner first name, evidence line) under a "no new features after today, only finishing" line, filled by hand in the Day 9 prep from the QC gates as they come off the wall. The diploma ("Producer of AI Projects, Certified Human"): the title line, a camper-name line, the certification line naming the camp and the ten-day program, the instructor signature line, and the date line; the twelve print personalized at Hour 1 from the session roster file, so no names are hand-written on a teaching morning. The taste-panel award card: an award-name line, a recipient-name line, and the "voted by the Day 8 taste panel" line; printed blank, names copied in the Day 10 prep from the circled scorecards. The showcase program master: a program header with the 11:15-11:45 showcase line, twelve station rows in pitch order (order number, camper first name, capstone title, track), and the room-map note; filled in Thursday during the Day 9 project block at the instructor table. Save all ten print-ready; the Session Zero Hour 1 run prints them from those files, and Hour 2 packs them into their day boxes (the mission cards laminate at Hour 2 with the Producer's Checklists, already in the cut list, and the laminated set is reused across sessions). The April pilot's prep week lays them out once and the June session reuses the saved files the same way it reuses the Gauntlet deck, except the diplomas, which re-print personalized from each session's roster. No budget-line changes: every one of the ten already rides in the 599-sheet Hour 1 count, pre-session instructor labor like the claim research.

Snacks-and-prints order rule: one retail grocery-and-copy-shop cart, bought in this window; the ceiling is the $90 tags (55 + 12 + 15 + 3 + 4 + 1); whatever the cart comes in under is released, never re-budgeted; an overage is a founder decision in the open at buy time, scope cut or contingency draw, never a silent shortfall; the rule can only release money, never demand it. Gate 6 (continuous), Any unsupervised-tool incident or ratio breach = same-day stop-work on that activity, tracker entry, and parents notified within the hour. Two breaches = that activity is done for the summer.

the logged no-clerk failure is terminal for the day at that store, so the founder may not re-enter the same store later that day to re-run the check once staff appear, because the logged failure already closed the hand-carry and a return would reopen it; a same-store re-entry takes the same closure, the hand-carry staying closed on the recorded failure with the incident log as the evidence of record, no new check and no new question; The terminality is for that calendar day only: the next calendar day, the founder may re-attempt the same store's kiosk once, running the full two-part check fresh as that day's one visit under the same one-store bound, because a clerk staffing the kiosk on a new day is a new condition, not a reopening of the closed hand-carry; the one re-attempt is the whole window, no same-store re-entry that next day and no second store's kiosk that day; and if the next-day attempt also closes on a locked-port failure, the founder marks the USB route unavailable on the card for the rest of the session and the card's email route becomes the pinned transfer path, so no third-day kiosk attempt happens; where the card already carries the USB-route-unavailable mark, the window never opens, because the check reads the mark, skips the USB attempt, and defaults to the card's pinned email-to-print address without a visit. A missed window has a bounded meaning: if the store's weekday hours on the card or the camp schedule keep the founder from reaching the store the next calendar day, the window rolls one day to the next day the founder can reach the store, still one visit, still the full two-part check fresh, still under the same one-store bound, and the founder logs the rolled window with time and the founder's initials; before the rolled visit leaves, the founder re-confirms the third store's weekday hours against the card, calling the card's phone number or checking the store's posted hours, because the card's hours were inked at the T-6w build and the store may have changed them since; on the call the founder also asks whether the store has any exception closure on the rolled day's date, a holiday shutdown, seasonal closure, inventory day, or other posted one-off the weekly hours never show; when the founder reads posted hours instead of calling, the founder reads the posted notice for exception-day postings too; the founder updates the card's weekday-hours line against a changed answer, logs the re-confirm with time and the founder's initials, the exception answer riding the same log line, and the roll's reachability reads the re-confirmed hours plus the exception answer, never the stale ink, so a store closed on the rolled day never reads as reachable: if the re-confirmed hours close the rolled day, or the exception answer covers the rolled day's date, the visit moves to the next day the re-confirmed hours, the exception answer, and the camp schedule allow, still one visit, still the full two-part check fresh, still inside the two-calendar-day end the roll already pins; if the store does not answer and posts no hours, the founder keeps the card's existing hours as the pinned fallback, logs the failed re-confirm attempt with time and the founder's initials, and the roll reads the existing hours, so a silent phone never strands the window with no read at all; the re-confirm is true at the call: a same-day emergency closure, an early close or a staff-event shutdown enacted after the call, could lock the door between the re-confirm and the founder's arrival. The founder pins the arrival-door read: at the door, before entering, the founder reads the door for any posted closure notice covering that day; if a posted notice covers the day, the founder logs the arrival-door read with time and the founder's initials (the notice's wording), does not enter, and the visit is not spent; if the posted notice is a partial-day notice, a sign reading 'closed until 2 PM' or 'opens late' that names a specific opening time that day rather than a full-day closure, the founder logs the arrival-door read with time and the founder's initials (the notice's wording and the stated opening time), does not run the bounded wait, because the notice already explains the dark door, and compares the stated opening time to the founder's visit window that day. The founder's visit window is the 90 minutes from 11:15 to 12:45 on the trip day, 11:45 to 1:15 when the trip day is Day 10; on a teaching day when the late-pickup escalation pushes dismissal past 11:15, the window starts at dismissal and still ends at 12:45, because the founder never leaves a waiting camper and the fixed end is what the stated-opening comparison reads against; the window must contain the full check, not just the door read: the check's own clock is fifteen minutes on the founder's phone clock, from the door read through the locked-port look, one clerk question, the send, and the ninety-second settle; the window never overlaps the 9:15-11:15 teaching block, because one adult cannot leave the room with campers in it; and the founder's window is not the shop owner's storefront visit window the Day 3 prep checklist re-confirms by text. If the founder can return to the store at or after the stated opening with the check's own fifteen minutes still inside that day's visit window, the visit is not spent, the founder returns once that same day at or after the stated opening, reads the door fresh, and runs the check if the door opens, a posted full-day notice or a new notice covering the rest of the day firing the arrival-door read normally with the visit not spent and the window rolling one day inside the two-calendar-day end, while a still-dark door with no new notice at the stated opening runs the bounded wait before the read fires, the two logged pulls standing as the waits, because a stale notice carries the exception answer's same freshness gap; if the door opens after the wait, the founder runs the check only with the check's own fifteen minutes still inside the window, otherwise logging the read without running the check, the visit not spent, the day treated as covered, and the window rolling one day inside the two-calendar-day end; if the stated opening falls after the founder's visit window that day, or fewer than fifteen minutes of the window remain at the stated opening, the founder treats the day as covered like a full-day closure, the visit not spent, the window rolling one day inside the two-calendar-day end, because an hours-long wait on the storefront is the same unstaffable loitering the bounded wait was built to end, and a check tail past the fixed end reopens the same unbounded errand; if the stated opening is already past when the founder reads the notice and the door is still dark, the founder treats the notice as stale and runs the bounded wait on the dark door, because staff running late is the most plausible explanation for a notice whose opening should already have come, and a stale notice offers no future opening for a return to read against; the two pulls stand as the waits, the first on arrival, the five minutes on the phone clock, each pull logged with time and initials, and a third pull is never taken; if the door opens during the wait the founder enters and runs the check only with the check's own fifteen minutes still inside the window, otherwise the founder logs the arrival-door read with time and the founder's initials (the notice's wording marked stale, plus the locked-door fact), does not enter, does not run the check, and the visit is not spent: the window rolls one day to the next day the re-confirmed hours, the exception answer, and the camp schedule allow, still one visit, still the full two-part check fresh, still inside the two-calendar-day end; a stale notice on an open door sends the founder in normally, the check running under the same fifteen-minute containment; Before any clocked branch runs, the founder checks the phone at the arrival-door read: if the phone is dead or its screen unreadable, the founder runs no clocked step at all, no stated-opening comparison, no bounded wait, and no check, because every trip-day clock, the window comparison, the bounded wait's five minutes, and the check's own fifteen minutes, reads the founder's phone clock, and a founder who guesses times can run a fifteen-minute check against a window that already closed with the incident log unable to verify a time it never read. The founder reads the posted notice, if any, and the door, and logs the arrival-door read with the founder's initials and the line 'phone dead, time unlogged' in place of the time, the notice wording recorded verbatim and the locked or dark door fact recorded beside it; the founder does not enter and does not run the check, and the visit is not spent: the window rolls one day to the next day the re-confirmed hours, the exception answer, and the camp schedule allow, and the return runs the full visit with a charged phone, the window comparison and the fifteen-minute check containment still governing. A dead or unreadable phone is the only branch that writes a log line without a time; every other branch still writes time and initials. The time-unlogged line sequences by visit, not by clock: the founder writes it in the day's trip log in the order the visits happened that day, and at the return the founder reconciles the hand-carry's evidence visit by visit, so the un-timed entry reads in its visit position between the timed entries around it, and an audit never time-sorts the hand-carry record, because a time sort would drop the un-timed line to the top or the bottom and read the visit out of order. The time-unlogged line stays time-unlogged through reconciliation: the founder never annotates it at the return with a recovered or estimated time, from the charged phone's step log, a drive receipt, or memory, because a backfilled estimate would read as a logged fact in an audit while the hand-carry record never read that time; the line's visit-order position is the audit's time reference, and charging the phone back up at the return does not rewrite the trip log. The dead-clock line's row in the timestamped incident log carries the row-timestamp marker: when the founder writes the arrival-door read into the log, the row's time field reads 'time unlogged, row timestamp is log write time, not read time', because the log auto-stamps every row with the write moment and an unmarked auto-timestamp on a time-unlogged line would be read as the arrival read time, the exact clock reading the no-backfill rule was built to keep out. The marker rides the row into every export and print, so no audit and no county inspector reads the write moment as the visit moment. On the paper backstop the marker takes the paper phrasing: when the founder writes the arrival-door read onto the one-page paper med/incident log sheet during an outage, the row's time field reads 'time unlogged, paper row has no auto timestamp, write time is not read time', because the paper sheet has no auto-stamp and a phrasing that names one would read as nonsense on the sheet; and at the evening transcription the founder writes the full marker into the Airtable row's time field, so the paper-to-Airtable path lands on the same marker the direct write carries and the transcription never drops the marker. Honest seams, the humans this plan refuses to remove

Each seam is compressed to a single human decision, made once (or once a day), with everything else prepared by the stack:

  • 1 · County health inspector, the organized-camp permit requires a human walk-through and sign-off at the venue. One meeting, T-2 weeks.
  • 2 · Insurance broker, the abuse & molestation endorsement isn't self-serve on any API. One call, T-20 weeks.
  • 3 · Background-check adjudication, FCRA adverse-action review of any flagged candidate. A human reads, a human decides, per candidate.
  • 4 · Venue deposit approval, one decision on a shortlist of six. Money moves only after the click.
  • 5 · The adult in the room, one trained, background-checked, first-aid-certified adult on site every single minute campers are present, backed by the automated admin stack (Airtable, Twilio, Typeform). The 1:12 cap is a hard ceiling, and the 60-minute lesson format with self-directed project blocks is what makes one adult sufficient. This isn't overhead; it's the product.
  • 6 · Medication & incident command, the instructor administers meds and owns every incident call. Never automatable, never delegated to software.
  • 7 · Pickup authorization, photo-ID match against the signed authorization record, at the door, every day. The robot never dismisses a child.

API reality check (audited Aug 2026)

Every integration named in this plan, verified, including the ones that don't exist, because planning around a hallucinated API is how autonomous launches die:

ServiceStatusRole in the launch
Stripe Atlas / Payment Linksverified APIEntity, EIN, banking; tuition collection & refunds

Thimbleverified APIGeneral-liability bind (thimble.com/api), base GL only; rider via broker

Gustoverified APIW-2 payroll for the single instructor (contractor arrangements off the table; AB5 settled)
Meta Marketing / Google Adsverified APIsCamper acquisition spend + automated kill rules
Typeform / Airtable / Twilioverified APIsIntake, database of record, parent comms
Digi-Key / Mouserverified APIsProgrammatic electronics ordering (sensors, micro:bits, solder stock)
Peerspace / Tagvenueno public APIShortlist via scripted search + browser automation; booking & deposit = human seam
Amazon Businessno public ordering APIAgent-assembled carts; one human checkout click for big-ticket equipment
Upwork hiringrestricted APIAgent-assisted posting via the UI; sourcing diversified to local boards

Correction for the record: an earlier draft cited "AB-2621" as the CA camp-licensing bill. Verified against leginfo, AB 2621 is not camp legislation (it's regional climate-planning in 2020, unemployment-insurance in 2022). The real anchors are HSC §1596.792 (child-care exemption), HSC §18897 et seq. + Title 17 CCR §§30700-30741 (organized camps), and AB 506 (youth-org background checks + mandated-reporter training). This page keeps the receipts.

11 · The red team

We grade ourselves harder than any parent will.

This camp teaches kids to check AI's work. The least we can do is check our own. Before a single parent sees this plan, a seven-person panel red-teams it against one question: could one instructor plausibly teach this camp with one hour of direct instruction a day? The panel's scores and its demands are published here, including the ones we have not cleared yet. Re-scored 2026-09-07 after the device-free take-home option and the pilot evidence plan were published: 8.3/10. The plan ships no session until the current dated mean clears 9.0: the May 12 binding mean, the mean of the five cards the pilot answers, before the July 2 subsection posts, and the July 2 panel-verdict figure, the mean of the seven posted cards, after it.

The instructor · 8.5/10

"Teachable. The handouts exist."

The run-of-show plans, the 30-minute prep lists, and now the ten printed handouts are real lesson plans. The project block states plainly that the instructor stays in the room, silent, supervising. The remaining points are a pilot session: no paper plan, however complete, finds every surprise a real ten-year-old will invent.

The parent · 8.5/10

"The take-home question is answered."

The day is pinned to clock times (9:15 drop-off, 11:15 pickup), the project block's supervision is stated in plain language, and every project block runs on printed sheets with no device assumed. The device-free take-home option is now stated outright: paper paths for everything, no shared device goes home, conversation cards for the dinner table. Still open: the first real parent walkthrough night of the safety plan, scheduled for the week of the April 2027 pilot. Flagged and fixed in this pass: the night was the only panel-scheduled evening with no written run-of-show, while this page's own standard is minute-by-minute plans; the pilot evidence plan's walkthrough-night paragraph now carries the written 45-minute sequence (door-table sign-in with pickup-authorization-list initialing, the safety-plan walk with the room script, the field variant, and the incapacitation variant read aloud, questions with written capture of requested changes, the 48-hour-answer close), and the night's 45 minutes stay inside the pilot's existing about-$381 room cost. Scores untouched: the remaining points still belong to the real Tuesday night, not to the written plan.

The 11-year-old camper · 8.5/10

"The days sound fun. Don't leave me stuck."

The handouts give the quiet 45 minutes a real structure: numbered steps, minute marks, a named job for finishing early, and a stuck protocol. A ten-year-old with no adult teaching can follow a printed page. The remaining points belong to a real ten-year-old in the room; paper cannot spend them.

The 14-year-old camper · 8/10

"Not babyish. Keep the hard versions real."

The senior extensions now sit inside the handouts themselves, not just the lesson plans: computing forecast error, writing the "what we almost let through" case study, sketching the rover waypoint plan. The core pace still skews young on paper; a pilot will tell whether the hard versions are hard enough. Flagged and fixed since the re-score: the pilot recruited ages 10 to 12 only, so the senior extensions had no testers and this card's open question could not be answered on pilot day. Panel verdict

8.3/10. Below the 9.0 bar. Not launched.

What the panel demanded, and what changed on this page: the full handout kit is now published in §03b, all ten project-block sheets written in full; the day is pinned to clock times (9:15 drop-off, 11:15 pickup, Day 10 to 11:45 with the parents' showcase at 11:15-11:45) in §04; the instructor stays in the room through the project block, silent, supervising; the Health Supervisor role is named in the safety plan; parent pings match the day; pay is stated against all present hours; the device-free take-home option is stated outright in the FAQ; and the pilot evidence plan is published in §04, with a date (April 17, 2027), a recruiting channel, a published success bar, and a binding re-score on this page, posted as a dated May 12, 2027 subsection at the end of section 11. The same edit rewrites the lede re-score line and the panel verdict figure to the May 12 binding mean defined in the T-4w row, the mean of the five cards the pilot answers, with the seven-card mean stated in the subsection as the audit figure, so the subsection and the headlines never disagree. The T-minus timeline in §10 now schedules the pilot, the walkthrough, and the binding re-score, and the hard kill gate at T-4 cannot clear until the re-score clears 9.0, so the 9.0 precondition is wired into the launch plan, not just stated here. What remains is all real-world: the pilot itself, the parent walkthrough night, the 14-year-old's question about whether the hard versions are hard enough, and the county inspector's walk-through, and the first session's real books against the budget lines. Those points belong to real people and a real day, not to better copywriting. 9.0 or the camp does not run.
