12 · FAQ

The questions parents actually ask.

Does my kid need AI or coding experience?

None. We assume zero on Day 1 and teach every tool from the button up. The camp's core skills, observing, checking, building, asking, deciding, need no prerequisites. Kids who already code get harder variants of every mission.

Wait, is this two weeks of screens?

The opposite. Roughly half of every day is outside or at a workbench: walking the block with a camera, soldering a sensor, testing prints, driving rovers through an obstacle course. AI tools appear constantly, but as the thing being directed, checked, and out-performed, not the destination.

Soldering irons and 3D printers at age 10?

Every tool is introduced with a supervised demonstration, and no tool is ever used solo: heated or sharp tools run only at supervised stations with the instructor within arm's reach, with ventilation and PPE. The soldering iron is touched by the instructor only, while campers position parts and read the meter. With one adult and twelve campers, tool use is station-gated: only one heated station runs at a time, the rest of the group works on non-tool tasks. Our instructor owns tool safety personally.

Which AI tools do you use, and are they safe for kids?

School-managed accounts on mainstream, kid-safe AI tools (image generation, chat, CAD assistants) with content filtering on, no personal data entered, and strict "we don't feed it our faces or friends' names" rules taught on Day 2. Accounts are camp-owned and wiped at session end.

What does my kid actually take home?

A producer's portfolio: their verified dataset, the before-and-after portfolio page (their failed AI print photographed beside their human-fixed revision, with their own captions), sensor charts, the interview transcript (three hand-transcribed marker moments from the Moment Marker handout; the interview audio itself stays on the camp's recorders, which never leave the room), the debugging log, and the signed credit roll, plus a diploma reading Producer of AI Projects, Certified Human.

Is there a device-free option for take-home work?

Yes, and it is the default, not the exception. Every take-home item has a paper path: each day's project-block handout carries a perforated dinner-table conversation card as a tear-off strip (the ten questions are printed in the handout kit spec in §03b, one per day), the journal is a physical notebook, and anything that uploads to the camp gallery has a paper alternative (bring the journal in; at pickup the instructor runs the flagged pages through the flatbed scanner on the door table, about 30 seconds a page, and each scan lands in that camper's QR-linked gallery). Campers never take a shared device home; nothing that leaves the room needs a charger. Each day the camper tears off the conversation card at pickup, a single question for the dinner table, like "Ask a grown-up: when has a computer been confidently wrong in your job?" No screen, no app, no login required for any of it.

Is there financial aid?

Yes, a donor-funded scholarship bench covers 2 of 12 seats per session, prioritized through local school partners. Application is one short form; decisions are need-blind and made before enrollment closes.

My child has an IEP, ADHD, or sensory needs. Can they attend?

Very likely yes, and we tell you honestly before you pay. Enrollment asks about accommodations on the same form as allergies and pickup authorizations, and every family that names one gets a 15-minute call with us the week before camp to agree in writing on exactly what we will do: large-print handouts, seating with extra space, fidget tools and earplugs at your child's own seat, handout steps read aloud at the handoff, and field-mission routes checked for accessibility at our venue walk-through. What we cannot do with one instructor and twelve campers: provide 1:1 aides, run a separate quiet room, or change the line-of-sight supervision model. If we cannot support your child safely, you hear that before paying, and an enrolled family in that position gets a full refund.

What happens if my child feels sick at camp?

They never leave the instructor's sight. The sick camper rests in a chair beside the instructor's table while the rest of the group keeps working, the instructor takes a temperature, and a fever or any vomiting triggers same-day pickup: we message you over the camp number and your child rests until a listed adult arrives, expected within 30 minutes. A camper who vomited or ran a fever returns only after 24 hours symptom-free without medication, and you see that rule in the enrollment confirmation before you pay. If symptoms escalate, our incident tiers take over: first aid, then 911, then the parent call.

What happens if my child has a seizure during the camp day?

A seizure is the one emergency the whole group rehearses for on Day 1. If your child seizes during a lesson or the project block, the instructor freezes the group: the other eleven campers sit in the U with hands off their tools, check in with their buddy pairs, and nobody crowds your child. The instructor clears space around your child, pushing chairs and trays back without moving your child, cushions the head with a jacket or a bag if one is in reach, and stays at your child's side timing the seizure. Nobody holds your child down, nobody puts anything in the mouth, and nobody gives food, water, or medicine while the shaking lasts. When it stops, your child is rolled onto one side if breathing, kept warm, watched, and never left alone, while the instructor counts the group aloud in buddy pairs to make sure nobody left the U. If your child has a written seizure action plan on file, that plan rules: you gave us its steps at enrollment, and the instructor follows it exactly, including any rescue medication it names. A seizure that ends within five minutes with your child returning to normal means a message to you over the camp number within 15 minutes, the incident form filed the same hour, and your child resting in a chair in the instructor's sightline for the rest of the session, with no tool stations that day. Anything bigger calls 911 first, then you, then the incident form: a seizure longer than five minutes, a second seizure without full recovery, troubled breathing after the shaking stops, an injury during the seizure, a seizure after a head injury, or no seizure history and no action plan on file. If the ambulance takes your child, the instructor stays with the group under the hospital-transport rule, which the camp-day FAQ answers separately. Every seizure is logged with the time, the place in the room, the timed length, whether 911 was called, and whether any rescue medication was given per the action plan. The Day 1 freeze and the Day 2 walk-out count already rehearse every rule in this answer, so no new drill is needed.

What happens if my child has an allergic reaction during the camp day?

Allergies are planned for, not improvised. At enrollment you gave us your child's allergy action plan, and a laminated copy of it rides the field bag clipped next to the EpiPens, with your child's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector in the med lockbox under the signed authorization form. If your child reports a reaction at the tables, during snack time or any session, the instructor freezes the group first: hands off tools, the group holds the U in the instructor's sightline, and nobody crowds your child. The instructor reads your child's laminated action plan off the field-bag clip and follows it exactly as written, the named symptoms, the named response, the named medication and dose, no improvisation and no diagnosis. If the airway is involved or anaphylaxis is suspected, the Tier 3 chain runs at once: your child's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first, then 911 from the instructor-table phone with the venue address and street cross from the session contact card, then you on the camp number, then the incident form, in that order. Hives or swelling without airway involvement follows only the antihistamine path your plan names, and only if your plan and the signed authorization form both name it. You are called at once on the camp number, and your child stays at the instructor's side in the sightline, not back at the tables, until you answer. The episode is logged to the Airtable incident log the same hour with the symptom time, the action-plan steps followed, the epinephrine time if given, the 911 time, and the parent-contact time. The freeze, the U, the EpiPens, the med lockbox, the laminated plans, the session contact card, and the Airtable log all already exist and are already rehearsed, so no new drill is needed.

What happens if my child chokes during the camp day?

Snacks and drinks are at the tables during lessons and the project block, so choking is planned for, not improvised. If your child chokes, the Day 1 freeze governs the first minute: the other eleven campers stay seated in the U with hands off tools (the soldering iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), buddy pairs check each other in, and nobody crowds your child, nobody leaves the U to fetch help, and nobody gives your child food or water. If your child is coughing strongly and can speak, the instructor stays at your child's side and encourages the cough, and does not slap the back, does not reach into the mouth, and does not start thrusts while an effective cough is clearing the airway. A cough that clears the object with breathing back to normal gets a message to you over the camp number within 15 minutes and the incident form filed the same hour, and your child rests in the sick chair in the instructor's sightline for 20 minutes before rejoining, only if breathing stays normal. If your child cannot cough, cannot speak, or cannot breathe, or a strong cough weakens and turns silent, the instructor calls 911 on speakerphone first, then gives cycles of 5 back blows between the shoulder blades with the heel of the hand, your child leaning slightly forward, followed by 5 abdominal thrusts, fist above the navel and below the ribs with quick inward and upward thrusts, repeating the cycles until the object clears or help arrives. Chest thrusts replace abdominal thrusts in the same cycle if abdominal thrusts are not possible. Your call follows 911 but waits until the object clears or EMS is on the line, because the instructor's hands are on your child, and the incident form follows after. If your child becomes unconscious, the instructor lowers your child gently to the floor, keeps 911 on speaker if it is not already called, and starts CPR as the instructor's first-aid and CPR certification trains, checking the mouth only if an object is seen and never doing a blind finger sweep. Care continues until EMS takes over or breathing resumes. If the ambulance takes your child, the instructor stays with the group under the hospital-transport rule, which the camp-day FAQ answers separately. Every episode is logged to the Airtable incident log the same hour. The Day 1 freeze, the U, the speakerphone path, the sick chair, and the Airtable log all already exist and are already rehearsed, so no new drill is needed.

What happens if my child has an asthma attack during the camp day?

Breathing trouble at the tables is planned for, not improvised. If your child reports trouble breathing, wheezing, or a tight chest, they sit upright at the instructor's side, never lying flat, while the other eleven campers stay seated in the U and keep working and nobody leaves the room to fetch help. The instructor takes your child's own prescribed rescue inhaler from the med lockbox, checked against the med/allergy sheet in the instructor folder and your child's written asthma action plan, and your child uses it exactly as the plan directs: inhalers are never shared between campers, and the instructor never gives a dose the action plan does not name.

Your child rests at the instructor's side while the group keeps working, and the lesson resumes its normal pace only when your child says breathing feels normal again. An attack that eases is Tier 2: a message to you over the camp number within 15 minutes and the incident form filed the same hour. Anything bigger calls 911 first, then you, then the incident form: breathing that does not ease after the action plan's doses, your child unable to speak a full sentence, lips or face looking blue or gray, no action plan on file for your child, or an attack that follows a sting or a food exposure with airway signs, in which case the epinephrine line runs first.

Every attack is logged with the time, the place in the room, the trigger if known, the inhaler doses given per the action plan, and whether 911 was called, and each inhaler use is also logged to the medication log. The sit-upright rule, the U, the own-inhaler rule, the med lockbox, the action plan, and the logging all already exist and are already rehearsed, so no new drill is needed.

What happens if my child gets sick during a field walk?

The instructor stops the group on the route: everyone sits off the road in buddy pairs while your child sits with the instructor. An emesis bag and the thermometer come out of the field bag's first-aid kit right there, and a headache or nausea without fever gets water and about ten minutes seated, the group resuming if it passes. A fever or any vomiting means we message you over the camp number for same-day pickup, but the pickup is at the venue: the whole group walks straight back together along the mapped route, the instructor's count running aloud, and your child rests at the venue until you arrive. If your child cannot walk, the 911-first emergency chain fires immediately. The 24-hour symptom-free return rule applies on the walk as at the venue.

What happens if my child needs a midday medication during a field walk?

The dose is given on the route, not postponed to the venue: every route has a pre-mapped dose gate, a bench or shade point where the whole group sits together inside the instructor's sightline. Your child's buddy stands with them as the witness while the instructor gives the dose from the med lockbox or the lockable medication cooler bag, and the dose is logged with time and the instructor's initials beside the words exactly like every room dose. No one leaves the group, and the walk continues after the aloud count resumes.

What happens if my child needs a midday medication during the camp day?

The dose is given at the instructor table, not postponed to a walk: your child's dose time is known at enrollment from the signed authorization form, and the med/allergy sheet lists it. At a natural break in the block the instructor calls a planned pause, never the emergency Freeze call, and your child comes to the instructor's side with their buddy while the group keeps working in the U in the instructor's sightline. The buddy stands as the accountability witness, watching the dose and the log, never handling medication. The instructor opens the med lockbox or the lockable medication cooler bag with their body shielding the combination code from the group: the cooler bag's thermometer strip is read first and logged with the dose, the bag opens for seconds, the dose comes out per the label and the authorization form, and the bag is re-locked before the pause ends; your child's own water bottle covers a dose that needs water. The dose is logged with time and the instructor's initials beside the words, the same chain the field-walk answer describes. If anything is ambiguous the room rule holds: the instructor calls the parent before the dose, the group keeps working meanwhile. A dose that comes back up hands the script to the illness response, the sick chair and the sick kit.

What happens if my child's medication changes mid-session?

The change takes effect only on a fresh signed authorization form: no dose is ever given on a verbal instruction alone. Send the change over the camp number the evening before, sign the new form (DocuSign or paper at the door), and the instructor swaps the lockbox contents, updates the med/allergy sheet, and logs the change with time and the instructor's initials beside the words, all before the first dose under the new instruction. A discontinued medication comes home with you at pickup the same day, and a missed dose is logged with its reason, never guessed.

What if I need to reach camp after hours?

Send it to the camp number anyway. Nobody watches the line after the 11:15 pickup, but the instructor reads every message first thing each teaching morning and replies "got it" to each one, so anything you send overnight is handled before the doors open. A call after hours never rings the instructor's phone: it goes straight to the camp's voicemail greeting, which repeats the hours line, and the message is heard at the morning read. The hours line is printed in the footer of your pickup-authorization list. A true emergency after hours is a 911 call: the camp is closed and there is no night desk. Voicemail counts the same as a text: the instructor listens to every voicemail in the morning read. A voicemail never changes a medication or a pickup on its own: the signed form still has to land before anything changes, and a voicemail that disagrees with the signed form loses to the form.

If my child is taken to the hospital, who rides with them?

The paramedics transport your child; the instructor stays with the other eleven campers. One adult cannot leave the group unsupervised, and you agree to this configuration in the emergency-medical-treatment authorization you sign before paying. The 911 call comes first, then we call you, and you meet your child at the emergency department: your drive starts on the 911 call so you arrive with the ambulance. A one-page handoff sheet with your child's medication and allergy summary, what happened, and what was given rides with the paramedics, and we keep updating the camp number with the hospital name and address until a listed adult confirms they are on the way.

What happens if a thunderstorm rolls in during a field walk?

The mission aborts: thunder heard or lightning seen means the instructor calls the group together, counts aloud, and walks everyone straight back to the venue along the route you already see in the logistics pack, no sheltering under trees, no waiting it out in the open. The group does not step out again until 30 minutes after the last thunder. The day continues on the same indoor backup activities we pre-build for every weather day, so nothing is lost. You get a one-line message on the camp number when a mission comes in early, and the abort is logged.

What happens if a thunderstorm hits while the group is in the room?

The field-walk answer above covers a storm that catches the group out walking. The room form covers the storm that reaches twelve campers already inside: thunder starting during the project block, the walk never leaving because the pre-departure radar check caught the warning, or a mission aborting back through the door.

The Day 1 freeze governs the first minute, with hands off tools and nobody crowding the doors or the windows. The instructor calls the hold, the group stays in the room, the door locks from inside, the instructor closes the doors and the windows, and the group moves to the tables in the U, away from the glass. Those are the same tables the Day 1 drop, cover, and hold rehearsal uses, so your child has already practiced this move.

Tool stations go cold at the instructor's call and stay down until the all-clear below, because no powered tool runs while the room shelters. Nobody steps out until 30 minutes after the last thunder, counted from inside, the same clock the field abort runs.

The pre-built indoor pivots absorb the rest of the day, because every pivot is pre-built before the session and the day already continues indoors. You get the same one-line message on the camp number the field abort carries: "storm shelter in place, campers inside, day continues indoors." Every shelter-in-place is logged with a timestamp.

No new tool and no new skill: doors, windows, the freeze, the U, and the indoor pivots already exist, so nothing new was rehearsed on Day 1 and the parent walkthrough night promise is unchanged.

What happens if the instructor is injured during a field walk?

The group freezes: the oldest camper says "Freeze, buddies" once, and everyone sits on the grass edge or the sidewalk, off the road, buddy pairs hold, and nobody leaves the route. The oldest camper pulls the instructor's phone from the field bag's named zip phone pocket and runs the locked-phone path, rehearsed at the Day 2 walk-out count: open the Control Center on an iPhone, or the quick-settings shade on an Android, from the lock screen with no passcode, tap Do Not Disturb on so no parent call can ring over the 911 call, then tap the Emergency call button and dial 911 with no passcode. The oldest camper reads the route card's printed 911 line to the dispatcher, the route street, the nearest cross street, and the mapped hard shelter, or the venue address off the pickup-list reverse on the venue-adjacent Day 2 safari; if the dispatcher asks what is wrong with the instructor, the oldest camper reads the instructor medical card from the same pocket, to the dispatcher only, never aloud to the group. When the 911 call ends, the oldest camper turns Do Not Disturb off by the same lock-screen path, no passcode needed, so the dispatcher's callback rings through, and answers the dispatcher first. No camper sends a parent message from the phone: the parent broadcast is pre-scheduled before the session and fires at 11:16 that day on its own if the instructor never cancels it, so parent comms arrive after the emergency response, in the same order the Tier 3 chain holds. The second oldest runs the aloud buddy count and keeps the group inside the instructor's sightline. The procedure is rehearsed with the campers at the Day 2 walk-out count and walked with parents at the parent walkthrough night, so every family knows it before paying. Like the room version, nobody touches the medication lockbox, the tools, the field kits, or the epinephrine.

What happens if the instructor is injured during the camp day?

The field-walk answer above names the walk form, but your child spends the 60-minute block in the room, so this is the room form, in the language the enrollment decision needs. The one-adult reality does not change indoors: there is no second adult, so the room runs a script the campers rehearse on Day 1, in the same walk as the fire, lockdown, lost-camper, and earthquake drill, and we walk your family through it at the parent walkthrough night before you pay. If the instructor is hurt mid-lesson and cannot lead, the room freezes on the Day 1 call, everyone stays in the room, and the door locks from inside. The two oldest campers present take the two named roles they were given in the Day 1 walk: the oldest camper runs the phone, the second oldest runs the aloud buddy count. The oldest camper calls 911 from the instructor's phone on the instructor table, and the script names the locked-phone path because the phone is locked and no camper knows the passcode: open the Control Center on an iPhone, or the quick-settings shade on an Android, from the lock screen with no passcode, tap Do Not Disturb on so no call rings over the 911 call, then tap the Emergency call button and dial 911 with no passcode. When the 911 call ends, the oldest camper turns Do Not Disturb off by the same lock-screen path, because 911 dispatchers call the reporting number back and that callback must ring through, and answers the dispatcher first. The oldest camper reads the venue address to the dispatcher off the reverse of the pickup-authorization list, and if the dispatcher asks what is wrong with the instructor, reads the instructor's medical card clipped face-down beside the phone, to the dispatcher only, never aloud to the room. No camper sends a parent message from the phone: the parent broadcast is armed at the T-6w build and fires on its own at 11:16 that day if the instructor never cancels it, a pre-written "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held" message to every session parent, the founder's cell, and the designated break-glass adult's cell, so the employer and the keyholder learn the same minute you do and the pickup coverage is already moving. While the oldest camper runs the phone, the second oldest runs the aloud buddy count and holds the group inside the room, in the sightline the campers already know. Nobody touches the medication lockbox, the tools, or any medication, the same rule the field form holds. If the instructor is taken to the hospital, the group stays in the locked room and your child is released only to a person on the pickup-authorization list under the photo-ID door rule, the same rule that governs every pickup, with the break-glass adult as the keyholder if the door has to be opened from the outside.

What happens if the power goes out during the camp day?

The room day is mostly powered: the room screen, the cart laptops, and the instructor-run soldering station all draw on the venue. If the power drops mid-block, your child sees the lights go out or flicker, the screen goes dark, and the laptops keep running on battery. The first minute is the Day 1 freeze, the same call that governs every room incident: hands off tools at once, nobody leaves a table, nobody stands up to see what happened. The instructor's first move is to the soldering station, not to the campers: the hot iron goes into its stand, the station is switched off, and it stays cold until the power is back, because a hot iron no one can see is the worst thing in a dark room. Your child stays seated in the U in the instructor's sightline while this happens: nobody crosses the room in the dark, nobody steps into the hallway to look, nobody crowds the station. Where the venue allows, the instructor opens the door for daylight from the door side, without leaving the sightline. The group holds a settle minute with nobody moving until the instructor calls the move. Then the day flips to its paper version: the paper day the tech-outage plan names, the lost segment is not chased, and the paper log backstops attendance and incidents. An injury in the transition runs the same injury forms with the same 911 triggers as any other injury, and ending the session early runs the existing early-dismissal rule with its parent-ping path. Every outage, even one that flickers back after two minutes, is logged with the time the power went, how long it stayed out, whether anyone was hurt in the transition, and whether the paper cut-over ran. None of this is new equipment or a new camper skill: the freeze, the U, the iron stand, and the paper day are all rehearsed on Day 1, so this answer adds no new rehearsal minute and changes nothing about the parent walkthrough night.

It is hot but not hot enough to cancel the walk. How do you handle heat?

Sunscreen is self-applied at the door before the group leaves, and every camper carries a full water bottle with a spare bottle in the instructor's bag, so water is never the constraint. The instructor knows the signs: sweating through, headache, dizziness, or nausea means the camper sits in the shade with water for ten minutes while the group keeps working nearby, always in sight. If the camper is not back to normal after ten minutes, the walk ends early and the group comes back, with a message to you on the camp number. Anything more serious, confusion, slurred speech, or fainting, gets a 911 call first, like any emergency. Heat incidents are logged the same as every other safety incident.

What happens if my child gets separated from the group on a walk?

The group freezes on the spot: the instructor calls everyone together, sits them off the road in buddy pairs, and counts aloud. If your child is visible, the whole group walks together to close the gap, nobody runs ahead alone. If your child is not visible, the instructor calls 911 immediately from the phone in the field bag, reading the exact location off the route card, then calls you, the same 911-then-parent order as every emergency. One adult cannot leave eleven children to search, so nobody searches: the group stays together at the last point where your child was seen until the dispatcher or you say otherwise. The drill is rehearsed with the campers on the second walk of the session and walked with parents at the walkthrough night, so every family knows it before paying. Every incident is logged with a timestamp.

What happens if my child needs the restroom during a field walk?

The walk comes home. The mapped routes have no public restrooms, so before every walk the instructor sends everyone to the venue restroom first, and if your child needs one mid-walk they say so and the whole group walks straight back to the venue together; nobody is sent off alone and nobody ducks into a shop. Your child uses the venue restroom while the group counts back in, and the mission continues on the indoor backup plan. It is announced matter-of-factly at every walk-out, so asking is normal.

Why "producers" and not "prompt engineers"?

Prompting is a technique; producing is a job. A producer owns the outcome: the data, the checks, the fixes, and the blame when it's wrong. Prompting is one arrow in that quiver, we teach all ten.

What if AI gets better at all this by 2027?

Then the curriculum sharpens, the weakness list is re-audited each spring and days are rebuilt around whatever AI is currently worst at. The mission outlives any specific failure mode: the human who verifies, builds, and takes responsibility stays essential. (Also: we'll believe the demo when we can check it. That's the whole camp.)

Back the camp. Send a kid. Teach a day.

Follow the project and get involved: github.com/rayhe/ai-producer-camp
